Slide mistakes to watch for

  • Lesson 1, other example 1: the last line says \(\frac{(x^4+8)^{10}}{10}\). The line before it correctly gives \(\frac{u^{10}}{40}\), so the answer is \(\frac{(x^4+8)^{10}}{40}+C\).
  • Lesson 9, Weierstrass example 2: with \(z=\tan x\), \(dx=\frac{dz}{1+z^2}\), not \(\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\). The correct answer is \(\frac{1}{2\sqrt2}\arctan\frac{\tan x}{\sqrt2}+C\), which is half the slide's answer.
  • Lesson 5, example 3: \(\sin^2x-16\) is always negative, so \(\sqrt{\sin^2x-16}\) is never a real number. Treat it as practice for the arcsec pattern only.
  • Small typos: Lesson 9, example 4 says "divide \(z^2\) by \(z^2+1\)" but means \(z^2+9\). Lesson 10, exercise 6 writes \(dy\) for an integrand in x.
START HERE

Key terms & formula sheet

All the formulas from every lesson in one place. The lesson sections below refer back to these.

Key terms

Antiderivative
\(F\) is an antiderivative of \(f\) if \(F'(x)=f(x)\). Integrating means finding an antiderivative.
Indefinite integral
\(\int f(x)\,dx=F(x)+C\). The \(\int\) sign is the integral sign, \(f(x)\) is the integrand, and \(dx\) names the variable of integration.
Constant of integration \(C\)
Any two antiderivatives differ by a constant, so always write \(+C\). \(F(x)\) alone is called the particular integral.
u-substitution
Let \(u=g(x)\), so \(du=g'(x)\,dx\). This turns \(\int f(g(x))g'(x)\,dx\) into \(\int f(u)\,du\).
Correction factor
A constant multiplied inside the integral to complete \(du\), with its reciprocal placed outside. It only works for constants, never variables.
Improper rational function
The numerator's degree is ≥ the denominator's. Do long division first. A proper one has a lower numerator degree. If the bottom is \(x-c\) (like \(x+1\)), synthetic division is a faster way to divide.
Integration by parts (IBP)
\(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). Use it for products of different function types. Pick u with LIATE: Log, Inverse trig, Algebraic, Trig, Exponential.
Tabular method
Repeated IBP set up as a table. Differentiate the polynomial down to 0, integrate the other factor, then multiply along the diagonals with signs \(+,-,+,\dots\)
Algebraic substitution
Removes radicals. \(x=z^n\) (n = LCD of the fractional exponents) or \(ax+b=z^n\).
Trigonometric substitution
\(a^2-u^2\to u=a\sin\theta\), \(a^2+u^2\to u=a\tan\theta\), \(u^2-a^2\to u=a\sec\theta\). Convert back with a reference triangle.
Completing the square
Rewrites \(ax^2+bx+c\) as \(\pm(u^2\pm a^2)\) so an inverse-trig or log form applies.
Partial fractions
Splits a proper rational function into simple fractions over the factors of the denominator: linear, repeated, or irreducible quadratic.

Rewriting rules the "why" behind most steps

Reciprocal identities

\[\sec\theta=\frac1{\cos\theta}\qquad\csc\theta=\frac1{\sin\theta}\qquad\cot\theta=\frac1{\tan\theta}\]

These are definitions: sec, csc and cot are just 1 over cos, sin and tan. Squaring keeps them true, so \(\frac1{\cos^2\theta}=\left(\frac1{\cos\theta}\right)^2=\sec^2\theta\). Pairs to remember: sin ↔ csc, cos ↔ sec, tan ↔ cot.

Quotient identities

\[\tan\theta=\frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta}\qquad\cot\theta=\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\]

On the unit circle, sin is the height and cos is the width, so tan is height ÷ width (the slope). Combined with the reciprocals: \(\frac{\sin x}{\cos^2x}=\frac1{\cos x}\cdot\frac{\sin x}{\cos x}=\sec x\tan x\).

Derivatives you need for du

\[(\sin u)'=\cos u\quad(\cos u)'=-\sin u\quad(\tan u)'=\sec^2u\]\[(\cot u)'=-\csc^2u\quad(\sec u)'=\sec u\tan u\quad(\csc u)'=-\csc u\cot u\]\[(e^u)'=e^u\qquad(a^u)'=a^u\ln a\qquad(\ln u)'=\frac1u\]

Every "find du" step uses one of these. Chain rule: multiply by the derivative of the inside, so \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\) and \(\frac{d}{dx}e^{x^2}=2xe^{x^2}\).

Negative & fractional exponents

\[\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\qquad\sqrt[n]{a^m}=a^{m/n}\]

A power in the denominator moves to the top with its sign flipped, and back again. A root is a fractional power: square root = power ½, cube root = power ⅓. Example: \(\frac1{\sqrt x}=x^{-1/2}\).

Exponent laws

\[a^m\cdot a^n=a^{m+n}\qquad\frac{a^m}{a^n}=a^{m-n}\qquad(a^m)^n=a^{mn}\]

Multiplying adds the exponents, dividing subtracts them, and a power of a power multiplies them. Example: \(\frac{x^2}{x^{1/2}}=x^{2-\frac12}=x^{3/2}\).

e and ln

\[e^{\ln u}=u\qquad\ln e^u=u\qquad k\ln x=\ln x^k\]

\(e^x\) and \(\ln x\) are inverse functions, so one undoes the other. The log power rule moves a number in front of ln up into the exponent.

Fraction moves

\[a\div\frac bc=a\cdot\frac cb\qquad\frac{a+b}{c}=\frac ac+\frac bc\]

Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip. A sum on top can be split into separate fractions, but a sum on the bottom cannot.

Algebra moves

\[a^2-b^2=(a+b)(a-b)\qquad(a\pm b)^2=a^2\pm2ab+b^2\]\[x^2+bx=\left(x+\tfrac b2\right)^2-\tfrac{b^2}4\]

Difference of squares, expanding a square, and completing the square: take half the x-coefficient, square it, then add and subtract it.

Formula sheet

Basic rules & power formula L1

  1. \[\int dx=x+C\]

    Integral of 1. Integrating plain \(dx\) gives the variable itself. Every answer gets \(+C\), because the derivative of any constant is 0. Example: \(\int dt=t+C\).

  2. \[\int cf\,dx=c\int f\,dx\]

    Constant multiple rule. Move a number out front, integrate, then multiply it back in. Example: \(\int5x^2dx=5\cdot\frac{x^3}3=\frac53x^3+C\).

  3. \[\int(f_1\pm f_2)\,dx=\int f_1\,dx\pm\int f_2\,dx\]

    Sum and difference rule. Integrate one term at a time. Example: \(\int(x^2+3)\,dx=\frac{x^3}3+3x+C\).

  4. \[\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}+C,\quad n\neq-1\]

    Power formula. Add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent. It fails for \(n=-1\) (you'd divide by 0), and that case gives \(\ln|u|\) instead (Lesson 2). \(u\) can be plain \(x\) or an inner function, as long as \(du\) is there. Examples: \(\int x^4dx=\frac{x^5}5+C\), \(\int x^{-1/2}dx=2x^{1/2}+C\).

More worked examples of these rules →

Logarithm L2

  1. \[\int\frac{du}{u}=\ln|u|+C\]

    Log rule. If the top of a fraction is the derivative of the bottom, the answer is ln of the bottom. Fix a missing constant with a correction factor. The absolute value is there because ln only accepts positive numbers. This is the \(n=-1\) case the power formula can't handle. Example: \(\int\frac{2x\,dx}{x^2+1}=\ln(x^2+1)+C\).

More worked examples →

Exponential L3

  1. \[\int e^u\,du=e^u+C\]

    Base e. \(e\) to a power integrates to itself. Just divide by the constant that comes from \(du\). Example: \(\int e^{3x}dx=\frac13e^{3x}+C\).

  2. \[\int a^u\,du=\frac{a^u}{\ln a}+C\]

    Any other base. Same idea, then also divide by \(\ln a\). Example: \(\int2^x\,dx=\frac{2^x}{\ln2}+C\).

  3. \[e^{\ln u}=u\qquad a^{\log_a x}=x\qquad \ln e^u=u\]

    Simplify first. e and ln cancel each other. Example: \(e^{2\ln x}=e^{\ln x^2}=x^2\).

More worked examples →

Trig, basic six L4

  1. \[\int\sin u\,du=-\cos u\qquad\int\cos u\,du=\sin u\]

    Sine and cosine. These are derivative rules read backwards. Watch the minus sign on sine. Example: \(\int\sin2x\,dx=-\frac12\cos2x+C\).

  2. \[\int\sec^2u\,du=\tan u\qquad\int\csc^2u\,du=-\cot u\]

    Squares of sec and csc. These come from the derivatives of tan and cot. Example: \(\int\sec^23x\,dx=\frac13\tan3x+C\).

  3. \[\int\sec u\tan u\,du=\sec u\qquad\int\csc u\cot u\,du=-\csc u\]

    Pairs. These come from the derivatives of sec and csc. Example: \(\int\sec2x\tan2x\,dx=\frac12\sec2x+C\).

Tip: an inside angle \(ax\) always adds a factor of \(\frac1a\) in front.

More worked examples →

Trig, log type L4

  1. \[\int\tan u\,du=\ln|\sec u|=-\ln|\cos u|\]\[\int\cot u\,du=\ln|\sin u|\]

    Tangent and cotangent. These come from the log rule, because \(\tan u=\frac{\sin u}{\cos u}\) and the top is (minus) the derivative of the bottom. Example: \(\int\tan2x\,dx=\frac12\ln|\sec2x|+C\).

  2. \[\int\sec u\,du=\ln|\sec u+\tan u|\]\[\int\csc u\,du=\ln|\csc u-\cot u|\]

    Secant and cosecant. There's no easy derivation, so memorize these two. Example: \(\int\sec3x\,dx=\frac13\ln|\sec3x+\tan3x|+C\).

More worked examples →

Identities you'll need

  1. \[\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\quad 1+\tan^2\theta=\sec^2\theta\quad 1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\]

    Pythagorean. Swap between sin and cos, tan and sec, or cot and csc. Example: \(\tan^2x=\sec^2x-1\), so \(\int\tan^2x\,dx=\tan x-x+C\).

  2. \[\sin2\theta=2\sin\theta\cos\theta\qquad \cos2\theta=\cos^2\theta-\sin^2\theta\]

    Double angle. Turns a product into a single function. Example: \(\sin x\cos x=\frac12\sin2x\).

  3. \[\sin^2\theta=\tfrac12(1-\cos2\theta)\qquad \cos^2\theta=\tfrac12(1+\cos2\theta)\]

    Half-angle (power reducing). Gets rid of a square so you can integrate. Example: \(\int\sin^2x\,dx=\frac x2-\frac14\sin2x+C\).

More worked examples →

Product-to-sum L4, L8

  1. \[\sin A\cos B=\tfrac12[\sin(A+B)+\sin(A-B)]\]\[\cos A\cos B=\tfrac12[\cos(A+B)+\cos(A-B)]\]\[\sin A\sin B=\tfrac12[\cos(A-B)-\cos(A+B)]\]

    Product-to-sum. Turns a product of two trig functions with different angles into a sum you can integrate term by term. Example: \(\sin3x\cos x=\frac12(\sin4x+\sin2x)\).

More worked examples →

Inverse trig forms L5, a > 0

  1. \[\int\frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2-u^2}}=\arcsin\frac ua+C\]

    Arcsin. Square root of (constant − variable²) in the denominator. Example: \(\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{4-x^2}}=\arcsin\frac x2+C\).

  2. \[\int\frac{du}{a^2+u^2}=\frac1a\arctan\frac ua+C\]

    Arctan. Constant + variable² in the denominator, with no root. Don't forget the \(\frac1a\) in front. Example: \(\int\frac{dx}{9+x^2}=\frac13\arctan\frac x3+C\).

  3. \[\int\frac{du}{u\sqrt{u^2-a^2}}=\frac1a\operatorname{arcsec}\frac{|u|}a+C\]

    Arcsec. u times the root of (variable² − constant). Example: \(\int\frac{dx}{x\sqrt{x^2-1}}=\operatorname{arcsec}|x|+C\).

How to read a and u: \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part. Take square roots to get a and u.

More worked examples →

Log / inverse-hyperbolic forms L5

  1. \[\int\frac{du}{\sqrt{u^2\pm a^2}}=\ln\left(u+\sqrt{u^2\pm a^2}\right)+C\]

    Root of u² ± a². The variable comes first under the root, so it's not arcsin. Example: \(\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{x^2+1}}=\ln\left(x+\sqrt{x^2+1}\right)+C\).

  2. \[\int\frac{du}{a^2-u^2}=\frac1{2a}\ln\left|\frac{a+u}{a-u}\right|+C\]

    a² − u², no root. A minus sign instead of plus, so it's not arctan. Example: \(\int\frac{dx}{1-x^2}=\frac12\ln\left|\frac{1+x}{1-x}\right|+C\).

  3. \[\int\frac{du}{u\sqrt{a^2\pm u^2}}=-\frac1a\ln\frac{a+\sqrt{a^2\pm u^2}}{|u|}+C\]

    u times root of a² ± u². The constant comes first, so it's not arcsec. Example: \(\int\frac{dx}{x\sqrt{1+x^2}}=-\ln\frac{1+\sqrt{1+x^2}}{|x|}+C\).

Compare with the inverse trig forms: the order and sign inside decide which formula applies.

More worked examples →

Integration by parts L7

  1. \[\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\]

    Integration by parts. Trade a hard integral for an easier one. Pick u by LIATE (Log, Inverse trig, Algebraic, Trig, Exponential), differentiate it, and integrate dv to get v. If the original integral shows up again, move it to the left side and solve. Example: \(\int xe^x\,dx\) with \(u=x\), \(dv=e^xdx\) gives \(xe^x-e^x+C\).

More worked examples →

Standard results worth memorizing

  1. \[\int\ln x\,dx=x\ln x-x+C\]

    By parts with \(u=\ln x\), \(dv=dx\). See 7·E2.

  2. \[\int\sec^3x\,dx=\tfrac12\sec x\tan x+\tfrac12\ln|\sec x+\tan x|+C\]

    By parts, where the integral comes back and you solve for it. See 7·E4. You'll need it in Lesson 8.

  3. \[\int e^x\sin x\,dx=\tfrac12e^x(\sin x-\cos x)+C\]

    By parts twice, where the integral cycles back. See 7·E6.

More worked examples →

Weierstrass substitution L9, not included

  1. \[z=\tan\tfrac x2:\ \sin x=\frac{2z}{1+z^2},\ \cos x=\frac{1-z^2}{1+z^2},\ dx=\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\]

    Half-angle substitution. Turns any fraction built from sin x and cos x into an ordinary fraction in z. Example: \(\int\frac{dx}{1+\cos x}=\tan\frac x2+C\). Your slides mark this topic "not included".

More worked examples →

\(\int\sin^mx\cos^nx\,dx\)n oddSplit off one \(\cos x\). Change the rest using \(\cos^2x=1-\sin^2x\).\(u=\sin x\)
m oddSplit off one \(\sin x\). Change the rest using \(\sin^2x=1-\cos^2x\).\(u=\cos x\)
both evenLower the powers with the half-angle formulas \(\sin^2x=\frac12(1-\cos2x)\), \(\cos^2x=\frac12(1+\cos2x)\).—
\(\int\tan^mx\sec^nx\,dx\)n evenSplit off \(\sec^2x\). Change the rest using \(\sec^2x=1+\tan^2x\).\(u=\tan x\)
m oddSplit off \(\sec x\tan x\). Change the rest using \(\tan^2x=\sec^2x-1\).\(u=\sec x\)
m even, n oddConvert everything to powers of \(\sec x\), then use \(\int\sec^3x\) (by parts).—
\(\int\cot^mx\csc^nx\,dx\)same as tan/secUse \(\csc^2x=1+\cot^2x\) and \(\cot^2x=\csc^2x-1\). Note \(d(\cot x)=-\csc^2x\,dx\).\(u=\cot x\) or \(\csc x\)

LESSON 1

Antiderivatives & the Power Formula

Undo differentiation. Rewrite roots and fractions as powers, then use \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}+C\). In u-substitution, the rest of the integrand must be \(du\), up to a constant.

  1. Rewrite: \(\sqrt[n]{x^m}=x^{m/n}\), \(\frac1{x^n}=x^{-n}\). Expand simple products.
  2. Look for an inner function \(u\) whose derivative appears as a factor.
  3. Write \(du\). If a constant is missing, add a correction factor (\(k\) inside, \(\frac1k\) outside).
  4. Apply the power formula, substitute back, and add \(C\).

Formula practice · basic rules

B1

\(\displaystyle\int7\,dx\)

rules 1 + 2

Uses∫dx = x Constant multiple

  1. Spot the rules: 7 is a constant multiplied by \(dx\).
  2. Move the constant out (rule 2): \(\int7\,dx=7\int dx\).
  3. Integrate (rule 1): \(\int dx=x\), so \(7\int dx=7x\).
  4. Add C: \(7x+C\).
  5. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}(7x)=7\) ✓

Why +C? Integrating asks "what function has this derivative?", and there are infinitely many answers. \(7x\), \(7x+3\) and \(7x-10\) all have derivative 7, because the derivative of a constant is 0. Differentiating erases any constant, so integrating can't tell which one was there. \(+C\) stands for "plus any constant" and covers all of them at once. Leaving it out is usually marked wrong.
When it's required: on every indefinite integral (no limits), which covers everything in this reviewer. When it isn't: definite integrals with limits like \(\int_1^3\), because the C cancels when you subtract, and the \(v\) you find in integration by parts.

Answer\(7x+C\)

B2

\(\displaystyle\int x^6\,dx\)

rule 4

UsesPower formula

  1. Identify n: \(x^6\) has exponent \(n=6\).
  2. Add 1 to the exponent: \(6+1=7\), giving \(x^7\).
  3. Divide by the new exponent: \(\frac{x^7}{7}\).
  4. Add C and check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{x^7}7=\frac{7x^6}7=x^6\) ✓

Answer\(\frac{x^7}7+C\)

B3

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x^3}\)

rule 4, negative power

UsesPower formula

  1. Rewrite as a power: \(\frac1{x^3}=x^{-3}\), so \(n=-3\).Why? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  2. Add 1 to the exponent: \(-3+1=-2\), giving \(x^{-2}\).
  3. Divide by the new exponent: \(\frac{x^{-2}}{-2}\).
  4. Clean up: move \(x^{-2}\) back to the denominator: \(\frac{x^{-2}}{-2}=-\frac1{2x^2}\).
  5. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left(-\frac12x^{-2}\right)=-\frac12\cdot(-2)x^{-3}=x^{-3}\) ✓

Answer\(-\dfrac1{2x^2}+C\)

B4

\(\displaystyle\int\sqrt[3]{x}\,dx\)

rule 4, fractional power

UsesPower formula

  1. Rewrite as a power: \(\sqrt[3]x=x^{1/3}\), so \(n=\frac13\).Why? A root is a fractional power: \(\sqrt[n]{a^m}=a^{m/n}\). A square root is the power ½. See the rule
  2. Add 1 to the exponent: \(\frac13+1=\frac13+\frac33=\frac43\), giving \(x^{4/3}\).
  3. Divide by the new exponent: \(\frac{x^{4/3}}{4/3}\).
  4. Clean up the fraction: to divide by a fraction, flip it and multiply: \[\frac{x^{4/3}}{\tfrac43}=x^{4/3}\times\frac34=\frac34x^{4/3}\] Same idea with numbers: \(6\div\frac12=6\times2=12\).Why? Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip: \(\div\frac ab=\times\frac ba\). See the rule
  5. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac34x^{4/3}=\frac34\cdot\frac43x^{1/3}=x^{1/3}\) ✓

Answer\(\frac34x^{4/3}+C\)

B5

\(\displaystyle\int(3x^2-4x+7)\,dx\)

all 4 rules

Uses∫dx = x Constant multiple Sum rule Power formula

  1. Split into terms (rule 3): \(\int3x^2dx-\int4x\,dx+\int7\,dx\).Why? A sum on top can be split into separate fractions, \(\frac{a+b}c=\frac ac+\frac bc\), and the sum rule lets you integrate each piece separately. See the rule
  2. Pull out the constants (rule 2): \(3\int x^2dx-4\int x\,dx+7\int dx\).
  3. Power formula on each (rule 4): \(\int x^2dx=\frac{x^3}3\), \(\int x\,dx=\frac{x^2}2\), \(\int dx=x\).
  4. Multiply back: \(3\cdot\frac{x^3}3-4\cdot\frac{x^2}2+7x=x^3-2x^2+7x\).
  5. Add one C at the end: \(x^3-2x^2+7x+C\).

Answer\(x^3-2x^2+7x+C\)

B6

\(\displaystyle\int(x+1)(x-2)\,dx\)

expand first

UsesSum rule Power formula

  1. No product rule for integrals: you can't integrate each factor separately, so multiply it out first.
  2. Expand (FOIL): \((x+1)(x-2)=x^2-2x+x-2=x^2-x-2\).Why? Expanding means multiplying every term in one bracket by every term in the other, e.g. \((a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2\). See the rule
  3. Integrate term by term: \(\frac{x^3}3-\frac{x^2}2-2x\).
  4. Add C.

Answer\(\frac{x^3}3-\frac{x^2}2-2x+C\)

B7

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{x^2+1}{\sqrt x}\,dx\)

split, then power

UsesSum rule Power formula

  1. Rewrite the root: \(\sqrt x=x^{1/2}\).Why? A root is a fractional power: \(\sqrt[n]{a^m}=a^{m/n}\). A square root is the power ½. See the rule
  2. Split the fraction: \(\frac{x^2+1}{x^{1/2}}=\frac{x^2}{x^{1/2}}+\frac{1}{x^{1/2}}\).Why? A sum on top can be split into separate fractions, \(\frac{a+b}c=\frac ac+\frac bc\), and the sum rule lets you integrate each piece separately. See the rule
  3. Simplify with exponent rules: \(\frac{x^2}{x^{1/2}}=x^{2-\frac12}=x^{3/2}\) and \(\frac1{x^{1/2}}=x^{-1/2}\).Why? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the ruleWhy? Dividing powers with the same base subtracts the exponents: \(\frac{a^m}{a^n}=a^{m-n}\). See the rule
  4. Power formula: \(\frac{x^{5/2}}{5/2}+\frac{x^{1/2}}{1/2}\).
  5. Flip and multiply: \(\frac25x^{5/2}+2x^{1/2}\). Add C.Why? Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip: \(\div\frac ab=\times\frac ba\). See the rule

Answer\(\frac25x^{5/2}+2\sqrt x+C\)

B8

\(\displaystyle\int(2x+5)^4\,dx\)

rule 4 with u

UsesPower formula u-substitution Correction factor

  1. Why substitute? The power formula is \(\int u^4\,du\). The thing raised to the power has to match the d-part. Here the base is \((2x+5)\), but we only have \(dx\), not \(d(2x+5)\). Expanding \((2x+5)^4\) would also work, but it's long.
  2. Name the inside: let \(u=2x+5\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate both sides: \(\frac{du}{dx}=2\) (the 2x gives 2, and the constant 5 gives 0). Multiply both sides by dx: \(du=2\,dx\).
  4. Solve for dx: divide by 2, so \(dx=\frac{du}2\).
  5. Substitute everything: replace \((2x+5)\) with \(u\), and replace \(dx\) with \(\frac{du}2\): \[\int(2x+5)^4\,dx=\int u^4\cdot\frac{du}2\] Where the ½ comes from: \(\frac{du}2\) is the same as \(\frac12\cdot du\). Since \(\frac12\) is just a number, the constant multiple rule lets you move it in front of the integral: \[\int u^4\cdot\tfrac12\,du=\frac12\int u^4\,du\] This \(\frac12\) is called the correction factor. It makes up for the fact that \(du\) is twice as big as \(dx\).
  6. Integrate: \(\frac12\cdot\frac{u^5}5=\frac{u^5}{10}\).
  7. Put x back: \(\frac{(2x+5)^5}{10}\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{(2x+5)^5}{10}=\frac{5(2x+5)^4\cdot2}{10}=(2x+5)^4\) ✓. The chain rule's ×2 is exactly what the \(\frac12\) cancels.Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Answer\(\frac{(2x+5)^5}{10}+C\)

B9

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x}\)

the n = −1 trap

UsesLog rule

  1. Rewrite: \(\frac1x=x^{-1}\), so \(n=-1\).
  2. Try the power formula: \(-1+1=0\), giving \(\frac{x^0}{0}\). That divides by zero, which is impossible.
  3. This is why the power formula says \(n\neq-1\). The case \(n=-1\) has its own rule, the log rule: \(\int\frac{du}u=\ln|u|\).
  4. Apply it with \(u=x\): \(\ln|x|+C\). The absolute value is needed because ln only takes positive numbers, but x can be negative.

Answer\(\ln|x|+C\)

Slide examples

1·E1

\(\displaystyle\int(6x^2-4x+5)\,dx\)

Uses∫dx = x Constant multiple Sum rule Power formula

  1. Spot the form: a polynomial: a sum of powers of x with numbers in front. Integrate it one term at a time with the power formula.
  2. Split into terms: \(\int6x^2dx-\int4x\,dx+\int5\,dx\).Why? Sum rule: integrate each term separately, then add the results. See the rule
  3. Pull out the constants: \(6\int x^2dx-4\int x\,dx+5\int dx\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  4. Power formula on \(x^2\): \(\int x^2dx=\frac{x^{3}}{3}\) (exponent \(2+1=3\), divide by 3).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  5. Power formula on \(x\): \(x=x^1\), so \(\int x\,dx=\frac{x^2}2\) (exponent \(1+1=2\), divide by 2).
  6. Constant term: \(\int dx=x\), so \(5\int dx=5x\).Why? \(\int k\,dx=kx\): a constant integrates to that constant times x, because \(\frac{d}{dx}(kx)=k\). See the rule
  7. Put it together: \(6\cdot\frac{x^3}3-4\cdot\frac{x^2}2+5x\).
  8. Simplify: \(\frac63=2\) and \(\frac42=2\), so \(2x^3-2x^2+5x+C\). Add C once at the end.
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}(2x^3-2x^2+5x)=6x^2-4x+5\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(2x^3-2x^2+5x+C\)

1·E2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{4t+2}{\sqrt t}\,dt\)

UsesSum rule Power formula

  1. Spot the form: a fraction with a single power of t on the bottom. Split it and turn each piece into a power of t, then use the power formula.
  2. Write the root as a power: \(\sqrt t=t^{1/2}\), so the integral is \(\displaystyle\int\frac{4t+2}{t^{1/2}}\,dt\).Why? A root is a fractional power: \(\sqrt[n]{a^m}=a^{m/n}\). A square root is the power ½. See the rule
  3. Split the fraction: \(\dfrac{4t+2}{t^{1/2}}=\dfrac{4t}{t^{1/2}}+\dfrac{2}{t^{1/2}}\).Why? A sum on top can be split into separate fractions, \(\frac{a+b}c=\frac ac+\frac bc\), and the sum rule lets you integrate each piece separately. See the rule
  4. Simplify the first piece: \(\frac{4t}{t^{1/2}}=4t^{1-\frac12}=4t^{1/2}\).Why? Dividing powers with the same base subtracts the exponents: \(\frac{a^m}{a^n}=a^{m-n}\). See the rule
  5. Simplify the second piece: \(\frac{2}{t^{1/2}}=2t^{-1/2}\). The power moves up and becomes negative.Why? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  6. Rewrite the integral: \(\displaystyle\int\left(4t^{1/2}+2t^{-1/2}\right)dt\).
  7. Power formula on \(t^{1/2}\): \(\frac12+1=\frac32\): \(4\cdot\frac{t^{3/2}}{\tfrac32}=4\cdot\frac23\,t^{3/2}=\frac83t^{3/2}\). Dividing by \(\frac32\) is multiplying by \(\frac23\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the ruleWhy? Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip: \(a\div\frac bc=a\cdot\frac cb\). See the rule
  8. Power formula on \(t^{-1/2}\): \(-\frac12+1=\frac12\): \(2\cdot\frac{t^{1/2}}{\tfrac12}=2\cdot2\,t^{1/2}=4t^{1/2}\).
  9. Put it together: \(\frac83t^{3/2}+4t^{1/2}+C\).
  10. Check: \(\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac83t^{3/2}+4t^{1/2}\right)=\frac83\cdot\frac32t^{1/2}+4\cdot\frac12t^{-1/2}=4t^{1/2}+2t^{-1/2}\), which is \(\frac{4t+2}{\sqrt t}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac83t^{3/2}+4t^{1/2}+C\)

1·E3

\(\displaystyle\int(3x+1)^2\,dx\)

UsesSum rule Power formula u-substitution

  1. Spot the form: a power of a linear expression, \((3x+1)^2\). You can expand it (the power is small) or use the power formula with u. Both are shown.
  2. Way 1, expand: \((3x+1)^2=9x^2+6x+1\).Why? Expanding a square: \((a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2\). See the rule
  3. Integrate term by term: \(9\cdot\frac{x^3}3+6\cdot\frac{x^2}2+x=3x^3+3x^2+x+C\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  4. Way 2, name the inside: \(u=3x+1\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  5. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=3\), so \(du=3\,dx\).
  6. Match: the integral has \(dx\), so \(dx=\frac{du}3\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  7. Substitute everything: \(\int u^2\cdot\frac{du}3=\frac13\int u^2du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  8. Power formula: \(\frac13\cdot\frac{u^3}3=\frac{u^3}9\).
  9. Put x back: \(\frac{(3x+1)^3}9+C\).
  10. Why both are right: expanding gives \(\frac{(3x+1)^3}9=3x^3+3x^2+x+\frac19\). The extra \(\frac19\) is a constant, and \(C\) absorbs it.Why? C stands for any constant. A constant plus a number is still just a constant, so the extra number gets absorbed into C. See the rule
  11. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{(3x+1)^3}9=\frac{3(3x+1)^2\cdot3}9=(3x+1)^2\) ✓Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Answer\(\frac19(3x+1)^3+C\) or \(3x^3+3x^2+x+C\). Either one is a correct final answer.

1·E4

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{x^3\,dx}{(2x^4-5)^7}\)

UsesPower formula u-substitution Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: a power of \((2x^4-5)\) on the bottom, times \(x^3\) on top. The derivative of \(2x^4\) has an \(x^3\) in it, so use the power formula with u.
  2. Rewrite as a power: move the denominator up with a negative exponent: \(\displaystyle\int(2x^4-5)^{-7}\,x^3\,dx\).
  3. Name the inside: let \(u=2x^4-5\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  4. Differentiate both sides: \(\frac{du}{dx}=8x^3\) (the \(2x^4\) gives \(8x^3\), and the constant gives 0), so \(du=8x^3\,dx\).
  5. Match what you have: the integral has \(x^3\,dx\), not \(8x^3\,dx\). Divide both sides by 8: \(x^3\,dx=\frac{du}8\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int u^{-7}\cdot\frac{du}8=\frac18\int u^{-7}\,du\). Moving \(\frac18\) out front uses the constant multiple rule. It's the correction factor.
  7. Power formula: \(n=-7\), so \(n+1=-6\): \(\displaystyle\int u^{-7}\,du=\frac{u^{-6}}{-6}\).
  8. Multiply by the correction factor: \(\frac18\cdot\frac{u^{-6}}{-6}=-\frac{u^{-6}}{48}=-\frac{1}{48u^6}\). The negative exponent moves back down to make it positive.Why? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  9. Put x back: \(-\dfrac{1}{48(2x^4-5)^6}+C\).
  10. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left[-\frac1{48}(2x^4-5)^{-6}\right]=-\frac1{48}\cdot(-6)(2x^4-5)^{-7}\cdot8x^3=\frac{x^3}{(2x^4-5)^7}\) ✓

Answer\(-\dfrac{1}{48(2x^4-5)^6}+C\)

1·E5

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(2+3\tan y)^4}{\cos^2y}\,dy\)

UsesPower formula u-substitution

  1. Spot the form: a power of \((2+3\tan y)\) times something. If that something is the derivative of \(2+3\tan y\), use the power formula with u.
  2. Rewrite: \(\frac1{\cos^2y}=\sec^2y\), so the integral is \(\int(2+3\tan y)^4\sec^2y\,dy\).Why? \(\sec\theta\) is defined as \(\frac1{\cos\theta}\). Squaring both sides gives \(\frac1{\cos^2\theta}=\sec^2\theta\). See the rule
  3. Name the inside: \(u=2+3\tan y\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  4. Differentiate: \(\frac{d}{dy}\tan y=\sec^2y\), and the 2 gives 0, so \(\frac{du}{dy}=3\sec^2y\) and \(du=3\sec^2y\,dy\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\tan u=\sec^2u\), so going backwards \(\int\sec^2u\,du=\tan u\). See the rule
  5. Match: the integral has \(\sec^2y\,dy\), so \(\sec^2y\,dy=\frac{du}3\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(\int u^4\cdot\frac{du}3=\frac13\int u^4du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  7. Power formula: \(\frac13\cdot\frac{u^5}5=\frac{u^5}{15}\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  8. Put y back: \(\frac1{15}(2+3\tan y)^5+C\).
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dy}\frac{(2+3\tan y)^5}{15}=\frac{5(2+3\tan y)^4\cdot3\sec^2y}{15}=(2+3\tan y)^4\sec^2y\) ✓Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Answer\(\frac1{15}(2+3\tan y)^5+C\)

1·E6

\(\displaystyle\int\cot x\,\ln(\sin x)\,dx\)

UsesPower formula u-substitution

  1. Spot the form: a product of \(\ln(\sin x)\) and \(\cot x\). If one piece is the derivative of the other, use u-substitution and the power formula.
  2. Look for u: try \(u=\ln(\sin x)\) and check whether its derivative is in the integral.
  3. Differentiate with the chain rule: \(\ln(\sin x)\) is ln of an inside function, \(\sin x\). The rule is \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln(\text{inside})=\frac{1}{\text{inside}}\cdot(\text{derivative of inside})\). \[\frac{d}{dx}\ln(\sin x)=\frac{1}{\sin x}\cdot\underbrace{\cos x}_{\text{derivative of }\sin x}\] That's where the cos comes from: the derivative of \(\sin x\) is \(\cos x\), and the chain rule multiplies it on.Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside (ln gives \(\frac1{\text{inside}}\)) times the derivative of the inside (\(\sin x\to\cos x\)). See the rule
  4. Simplify: \(\frac{1}{\sin x}\cdot\cos x=\frac{\cos x}{\sin x}=\cot x\), and \(\cot x\) is right there in the integral.Why? \(\cot\theta=\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\), the flip of tan. See the rule
  5. Name it: \(u=\ln(\sin x)\), so \(du=\cot x\,dx\). This matches exactly, so the correction factor is 1.Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(\int\ln(\sin x)\cdot\cot x\,dx=\int u\,du\).
  7. Power formula: \(n=1\): \(\int u\,du=\frac{u^2}2\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  8. Put x back: \(\frac12\ln^2(\sin x)+C\), meaning \(\frac12[\ln(\sin x)]^2\).
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12[\ln(\sin x)]^2=\ln(\sin x)\cdot\frac{\cos x}{\sin x}=\ln(\sin x)\cot x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac12\ln^2(\sin x)+C\)

1·E7

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(4x-1)\,dx}{(2x+3)^5(x-2)^5}\)

UsesPower formula u-substitution

  1. Spot the form: two factors on the bottom, both to the power 5, and a polynomial on top. Combine the bottom into one power, then check if the top is its derivative.
  2. Combine the two bases: both have power 5, so \((2x+3)^5(x-2)^5=[(2x+3)(x-2)]^5\).Why? Powers with the same exponent can be combined: \(a^nb^n=(ab)^n\). See the rule
  3. Multiply out: \((2x+3)(x-2)=2x^2-4x+3x-6=2x^2-x-6\).
  4. Rewrite as a power: move the bottom up with a negative exponent: \(\int(2x^2-x-6)^{-5}(4x-1)\,dx\).Why? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  5. Name the inside: \(u=2x^2-x-6\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  6. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=4x-1\), so \(du=(4x-1)\,dx\). That's exactly the numerator, so no correction factor is needed.
  7. Substitute everything: \(\int u^{-5}du\).
  8. Power formula: \(-5+1=-4\), so \(\int u^{-5}du=\frac{u^{-4}}{-4}=-\frac1{4u^4}\). You divide by the new exponent \(-4\) (not \(-16\)).Common mistake: adding a \(\frac14\) in front. That's only needed when du is off by a constant. Here \(\frac{d}{dx}(2x^2-x-6)=4x-1\) is exactly the numerator, so the correction factor is 1. The \(\frac14\) in the answer comes from dividing by the exponent \(-4\). See the rule
  9. Put x back: \(-\frac1{4(2x^2-x-6)^4}+C\).
  10. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left[-\frac14u^{-4}\right]=u^{-5}\cdot u'=\frac{4x-1}{(2x^2-x-6)^5}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(-\dfrac{1}{4(2x^2-x-6)^4}+C\)

1·E8

\(\displaystyle\int(x^4+8)^9x^3\,dx\)

other ex. 1

UsesPower formula u-substitution Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: a power of \((x^4+8)\) times \(x^3\). The derivative of \(x^4\) has an \(x^3\) in it, so use the power formula with u.
  2. Name the inside: \(u=x^4+8\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=4x^3\), so \(du=4x^3\,dx\).
  4. Match: you have \(x^3\,dx\), so \(x^3\,dx=\frac{du}4\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\int u^9\cdot\frac{du}4=\frac14\int u^9du\). The \(\frac14\) is the correction factor.Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Power formula: \(9+1=10\): \(\frac14\cdot\frac{u^{10}}{10}=\frac{u^{10}}{40}\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  7. Put x back: \(\frac{(x^4+8)^{10}}{40}+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{(x^4+8)^{10}}{40}=\frac{10(x^4+8)^9\cdot4x^3}{40}=(x^4+8)^9x^3\) ✓Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Slide mistake: the slide's last line writes \(/10\). It should be \(/40\), as the line above it shows.

Answer\(\frac{(x^4+8)^{10}}{40}+C\)

1·E9

\(\displaystyle\int x\sqrt{x^2+1}\,dx\)

other ex. 2

UsesPower formula u-substitution Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: a root of \((x^2+1)\) times \(x\). The derivative of \(x^2\) is \(2x\), so use the power formula with u.
  2. Rewrite the root: \(\sqrt{x^2+1}=(x^2+1)^{1/2}\).Why? A root is a fractional power: \(\sqrt[n]{a^m}=a^{m/n}\). A square root is the power ½. See the rule
  3. Name the inside: \(u=x^2+1\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  4. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=2x\), so \(du=2x\,dx\).
  5. Match: you have \(x\,dx\), so \(x\,dx=\frac{du}2\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(\int u^{1/2}\cdot\frac{du}2=\frac12\int u^{1/2}du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  7. Power formula: \(\frac12+1=\frac32\): \(\frac12\cdot\frac{u^{3/2}}{3/2}=\frac12\cdot\frac23u^{3/2}=\frac13u^{3/2}\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the ruleWhy? Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip: \(a\div\frac bc=a\cdot\frac cb\). See the rule
  8. Put x back: \(\frac13(x^2+1)^{3/2}+C\).
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac13(x^2+1)^{3/2}=\frac13\cdot\frac32(x^2+1)^{1/2}\cdot2x=x\sqrt{x^2+1}\) ✓Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Answer\(\frac13(x^2+1)^{3/2}+C\)

1·E10

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{\cos x\,dx}{(2+\sin x)^4}\)

other ex. 3

UsesPower formula u-substitution

  1. Spot the form: a power of \((2+\sin x)\) on the bottom, with \(\cos x\) on top. \(\cos x\) is the derivative of \(\sin x\), so use the power formula with u.
  2. Rewrite as a power: \(\int(2+\sin x)^{-4}\cos x\,dx\).Why? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  3. Name the inside: \(u=2+\sin x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  4. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=\cos x\), so \(du=\cos x\,dx\). This matches exactly, so the correction factor is 1.
  5. Substitute everything: \(\int u^{-4}du\).
  6. Power formula: \(-4+1=-3\): \(\frac{u^{-3}}{-3}=-\frac1{3u^3}\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  7. Put x back: \(-\frac1{3(2+\sin x)^3}+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left[-\frac13(2+\sin x)^{-3}\right]=(2+\sin x)^{-4}\cos x\) ✓Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Answer\(-\dfrac{1}{3(2+\sin x)^3}+C\)

Exercises · full solutions

1·X1

\(\displaystyle\int\left(\sqrt t-\sqrt[3]{t^2}+\sqrt[5]{t^3}\right)dt\)

UsesSum rule Power formula

  1. Rewrite every root as a power using \(\sqrt[n]{t^m}=t^{m/n}\): \(\sqrt t=t^{1/2}\), \(\sqrt[3]{t^2}=t^{2/3}\), \(\sqrt[5]{t^3}=t^{3/5}\).Why? A root is a fractional power: \(\sqrt[n]{a^m}=a^{m/n}\). A square root is the power ½. See the rule
  2. Add 1 to each exponent: \(\frac12+1=\frac32\), \(\frac23+1=\frac53\), \(\frac35+1=\frac85\).
  3. Divide by the new exponents (flip and multiply): \(\frac23t^{3/2}-\frac35t^{5/3}+\frac58t^{8/5}\).Why? Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip: \(\div\frac ab=\times\frac ba\). See the rule
  4. Add C.

Answer\(\frac23t^{3/2}-\frac35t^{5/3}+\frac58t^{8/5}+C\)

1·X2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{\tan2x\,dx}{(3+\ln\cos2x)^6}\)

UsesPower formula u-substitution Correction factor

  1. Rewrite: \(\int(3+\ln\cos2x)^{-6}\tan2x\,dx\).
  2. Name the inside: \(u=3+\ln\cos2x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate (chain rule twice): \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln\cos2x=\frac{1}{\cos2x}\cdot(-\sin2x)\cdot2=-2\tan2x\). So \(du=-2\tan2x\,dx\).Why? \(\sec\theta\) is defined as \(\frac1{\cos\theta}\). Squaring both sides gives \(\frac1{\cos^2\theta}=\sec^2\theta\). See the ruleWhy? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule
  4. Match: \(\tan2x\,dx=-\frac{du}2\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute: \(-\frac12\int u^{-6}du\).
  6. Power formula: \(-\frac12\cdot\frac{u^{-5}}{-5}=\frac{u^{-5}}{10}=\frac1{10u^5}\).
  7. Put x back: \(\frac1{10(3+\ln\cos2x)^5}+C\).

Answer\(\dfrac{1}{10(3+\ln\cos2x)^5}+C\)

1·X3

\(\displaystyle\int\cot3x\csc^33x\,dx\)

UsesPower formula u-substitution

  1. Regroup so one factor is a derivative: \(\cot3x\csc^33x=\csc^23x\cdot(\csc3x\cot3x)\).
  2. Name the inside: \(u=\csc3x\). Since \(\frac{d}{dx}\csc3x=-3\csc3x\cot3x\), \(du=-3\csc3x\cot3x\,dx\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Match: \(\csc3x\cot3x\,dx=-\frac{du}3\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  4. Substitute: \(-\frac13\int u^2du\).
  5. Power formula: \(-\frac13\cdot\frac{u^3}3=-\frac{u^3}9\).
  6. Put x back: \(-\frac19\csc^33x+C\).

Answer\(-\frac19\csc^33x+C\)

1·X4

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(1+3e^{-2x})^8}{e^{2x}}\,dx\)

UsesPower formula u-substitution Correction factor

  1. Rewrite \(\frac1{e^{2x}}\) as \(e^{-2x}\): \(\int(1+3e^{-2x})^8e^{-2x}\,dx\).Why? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  2. Name the inside: \(u=1+3e^{-2x}\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(du=3\cdot(-2)e^{-2x}dx=-6e^{-2x}dx\).
  4. Match: \(e^{-2x}dx=-\frac{du}6\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute: \(-\frac16\int u^8du\).
  6. Power formula: \(-\frac16\cdot\frac{u^9}9=-\frac{u^9}{54}\).
  7. Put x back: \(-\frac1{54}(1+3e^{-2x})^9+C\).

Answer\(-\frac1{54}(1+3e^{-2x})^9+C\)

1·X5

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(2x+1)\,dx}{(x+4)^6(x-3)^6}\)

UsesPower formula u-substitution

  1. Combine the bases (same power 6): \((x+4)^6(x-3)^6=[(x+4)(x-3)]^6\).Why? Powers with the same exponent can be combined: \(a^nb^n=(ab)^n\). See the rule
  2. Multiply out: \((x+4)(x-3)=x^2+x-12\).
  3. Name the inside: \(u=x^2+x-12\), so \(du=(2x+1)\,dx\). That's exactly the numerator.Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  4. Substitute: \(\int u^{-6}du\).
  5. Power formula: \(\frac{u^{-5}}{-5}=-\frac1{5u^5}\).
  6. Put x back: \(-\frac1{5(x^2+x-12)^5}+C\).

Answer\(-\dfrac{1}{5(x^2+x-12)^5}+C\)

1·X6

\(\displaystyle\int\sin^4(e^{3x})\cos(e^{3x})\,e^{3x}\,dx\)

UsesPower formula u-substitution

  1. Find the inside of the power: \(\sin^4(e^{3x})=[\sin(e^{3x})]^4\), so let \(u=\sin(e^{3x})\).
  2. Differentiate (chain rule): \(du=\cos(e^{3x})\cdot3e^{3x}\,dx\).Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule
  3. Match: \(\cos(e^{3x})\,e^{3x}dx=\frac{du}3\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  4. Substitute: \(\frac13\int u^4du\).
  5. Power formula: \(\frac13\cdot\frac{u^5}5=\frac{u^5}{15}\).
  6. Put x back: \(\frac1{15}\sin^5(e^{3x})+C\).

Answer\(\frac1{15}\sin^5(e^{3x})+C\)

LESSON 2

Integrals Yielding Natural Logarithms

\(\int\frac{du}{u}=\ln|u|+C\). If the numerator is the derivative of the denominator, up to a constant, the answer is the log of the denominator.

  1. If the numerator's degree is ≥ the denominator's, do long division first.
  2. Let \(u\) = denominator and compute \(du\). Fix any constant with a correction factor.
  3. If the numerator only partly matches, split it into (multiple of \(du\)) + (leftover). See example 5.

Formula practice · log rule

2·P1

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x+4}\)

UsesLog rule

  1. Spot the form: a fraction with an expression in x on the bottom. If the top is the derivative of the bottom, use the log rule \(\int\frac{du}u=\ln|u|\).Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  2. Name the bottom: \(u=x+4\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=1\) (x gives 1, and the constant 4 gives 0), so \(du=dx\).
  4. Match: the top is \(dx\), which is exactly \(du\). The correction factor is 1, so nothing goes in front.
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x+4}=\int\frac{du}u\).
  6. Log rule: \(\int\frac{du}u=\ln|u|+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\ln|x+4|+C\). Keep the bars: \(x+4\) is negative when \(x<-4\).Why? ln only accepts positive inputs. The bars in \(\ln|u|\) keep the answer valid when u is negative. If u can never be negative (like \(x^2+5\)), you can drop them. See the rule
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|x+4|=\frac1{x+4}\cdot1=\frac1{x+4}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\ln|x+4|+C\)

2·P2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{3\,dx}{3x-2}\)

UsesLog rule

  1. Spot the form: a fraction. Check whether the top is the derivative of the bottom. If so, it is the log rule \(\int\frac{du}u=\ln|u|\).Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  2. Name the bottom: \(u=3x-2\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=3\) (the \(3x\) gives 3, and the constant \(-2\) gives 0), so \(du=3\,dx\).
  4. Match: the top is \(3\,dx\), which is exactly \(du\). The correction factor is 1. (If the top were only \(dx\), you would write \(dx=\frac{du}3\) and put \(\frac13\) in front.)Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{3\,dx}{3x-2}=\int\frac{du}u\).
  6. Log rule: \(\int\frac{du}u=\ln|u|+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\ln|3x-2|+C\). Keep the bars: \(3x-2\) is negative when \(x<\frac23\).Why? ln only accepts positive inputs. The bars in \(\ln|u|\) keep the answer valid when u is negative. If u can never be negative (like \(x^2+5\)), you can drop them. See the rule
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|3x-2|=\frac1{3x-2}\cdot3=\frac3{3x-2}\) ✓. The 3 comes from the chain rule (derivative of the inside).Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Answer\(\ln|3x-2|+C\)

2·P3

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{x\,dx}{x^2+5}\)

UsesLog rule Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: the top \(x\) is almost the derivative of the bottom (\(2x\)). It is off by a number, so use the log rule with a correction factor.Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  2. Name the bottom: \(u=x^2+5\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=2x\), so \(du=2x\,dx\).
  4. Match: the top is \(x\,dx\), not \(2x\,dx\). Divide both sides by 2: \(x\,dx=\frac{du}2\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac1u\cdot\frac{du}2=\frac12\int\frac{du}u\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Log rule: \(\frac12\ln|u|+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\frac12\ln(x^2+5)+C\). No bars needed: \(x^2\ge0\), so \(x^2+5\ge5>0\).Why? ln only accepts positive inputs. The bars in \(\ln|u|\) keep the answer valid when u is negative. If u can never be negative (like \(x^2+5\)), you can drop them. See the rule
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12\ln(x^2+5)=\frac12\cdot\frac{2x}{x^2+5}=\frac{x}{x^2+5}\) ✓

Answer\(\frac12\ln(x^2+5)+C\)

2·P4

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(2x+3)\,dx}{x^2+3x-1}\)

UsesLog rule

  1. Spot the form: a fraction. Differentiate the bottom and see if it matches the top.Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  2. Name the bottom: \(u=x^2+3x-1\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=2x+3\) (\(x^2\to2x\), \(3x\to3\), \(-1\to0\)), so \(du=(2x+3)\,dx\).
  4. Match: that is exactly the top \((2x+3)\,dx\). The correction factor is 1.
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{(2x+3)\,dx}{x^2+3x-1}=\int\frac{du}u\).
  6. Log rule: \(\ln|u|+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\ln|x^2+3x-1|+C\). Keep the bars: at \(x=0\) the bottom is \(-1\).Why? ln only accepts positive inputs. The bars in \(\ln|u|\) keep the answer valid when u is negative. If u can never be negative (like \(x^2+5\)), you can drop them. See the rule
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|x^2+3x-1|=\frac{2x+3}{x^2+3x-1}\) ✓

Answer\(\ln|x^2+3x-1|+C\)

2·P5

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{\cos x\,dx}{1+\sin x}\)

UsesLog rule

  1. Spot the form: the derivative of \(\sin x\) is \(\cos x\), and \(\cos x\) is on top. So the top is the derivative of the bottom: log rule.Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  2. Name the bottom: \(u=1+\sin x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=0+\cos x\), so \(du=\cos x\,dx\).
  4. Match: that is exactly the top \(\cos x\,dx\). The correction factor is 1.
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{\cos x\,dx}{1+\sin x}=\int\frac{du}u\).
  6. Log rule: \(\ln|u|+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\ln|1+\sin x|\). Since \(\sin x\ge-1\), \(1+\sin x\ge0\), so the bars can go: \(\ln(1+\sin x)+C\).Why? ln only accepts positive inputs. The bars in \(\ln|u|\) keep the answer valid when u is negative. If u can never be negative (like \(x^2+5\)), you can drop them. See the rule
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln(1+\sin x)=\frac{\cos x}{1+\sin x}\) ✓

Answer\(\ln(1+\sin x)+C\)

2·P6

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x\ln x}\)

UsesLog rule u-substitution

  1. Regroup: \(\frac1{x\ln x}=\frac1{\ln x}\cdot\frac1x\). Now you can see a \(\frac1x\), the derivative of \(\ln x\).Why? \(\frac1{ab}=\frac1a\cdot\frac1b\): a product on the bottom can be split into two fractions multiplied together. See the rule
  2. Name u: \(u=\ln x\), because its derivative \(\frac1x\) is sitting in the integral.Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=\frac1x\), so \(du=\frac1x\,dx\).
  4. Match: the \(\frac1x\,dx\) in the integral is exactly \(du\). The correction factor is 1.
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac1{\ln x}\cdot\frac{dx}x=\int\frac{du}u\).
  6. Log rule: \(\ln|u|+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\ln|\ln x|+C\). Keep the bars: \(\ln x\) is negative when \(0Why? ln only accepts positive inputs. The bars in \(\ln|u|\) keep the answer valid when u is negative. If u can never be negative (like \(x^2+5\)), you can drop them. See the rule
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|\ln x|=\frac1{\ln x}\cdot\frac1x=\frac1{x\ln x}\) ✓ (chain rule)Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Answer\(\ln|\ln x|+C\)

Slide examples

2·E1

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(x+3)\,dx}{x^2+6x-1}\)

UsesLog rule Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: a fraction with a polynomial on the bottom. Its derivative \(2x+6\) is a multiple of the top \(x+3\), so use the log rule.Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  2. Name the bottom: \(u=x^2+6x-1\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=2x+6\), so \(du=(2x+6)\,dx\).
  4. Factor: \(2x+6=2(x+3)\), so \(du=2(x+3)\,dx\). Now the top \((x+3)\) is visible inside du.
  5. Match: the top \((x+3)\,dx\) is half of \(du\), so \((x+3)\,dx=\frac{du}2\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{(x+3)\,dx}{x^2+6x-1}=\frac12\int\frac{du}u\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  7. Log rule: \(\frac12\ln|u|+C\).
  8. Put x back: \(\frac12\ln|x^2+6x-1|+C\). Keep the bars: \(x^2+6x-1\) can be negative (try \(x=0\)).Why? ln only accepts positive inputs. The bars in \(\ln|u|\) keep the answer valid when u is negative. If u can never be negative (like \(x^2+5\)), you can drop them. See the rule
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12\ln|x^2+6x-1|=\frac12\cdot\frac{2x+6}{x^2+6x-1}=\frac{x+3}{x^2+6x-1}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac12\ln|x^2+6x-1|+C\)

2·E2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(x^3+3)\,dx}{x+1}\)

UsesLong division Power formula Log rule

  1. Check the degrees: top degree 3 ≥ bottom degree 1, so this is improper. Divide first.Why? When the top's degree is at least the bottom's, divide first, like \(\frac72=3+\frac12\): quotient + remainder ÷ divisor. See the rule
  2. Long division, fill in the gaps: write the top with every power of x, using 0 for the missing ones: \(x^3+0x^2+0x+3\).Why? The zeros keep each power in its own column, so you never subtract an \(x^2\) from an \(x\). It's the same reason you write the 0 in 305 when dividing numbers. See the rule
  3. Round 1: divide the leading terms: \(x^3\div x=x^2\). That's the first term of the answer (the quotient). Multiply it back: \(x^2(x+1)=x^3+x^2\). Subtract: \((x^3+0x^2)-(x^3+x^2)=-x^2\). Bring down the next term: \(-x^2+0x\).Why? Polynomial long division repeats the same 4 moves as number long division: divide the leading terms, multiply back, subtract, bring down. You only ever divide the highest-power terms, and the subtraction always cancels that term. See the rule
  4. Round 2: divide: \(-x^2\div x=-x\), the next quotient term. Multiply: \(-x(x+1)=-x^2-x\). Subtract: \((-x^2+0x)-(-x^2-x)=-x^2+0x+x^2+x=x\). Bring down the 3: \(x+3\).Why? Subtracting a negative is adding: \(-(-x^2-x)=+x^2+x\). Most long-division mistakes are sign slips here, so change every sign of the line you subtract, then add. See the rule
  5. Round 3: divide: \(x\div x=1\), the next quotient term. Multiply: \(1\cdot(x+1)=x+1\). Subtract: \((x+3)-(x+1)=2\).
  6. Stop: 2 has no x (degree 0), which is lower than the degree of \(x+1\) (degree 1), so it can't be divided any more. It's the remainder. The quotient is the three terms you found: \(x^2-x+1\).
  7. Write the result: \(\dfrac{\text{top}}{\text{bottom}}=\text{quotient}+\dfrac{\text{remainder}}{\text{bottom}}\), so \(\dfrac{x^3+3}{x+1}=x^2-x+1+\dfrac2{x+1}\).Why? When the top's degree is at least the bottom's, divide first, like \(\frac72=3+\frac12\): quotient + remainder ÷ divisor. See the rule
  8. Check the division: quotient × bottom + remainder should give the top: \((x^2-x+1)(x+1)+2=x^3+x^2-x^2-x+x+1+2=x^3+3\) ✓
  9. Easier way, synthetic division: when the bottom is \(x\) plus or minus a number (like \(x+1\) or \(x-3\)), you can skip the long division and work with only the numbers in front of each power.
    x³x²xnum
    −11003
    −11−1
    1−112
    1. The box number: set the bottom equal to 0 and solve: \(x+1=0\Rightarrow x=-1\). Write \(-1\) on the left.
    2. Top row: the numbers in front of \(x^3,x^2,x,\) and the constant in \(x^3+0x^2+0x+3\): 1, 0, 0, 3. Don't skip the zeros.
    3. Drop the first number straight down: 1.
    4. Multiply, then add, and repeat: \(1\times(-1)=-1\), write it under the 0, and \(0+(-1)=-1\). Next, \(-1\times(-1)=1\), and \(0+1=1\). Then \(1\times(-1)=-1\), and \(3+(-1)=2\).
    5. Read the bottom row: the last number, 2, is the remainder. The others, 1, −1, 1, are the quotient, one power lower than the top: \(1x^2-1x+1=x^2-x+1\).
    Why does it work? It does the same divide, multiply, subtract moves as long division, but with only the numbers. Using \(-1\) instead of \(+1\) lets you add instead of subtract, which avoids the sign slips. It only works for a bottom of the form \(x-c\). For a bottom like \(x^2+1\) (2·X4), use long division. See the rule Quick remainder check: plug the box number into the top: \((-1)^3+3=2\). That's the remainder, so it matches (this is the remainder theorem).
  10. Integrate the polynomial part: \(\frac{x^3}3-\frac{x^2}2+x\).
  11. Integrate the remainder: \(2\int\frac{dx}{x+1}=2\ln|x+1|\) (log rule, \(u=x+1\)).
  12. Combine: \(\frac{x^3}3-\frac{x^2}2+x+2\ln|x+1|+C\).

Answer\(\frac{x^3}3-\frac{x^2}2+x+2\ln|x+1|+C\)

2·E3

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x(1+2\ln x)}\)

UsesLog rule Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: a fraction with \(x\) and \((1+2\ln x)\) on the bottom. The derivative of \(\ln x\) is \(\frac1x\), and that \(\frac1x\) is already there, so use the log rule.Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  2. Regroup: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x(1+2\ln x)}=\int\frac{1}{1+2\ln x}\cdot\frac{dx}x\).Why? \(\frac1{ab}=\frac1a\cdot\frac1b\): a product on the bottom can be split into two fractions multiplied together. See the rule
  3. Name the bottom factor: \(u=1+2\ln x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  4. Differentiate: \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln x=\frac1x\) and the 1 gives 0, so \(\frac{du}{dx}=\frac2x\) and \(du=\frac2x\,dx\).
  5. Match: the integral has \(\frac{dx}x\), so \(\frac{dx}x=\frac{du}2\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac1u\cdot\frac{du}2=\frac12\int\frac{du}u\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  7. Log rule: \(\frac12\ln|u|+C\).
  8. Put x back: \(\frac12\ln|1+2\ln x|+C\). Keep the bars: \(1+2\ln x\) is negative when \(xWhy? ln only accepts positive inputs. The bars in \(\ln|u|\) keep the answer valid when u is negative. If u can never be negative (like \(x^2+5\)), you can drop them. See the rule
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12\ln|1+2\ln x|=\frac12\cdot\frac{2/x}{1+2\ln x}=\frac1{x(1+2\ln x)}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac12\ln|1+2\ln x|+C\)

2·E4

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{\sin2x\,dx}{3+\sin^2x}\)

UsesLog rule Double angle

  1. Spot the form: a fraction with \(3+\sin^2x\) on the bottom. Check whether the top is its derivative. If so, it's the log rule.Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  2. Name the bottom: \(u=3+\sin^2x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\sin^2x=(\sin x)^2\). The chain rule gives \(2\sin x\cdot\cos x\), and the 3 gives 0, so \(du=2\sin x\cos x\,dx\).Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule
  4. Use the double angle: \(2\sin x\cos x=\sin2x\), so \(du=\sin2x\,dx\). That is exactly the top, so the correction factor is 1.Why? This comes from \(\sin(A+B)=\sin A\cos B+\cos A\sin B\) with \(A=B=\theta\). See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{\sin2x\,dx}{3+\sin^2x}=\int\frac{du}u\).
  6. Log rule: \(\ln|u|+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\ln(3+\sin^2x)+C\). No bars needed: \(\sin^2x\ge0\), so \(3+\sin^2x\) is always positive.Why? ln only accepts positive inputs. The bars in \(\ln|u|\) keep the answer valid when u is negative. If u can never be negative (like \(x^2+5\)), you can drop them. See the rule
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln(3+\sin^2x)=\frac{2\sin x\cos x}{3+\sin^2x}=\frac{\sin2x}{3+\sin^2x}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\ln(3+\sin^2x)+C\)

2·E5

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(x+8)\,dx}{(x+3)^2}\)

UsesLog rule Power formula

  1. Spot the form: a fraction with \((x+3)^2\) on the bottom. The bottom's derivative is \(2(x+3)\), which does not match the top \(x+8\). So split the top first.
  2. Rewrite the top: write \(x+8\) using the bottom's base: \(x+8=(x+3)+5\).Why? Rewrite the top as (a multiple of the derivative of the bottom) + (a leftover number), so one piece fits the log or power rule and the other fits a different rule. See the rule
  3. Split the fraction: \(\dfrac{(x+3)+5}{(x+3)^2}=\dfrac{x+3}{(x+3)^2}+\dfrac5{(x+3)^2}\).Why? A sum on top can be split into separate fractions, \(\frac{a+b}c=\frac ac+\frac bc\), and the sum rule lets you integrate each piece separately. See the rule
  4. Simplify the first piece: \(\dfrac{x+3}{(x+3)^2}=\dfrac1{x+3}\) (one \(x+3\) cancels).
  5. Name the inside: \(u=x+3\), so \(du=dx\). The integral is \(\displaystyle\int\frac{du}u+5\int u^{-2}du\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the ruleWhy? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  6. First part, log rule: \(\int\frac{du}u=\ln|u|\).Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  7. Second part, power formula: \(-2+1=-1\): \(5\cdot\frac{u^{-1}}{-1}=-\frac5u\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  8. Put x back and combine: \(\ln|x+3|-\frac5{x+3}+C\).
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left(\ln|x+3|-5(x+3)^{-1}\right)=\frac1{x+3}+\frac5{(x+3)^2}=\frac{(x+3)+5}{(x+3)^2}=\frac{x+8}{(x+3)^2}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\ln|x+3|-\dfrac5{x+3}+C\)

Exercises · full solutions

2·X1

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dy}{\sqrt y\,(3+\sqrt y)}\)

UsesLog rule Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: there's a \(\sqrt y\) inside \(3+\sqrt y\), and a \(\frac1{\sqrt y}\) outside it. The derivative of \(\sqrt y\) has a \(\frac1{\sqrt y}\) in it, so a u-substitution will work, and the result will be a log.Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  2. Regroup: split the bottom into two fractions: \(\dfrac1{\sqrt y\,(3+\sqrt y)}=\dfrac1{3+\sqrt y}\cdot\dfrac1{\sqrt y}\). Now the integral is \(\displaystyle\int\frac1{3+\sqrt y}\cdot\frac{dy}{\sqrt y}\).Why? \(\frac1{ab}=\frac1a\cdot\frac1b\): a product on the bottom can be split into two fractions multiplied together. See the rule
  3. Name the bottom factor: \(u=3+\sqrt y\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  4. Rewrite the root as a power: \(\sqrt y=y^{1/2}\), so \(u=3+y^{1/2}\). Now you can use the power rule to differentiate.Why? A root is a fractional power: \(\sqrt[n]{a^m}=a^{m/n}\). A square root is the power ½. See the rule
  5. Differentiate each term: the 3 is a constant, so its derivative is 0. For \(y^{1/2}\), bring the ½ down in front and subtract 1 from the power: \(\frac12y^{\frac12-1}=\frac12y^{-1/2}\). So \(\frac{du}{dy}=0+\frac12y^{-1/2}\).Why? Power rule for derivatives: \(\frac{d}{dy}y^n=ny^{n-1}\). Here \(n=\frac12\), and \(\frac12-1=-\frac12\). See the rule
  6. Turn the negative power back into a root: \(y^{-1/2}=\dfrac1{y^{1/2}}=\dfrac1{\sqrt y}\), so \(\dfrac{du}{dy}=\dfrac12\cdot\dfrac1{\sqrt y}=\dfrac1{2\sqrt y}\).Why? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  7. Write du: multiply both sides by \(dy\): \(du=\dfrac{dy}{2\sqrt y}\).
  8. Match: the integral has \(\dfrac{dy}{\sqrt y}\), not \(\dfrac{dy}{2\sqrt y}\). Multiply both sides of the du equation by 2: \(\dfrac{dy}{\sqrt y}=2\,du\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  9. Substitute everything: \(3+\sqrt y\) becomes u, and \(\frac{dy}{\sqrt y}\) becomes \(2\,du\): \(\displaystyle\int\frac1{3+\sqrt y}\cdot\frac{dy}{\sqrt y}=\int\frac1u\cdot2\,du=2\int\frac{du}u\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  10. Log rule: \(2\int\frac{du}u=2\ln|u|+C\).
  11. Put y back: \(2\ln|3+\sqrt y|+C\). Since \(\sqrt y\ge0\), \(3+\sqrt y\ge3>0\), so the bars can go: \(2\ln(3+\sqrt y)+C\).Why? ln only accepts positive inputs. The bars in \(\ln|u|\) keep the answer valid when u is negative. If u can never be negative (like \(x^2+5\)), you can drop them. See the rule
  12. Check: \(\dfrac{d}{dy}\,2\ln(3+\sqrt y)=2\cdot\dfrac1{3+\sqrt y}\cdot\dfrac1{2\sqrt y}=\dfrac1{\sqrt y\,(3+\sqrt y)}\) ✓ (chain rule: the derivative of the inside, \(\frac1{2\sqrt y}\), comes out, and its 2 cancels the 2 in front)Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(2\ln(3+\sqrt y)+C\)

2·X2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{\sec^2x\tan x\,dx}{5+\tan^2x}\)

UsesLog rule Correction factor

  1. Name the bottom: \(u=5+\tan^2x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  2. Differentiate: \(du=2\tan x\cdot\sec^2x\,dx\).
  3. Match: the top is \(\sec^2x\tan x\,dx=\frac{du}2\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  4. Log rule: \(\frac12\int\frac{du}u=\frac12\ln(5+\tan^2x)+C\).

Answer\(\frac12\ln(5+\tan^2x)+C\)

2·X3

\(\displaystyle\int\cot x\,dx\)

UsesLog rule

  1. Rewrite: \(\cot x=\frac{\cos x}{\sin x}\).Why? \(\cot\theta=\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\), the flip of tan. See the rule
  2. Name the bottom: \(u=\sin x\), so \(du=\cos x\,dx\), the top.Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Log rule: \(\int\frac{du}u=\ln|\sin x|+C\).

Answer\(\ln|\sin x|+C\)

2·X4

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(x^3+2x)\,dx}{x^2+1}\)

UsesLong division Power formula Log rule

  1. Check the degrees: 3 over 2, so this is improper. Divide first.Why? When the top's degree is at least the bottom's, divide first, like \(\frac72=3+\frac12\): quotient + remainder ÷ divisor. See the rule
  2. Divide: \(x^3+2x=x(x^2+1)+x\), so \(\frac{x^3+2x}{x^2+1}=x+\frac{x}{x^2+1}\).
  3. First part: \(\int x\,dx=\frac{x^2}2\).
  4. Second part: \(u=x^2+1\), \(du=2x\,dx\), giving \(\frac12\int\frac{du}u=\frac12\ln(x^2+1)\).
  5. Combine: \(\frac{x^2}2+\frac12\ln(x^2+1)+C\).

Answer\(\frac{x^2}2+\frac12\ln(x^2+1)+C\)

2·X5

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{e^{2x}\,dx}{e^x+1}\)

UsesLog rule u-substitution

  1. Spot the form: the bottom is \(e^x+1\), and its derivative is \(e^x\). The top \(e^{2x}\) contains an \(e^x\) (since \(e^{2x}=e^x\cdot e^x\)), so substitute for the bottom. One extra \(e^x\) will be left over, and you'll have to write it in terms of u too.
  2. Name the bottom: \(u=e^x+1\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=e^x+0\) (\(e^x\) is its own derivative, and the 1 gives 0), so \(du=e^x\,dx\).
  4. Split \(e^{2x}\): \(e^{2x}\,dx=e^x\cdot e^x\,dx\).Why? Exponent law: \(e^{2x}=e^{x+x}=e^x\cdot e^x=(e^x)^2\). See the rule
  5. Use one \(e^x\) for du: \(e^x\,dx=du\), from step 3. That leaves one \(e^x\) still in x.
  6. Write the leftover \(e^x\) in terms of u: start from \(u=e^x+1\) and solve for \(e^x\) by subtracting 1 from both sides: \(u-1=e^x\). So \(e^x=u-1\).Why? After substituting, the integral can only contain u and du, never x. When a piece is left over, go back to your u equation and solve it for that piece. It's the same as solving \(u=e^x+1\) like any equation. See the rule
  7. Substitute everything: the bottom \(e^x+1\) becomes u, \(e^x\,dx\) becomes du, and the leftover \(e^x\) becomes \(u-1\): \(\displaystyle\int\frac{e^x\cdot e^x\,dx}{e^x+1}=\int\frac{(u-1)\,du}u\).
  8. Split the fraction: \(\dfrac{u-1}u=\dfrac uu-\dfrac1u=1-\dfrac1u\), so the integral is \(\displaystyle\int\left(1-\frac1u\right)du\).Why? A sum or difference on top can be split into separate fractions, \(\frac{a-b}c=\frac ac-\frac bc\), and the sum rule lets you integrate each piece separately. See the rule
  9. Integrate each term: \(\int1\,du=u\) and \(\int\frac{du}u=\ln|u|\), so you get \(u-\ln|u|+C\).Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  10. Put x back: \(e^x+1-\ln(e^x+1)+C\). No bars are needed, because \(e^x>0\), so \(e^x+1>1\).
  11. Tidy up the constant: the \(+1\) is just a number, and \(1+C\) is still "some constant", so it is absorbed into C: \(e^x-\ln(e^x+1)+C\). Both forms are correct.Why? C stands for any constant. Adding 1 to any constant gives another constant, so you can write the whole thing as one C. See the rule
  12. Check: \(\dfrac{d}{dx}\left[e^x-\ln(e^x+1)\right]=e^x-\dfrac{e^x}{e^x+1}=\dfrac{e^x(e^x+1)-e^x}{e^x+1}=\dfrac{e^{2x}}{e^x+1}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(e^x-\ln(e^x+1)+C\)

2·X6

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x(1+x^2)}\)

UsesLog rule

  1. Trick: multiply top and bottom by \(x^{-3}\). The bottom \(x(1+x^2)=x^3(x^{-2}+1)\) becomes \(x^{-2}+1\), so the integral is \(\int\frac{x^{-3}\,dx}{x^{-2}+1}\).
  2. Name the bottom: \(u=1+x^{-2}\), so \(du=-2x^{-3}dx\) and \(x^{-3}dx=-\frac{du}2\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Log rule: \(-\frac12\int\frac{du}u=-\frac12\ln\left(1+\frac1{x^2}\right)\).Why? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  4. Simplify: \(1+\frac1{x^2}=\frac{x^2+1}{x^2}\), and \(\ln\frac ab=\ln a-\ln b\), so \(-\frac12[\ln(x^2+1)-2\ln|x|]=\ln|x|-\frac12\ln(1+x^2)\).Why? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the ruleWhy? Log quotient rule: \(\ln\frac ab=\ln a-\ln b\), and \(\ln x^2=2\ln|x|\). See the rule
  5. Partial fractions (Lesson 10) gives the same answer.

Answer\(\ln|x|-\frac12\ln(1+x^2)+C\)

LESSON 3

Integration of Exponential Functions

\(\int e^u\,du=e^u+C\) and \(\int a^u\,du=\frac{a^u}{\ln a}+C\). The exponent is your u, so make sure its derivative is in the integrand.

  1. Simplify first: \(e^{\ln x^3}=x^3\), \(\ln e^{2x}=2x\), \(\sqrt{a^u}=a^{u/2}\). Expand squares and split fractions over \(e^x\).
  2. Let \(u\) = the exponent, compute \(du\), and fix the constant.
  3. Base \(e\): the answer is the same function. Any other base \(a\): divide by \(\ln a\).

Formula practice · exponentials

3·P1

\(\displaystyle\int e^{5x}\,dx\)

Uses∫eᵘ Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: e to a power. Use \(\int e^u\,du=e^u\), with u = the exponent.Why? \(\frac{d}{du}e^u=e^u\): e to a power is its own derivative, so it is also its own antiderivative. Only the correction factor changes. See the rule
  2. Name the exponent: \(u=5x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=5\), so \(du=5\,dx\).
  4. Match: the integral only has \(dx\). Divide by 5: \(dx=\frac{du}5\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int e^u\cdot\frac{du}5=\frac15\int e^u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Exponential rule: \(\frac15e^u+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\frac15e^{5x}+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac15e^{5x}=\frac15\cdot e^{5x}\cdot5=e^{5x}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac15e^{5x}+C\)

3·P2

\(\displaystyle\int xe^{x^2}\,dx\)

Uses∫eᵘ Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: e to the power \(x^2\). The derivative of \(x^2\) is \(2x\), and there is an \(x\) outside, so substitution will work.Why? \(\frac{d}{du}e^u=e^u\): e to a power is its own derivative, so it is also its own antiderivative. Only the correction factor changes. See the rule
  2. Name the exponent: \(u=x^2\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=2x\), so \(du=2x\,dx\).
  4. Match: the integral has \(x\,dx\). Divide by 2: \(x\,dx=\frac{du}2\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int e^{x^2}\,x\,dx=\int e^u\cdot\frac{du}2=\frac12\int e^u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Exponential rule: \(\frac12e^u+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\frac12e^{x^2}+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12e^{x^2}=\frac12\cdot e^{x^2}\cdot2x=xe^{x^2}\) ✓

Answer\(\frac12e^{x^2}+C\)

3·P3

\(\displaystyle\int3^x\,dx\)

Uses∫aᵘ

  1. Spot the form: the base is 3, not e. Use \(\int a^u\,du=\frac{a^u}{\ln a}\).Why? \(\frac{d}{dx}a^x=a^x\ln a\), so going backwards you divide by \(\ln a\). When \(a=e\), \(\ln e=1\) and this turns into the e rule. See the rule
  2. Identify the pieces: \(a=3\) and \(u=x\), so \(du=dx\). That matches the integral exactly, so the correction factor is 1.
  3. Apply the rule: \(\displaystyle\int3^x\,dx=\frac{3^x}{\ln3}+C\). Note that \(\ln3\) is just a number (about 1.0986).
  4. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{3^x}{\ln3}=\frac{3^x\ln3}{\ln3}=3^x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\dfrac{3^x}{\ln3}+C\)

3·P4

\(\displaystyle\int2^{4x}\,dx\)

Uses∫aᵘ Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: base 2, so use \(\int a^u\,du=\frac{a^u}{\ln a}\) with \(a=2\).Why? \(\frac{d}{dx}a^x=a^x\ln a\), so going backwards you divide by \(\ln a\). When \(a=e\), \(\ln e=1\) and this turns into the e rule. See the rule
  2. Name the exponent: \(u=4x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=4\), so \(du=4\,dx\).
  4. Match: the integral has \(dx\). Divide by 4: \(dx=\frac{du}4\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int2^u\cdot\frac{du}4=\frac14\int2^u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Apply the rule: \(\frac14\cdot\frac{2^u}{\ln2}=\frac{2^u}{4\ln2}+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\dfrac{2^{4x}}{4\ln2}+C\).
