  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{2^{4x}}{4\ln2}=\frac{2^{4x}\cdot\ln2\cdot4}{4\ln2}=2^{4x}\) ✓ (the 4 is from the chain rule)Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Answer\(\dfrac{2^{4x}}{4\ln2}+C\)

3·P5

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{e^{\sqrt x}}{\sqrt x}\,dx\)

Uses∫eᵘ Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: e to the power \(\sqrt x\). The derivative of \(\sqrt x\) is \(\frac1{2\sqrt x}\), and there is a \(\frac1{\sqrt x}\) in the integral, so substitution will work.Why? \(\frac{d}{du}e^u=e^u\): e to a power is its own derivative, so it is also its own antiderivative. Only the correction factor changes. See the rule
  2. Rewrite the root as a power: \(\sqrt x=x^{1/2}\). This makes it easy to differentiate with the power rule.Why? A root is a fractional power: \(\sqrt[n]{a^m}=a^{m/n}\). A square root is the power ½. See the rule
  3. Name the exponent: \(u=\sqrt x=x^{1/2}\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  4. Differentiate: bring down the \(\frac12\) and subtract 1 from the power: \(\frac{du}{dx}=\frac12x^{-1/2}=\frac1{2\sqrt x}\), so \(du=\frac{dx}{2\sqrt x}\).Why? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  5. Match: the integral has \(\frac{dx}{\sqrt x}\). Multiply both sides by 2: \(\frac{dx}{\sqrt x}=2\,du\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int e^{\sqrt x}\cdot\frac{dx}{\sqrt x}=\int e^u\cdot2\,du=2\int e^u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  7. Exponential rule: \(2e^u+C\).
  8. Put x back: \(2e^{\sqrt x}+C\).
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}2e^{\sqrt x}=2\cdot e^{\sqrt x}\cdot\frac1{2\sqrt x}=\frac{e^{\sqrt x}}{\sqrt x}\) ✓

Answer\(2e^{\sqrt x}+C\)

3·P6

\(\displaystyle\int e^{2\ln x}\,dx\)

Usese/ln cancel Power formula

  1. Spot the form: e with a ln in the exponent. Don't substitute: simplify first, because e and ln cancel.Why? Before integrating, look for \(e^{\ln(\dots)}\) or \(\ln e^{(\dots)}\). They collapse to something much simpler. See the rule
  2. Use the log power rule: \(2\ln x=\ln x^2\). The 2 moves up as an exponent.Why? Log power rule: a number in front of ln can move up into the exponent, \(k\ln x=\ln x^k\). See the rule
  3. Cancel e and ln: \(e^{\ln x^2}=x^2\).Why? \(e^x\) and \(\ln x\) are inverse functions, so they undo each other: \(e^{\ln u}=u\) and \(\ln e^u=u\). See the rule
  4. Rewrite the integral: \(\displaystyle\int e^{2\ln x}\,dx=\int x^2\,dx\).
  5. Power formula: \(n=2\), so \(n+1=3\): \(\displaystyle\int x^2\,dx=\frac{x^3}3+C\).Why? Add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent: \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). See the rule
  6. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{x^3}3=x^2=e^{2\ln x}\) ✓ (for \(x>0\), where \(\ln x\) exists)Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac{x^3}3+C\)

Slide examples

3·E1

\(\displaystyle\int ye^{y^2+4}\,dy\)

Uses∫eᵘ Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: \(e\) to a power, times \(y\). The derivative of the exponent \(y^2+4\) is \(2y\), so use \(\int e^u\,du=e^u\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}e^u=e^u\): e to a power is its own derivative, so it is also its own antiderivative. Only the correction factor changes. See the rule
  2. Name the exponent: \(u=y^2+4\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dy}=2y\), so \(du=2y\,dy\).
  4. Match: the integral has \(y\,dy\), so \(y\,dy=\frac{du}2\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\int e^{y^2+4}\,y\,dy=\frac12\int e^u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(\frac12e^u+C\).
  7. Put y back: \(\frac12e^{y^2+4}+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dy}\frac12e^{y^2+4}=\frac12e^{y^2+4}\cdot2y=ye^{y^2+4}\) ✓Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Answer\(\frac12e^{y^2+4}+C\)

3·E2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{e^{\tan x}\,dx}{\cos^2x}\)

Uses∫eᵘ

  1. Spot the form: \(e\) to the power \(\tan x\). If the derivative of \(\tan x\) is also there, use \(\int e^u\,du=e^u\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}e^u=e^u\): e to a power is its own derivative, so it is also its own antiderivative. Only the correction factor changes. See the rule
  2. Rewrite: \(\frac1{\cos^2x}=\sec^2x\), so the integral is \(\int e^{\tan x}\sec^2x\,dx\).Why? \(\sec\theta\) is defined as \(\frac1{\cos\theta}\). Squaring both sides gives \(\frac1{\cos^2\theta}=\sec^2\theta\). See the rule
  3. Name the exponent: \(u=\tan x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  4. Differentiate: \(\frac{d}{dx}\tan x=\sec^2x\), so \(du=\sec^2x\,dx\). That is an exact match, so the correction factor is 1.Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\tan u=\sec^2u\), so going backwards \(\int\sec^2u\,du=\tan u\). See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\int e^u\,du\).
  6. Integrate: \(e^u+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(e^{\tan x}+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}e^{\tan x}=e^{\tan x}\sec^2x=\frac{e^{\tan x}}{\cos^2x}\) ✓Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Answer\(e^{\tan x}+C\)

3·E3

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(e^x+1)^2\,dx}{e^x}\)

UsesSum rule ∫eᵘ

  1. Spot the form: a square on top and \(e^x\) on the bottom. No single u fits, so expand and divide term by term.
  2. Expand the square: \((e^x+1)^2=(e^x)^2+2e^x+1=e^{2x}+2e^x+1\).Why? Expanding a square: \((a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2\). See the ruleWhy? A power of a power multiplies the exponents: \((a^m)^n=a^{mn}\). See the rule
  3. Split over \(e^x\): \(\dfrac{e^{2x}}{e^x}+\dfrac{2e^x}{e^x}+\dfrac1{e^x}\).Why? A sum on top can be split into separate fractions, \(\frac{a+b}c=\frac ac+\frac bc\), and the sum rule lets you integrate each piece separately. See the rule
  4. Simplify each piece: \(\frac{e^{2x}}{e^x}=e^{2x-x}=e^x\), \(\frac{2e^x}{e^x}=2\), and \(\frac1{e^x}=e^{-x}\).Why? Dividing powers with the same base subtracts the exponents: \(\frac{a^m}{a^n}=a^{m-n}\). See the ruleWhy? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  5. Rewrite the integral: \(\int\left(e^x+2+e^{-x}\right)dx\).
  6. First two terms: \(\int e^x\,dx=e^x\) and \(\int2\,dx=2x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}e^u=e^u\): e to a power is its own derivative, so it is also its own antiderivative. Only the correction factor changes. See the ruleWhy? \(\int k\,dx=kx\): a constant integrates to that constant times x, because \(\frac{d}{dx}(kx)=k\). See the rule
  7. Third term: for \(e^{-x}\), let \(u=-x\), so \(du=-dx\) and \(dx=-du\): \(\int e^{-x}dx=-\int e^u\,du=-e^{-x}\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  8. Combine: \(e^x+2x-e^{-x}+C\).Why? Sum rule: integrate each term separately, then add the results. See the rule
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}(e^x+2x-e^{-x})=e^x+2+e^{-x}\), and \(\frac{(e^x+1)^2}{e^x}=e^x+2+e^{-x}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(e^x+2x-e^{-x}+C\)

3·E4

\(\displaystyle\int5^{x+2}\,dx\)

Uses∫aᵘ

  1. Spot the form: a number (not e) raised to a power of x: use \(\int a^u\,du=\frac{a^u}{\ln a}\).Why? \(\frac{d}{dx}a^x=a^x\ln a\), so going backwards you divide by \(\ln a\). When \(a=e\), \(\ln e=1\) and this turns into the e rule. See the rule
  2. Name the exponent: \(u=x+2\). The base is \(a=5\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=1\), so \(du=dx\). Exact match, so the correction factor is 1.
  4. Substitute everything: \(\int5^u\,du\).
  5. Integrate: \(\frac{5^u}{\ln5}+C\).
  6. Put x back: \(\frac{5^{x+2}}{\ln5}+C\).
  7. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{5^{x+2}}{\ln5}=\frac{5^{x+2}\ln5}{\ln5}=5^{x+2}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\dfrac{5^{x+2}}{\ln5}+C\)

3·E5

\(\displaystyle\int4e^{3\ln x}\,dx\)

Usese/ln cancel Power formula

  1. Spot the form: \(e\) to a power that contains \(\ln x\). e and ln undo each other, so simplify before integrating.
  2. Move the 3 inside the log: \(3\ln x=\ln x^3\).Why? Log power rule: a number in front of ln can move up into the exponent, \(k\ln x=\ln x^k\). See the rule
  3. e and ln cancel: \(e^{\ln x^3}=x^3\), so the integral becomes \(4\int x^3dx\).Why? \(e^x\) and \(\ln x\) are inverse functions, so they undo each other: \(e^{\ln u}=u\) and \(\ln e^u=u\). See the rule
  4. Power formula: \(3+1=4\): \(4\cdot\frac{x^4}4=x^4\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  5. Add C: \(x^4+C\).
  6. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}x^4=4x^3=4e^{3\ln x}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(x^4+C\)

3·E6

\(\displaystyle\int10^{x^3}x^2\,dx\)

Uses∫aᵘ Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: a number (10) raised to a power of x, times \(x^2\). The exponent's derivative \(3x^2\) matches \(x^2\) up to a constant, so use \(\int a^u\,du=\frac{a^u}{\ln a}\).Why? \(\frac{d}{dx}a^x=a^x\ln a\), so going backwards you divide by \(\ln a\). When \(a=e\), \(\ln e=1\) and this turns into the e rule. See the rule
  2. Name the exponent: \(u=x^3\). The base is \(a=10\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=3x^2\), so \(du=3x^2dx\).
  4. Match: the integral has \(x^2dx\), so \(x^2dx=\frac{du}3\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\frac13\int10^u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(\frac13\cdot\frac{10^u}{\ln10}\).
  7. Put x back: \(\frac{10^{x^3}}{3\ln10}+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{10^{x^3}}{3\ln10}=\frac{10^{x^3}\ln10\cdot3x^2}{3\ln10}=10^{x^3}x^2\) ✓Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Answer\(\dfrac{10^{x^3}}{3\ln10}+C\)

3·E7

\(\displaystyle\int x^2\sqrt{7^{3x^3}}\,dx\)

Uses∫aᵘ Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: a root of \(7^{\text{something}}\), times \(x^2\). Turn the root into a power first, then use \(\int a^u\,du=\frac{a^u}{\ln a}\).Why? \(\frac{d}{dx}a^x=a^x\ln a\), so going backwards you divide by \(\ln a\). When \(a=e\), \(\ln e=1\) and this turns into the e rule. See the rule
  2. Rewrite the root: \(\sqrt{7^{3x^3}}=(7^{3x^3})^{1/2}\).Why? A root is a fractional power: \(\sqrt[n]{a^m}=a^{m/n}\). A square root is the power ½. See the rule
  3. Multiply the exponents: \((7^{3x^3})^{1/2}=7^{\frac12\cdot3x^3}=7^{\frac32x^3}\).Why? A power of a power multiplies the exponents: \((a^m)^n=a^{mn}\). See the rule
  4. Name the exponent: \(u=\frac32x^3\). The base is \(a=7\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  5. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=\frac32\cdot3x^2=\frac92x^2\), so \(du=\frac92x^2dx\).
  6. Match: the integral has \(x^2dx\). Multiply both sides by \(\frac29\): \(x^2dx=\frac29du\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  7. Substitute everything: \(\frac29\int7^u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  8. Integrate: \(\frac29\cdot\frac{7^u}{\ln7}\).
  9. Put x back: \(\frac{2\cdot7^{\frac32x^3}}{9\ln7}+C\).
  10. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{2\cdot7^{\frac32x^3}}{9\ln7}=\frac{2\cdot7^{\frac32x^3}\ln7\cdot\frac92x^2}{9\ln7}=x^2\,7^{\frac32x^3}\) ✓Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Answer\(\dfrac{2\cdot7^{\frac32x^3}}{9\ln7}+C\)

Exercises · full solutions

3·X1

\(\displaystyle\int2^{-5x}\,dx\)

Uses∫aᵘ

  1. Name the exponent: \(u=-5x\), so \(du=-5\,dx\) and \(dx=-\frac{du}5\). The base is \(a=2\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  2. Substitute: \(-\frac15\int2^u\,du\).
  3. Integrate: \(-\frac15\cdot\frac{2^u}{\ln2}=-\frac{2^{-5x}}{5\ln2}+C\).

Answer\(-\dfrac{2^{-5x}}{5\ln2}+C\)

3·X2

\(\displaystyle\int e^{2x}(1+e^{2x})^2\,dx\)

UsesPower formula u-substitution

  1. Spot the power: \((1+e^{2x})^2\) is something squared, so use the power formula with \(u=1+e^{2x}\).
  2. Differentiate: \(du=2e^{2x}dx\), so \(e^{2x}dx=\frac{du}2\).
  3. Substitute: \(\frac12\int u^2du=\frac12\cdot\frac{u^3}3=\frac{u^3}6\).
  4. Put x back: \(\frac16(1+e^{2x})^3+C\).

Answer\(\frac16(1+e^{2x})^3+C\)

3·X3

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{e^{3x}\,dx}{1+2e^{3x}}\)

UsesLog rule

  1. This is a fraction, so try the log rule with \(u\) = bottom: \(u=1+2e^{3x}\).
  2. Differentiate: \(du=6e^{3x}dx\), so \(e^{3x}dx=\frac{du}6\).
  3. Log rule: \(\frac16\int\frac{du}u=\frac16\ln(1+2e^{3x})+C\).

Answer\(\frac16\ln(1+2e^{3x})+C\)

3·X4

\(\displaystyle\int\ln e^{2x}\,dx\)

Usese/ln cancel Power formula

  1. ln and e cancel: \(\ln e^{2x}=2x\).Why? \(e^x\) and \(\ln x\) are inverse functions, so they undo each other: \(e^{\ln u}=u\) and \(\ln e^u=u\). See the rule
  2. Integrate: \(\int2x\,dx=2\cdot\frac{x^2}2=x^2+C\).

Answer\(x^2+C\)

3·X5

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{e^{2x}\,dx}{1+4e^{2x}+4e^{4x}}\)

UsesPower formula u-substitution

  1. Spot a perfect square: \(1+4e^{2x}+4e^{4x}=1+2(2e^{2x})+(2e^{2x})^2=(1+2e^{2x})^2\).Why? Perfect square trinomial: \(a^2+2ab+b^2=(a+b)^2\). Here \(a=1\) and \(b=2e^{2x}\). See the rule
  2. Name the inside: \(u=1+2e^{2x}\), so \(du=4e^{2x}dx\) and \(e^{2x}dx=\frac{du}4\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Substitute: \(\frac14\int u^{-2}du\).
  4. Power formula: \(\frac14\cdot\frac{u^{-1}}{-1}=-\frac1{4u}\).
  5. Put x back: \(-\frac1{4(1+2e^{2x})}+C\).

Answer\(-\dfrac{1}{4(1+2e^{2x})}+C\)

LESSON 4

Integration of Trigonometric Functions

Use the ten basic trig formulas. Most problems need an identity first: split the fraction, use a Pythagorean identity, or use product-to-sum.

  1. Split fractions term by term and rewrite in sec, csc, tan, cot (\(\frac1{\cos x}=\sec x\), \(\frac{\sin x}{\cos x}=\tan x\)).
  2. Look for a Pythagorean identity, a difference of squares, or a double angle that simplifies.
  3. For \(\sin mx\cos nx\) and similar products, use product-to-sum.
  4. For \(\int\sin(ax)\,dx\) and similar, the inside \(ax\) adds a factor of \(\frac1a\).

Formula practice · basic trig

4·P1

\(\displaystyle\int\sin3x\,dx\)

Uses∫sin / ∫cos

  1. Spot the form: sine of \(3x\), not plain x. Use \(\int\sin u\,du=-\cos u\), with u = the angle.Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\cos u=-\sin u\), so the antiderivative of \(\sin u\) is \(-\cos u\). Forgetting that minus is the most common slip. See the rule
  2. Name the angle: \(u=3x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=3\), so \(du=3\,dx\).
  4. Match: the integral has \(dx\). Divide by 3: \(dx=\frac{du}3\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\sin u\cdot\frac{du}3=\frac13\int\sin u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(\frac13(-\cos u)=-\frac13\cos u+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(-\frac13\cos3x+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left(-\frac13\cos3x\right)=-\frac13\cdot(-\sin3x)\cdot3=\sin3x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(-\frac13\cos3x+C\)

4·P2

\(\displaystyle\int\cos\frac x2\,dx\)

Uses∫sin / ∫cos

  1. Spot the form: cosine of \(\frac x2\). Use \(\int\cos u\,du=\sin u\), with u = the angle.Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\sin u=\cos u\), so going backwards \(\int\cos u\,du=\sin u\). No minus sign here. See the rule
  2. Name the angle: \(u=\frac x2\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=\frac12\), so \(du=\frac12\,dx\).
  4. Match: the integral has \(dx\). Multiply both sides by 2: \(dx=2\,du\). (Dividing by \(\frac12\) is the same as multiplying by 2.)Why? Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip: \(a\div\frac bc=a\cdot\frac cb\). See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\cos u\cdot2\,du=2\int\cos u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(2\sin u+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(2\sin\frac x2+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}2\sin\frac x2=2\cdot\cos\frac x2\cdot\frac12=\cos\frac x2\) ✓

Answer\(2\sin\frac x2+C\)

4·P3

\(\displaystyle\int\sec^24x\,dx\)

Uses∫sec² / ∫csc²

  1. Spot the form: \(\sec^2\) of \(4x\). Use \(\int\sec^2u\,du=\tan u\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\tan u=\sec^2u\), so going backwards \(\int\sec^2u\,du=\tan u\). See the rule
  2. Name the angle: \(u=4x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=4\), so \(du=4\,dx\).
  4. Match: divide by 4: \(dx=\frac{du}4\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\sec^2u\cdot\frac{du}4=\frac14\int\sec^2u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(\frac14\tan u+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\frac14\tan4x+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac14\tan4x=\frac14\cdot\sec^24x\cdot4=\sec^24x\) ✓

Answer\(\frac14\tan4x+C\)

4·P4

\(\displaystyle\int\csc2x\cot2x\,dx\)

Uses∫sec tan / ∫csc cot

  1. Spot the form: \(\csc\cdot\cot\) with the same angle \(2x\). Use \(\int\csc u\cot u\,du=-\csc u\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\csc u=-\csc u\cot u\), so going backwards \(\int\csc u\cot u\,du=-\csc u\). See the rule
  2. Name the angle: \(u=2x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=2\), so \(du=2\,dx\).
  4. Match: divide by 2: \(dx=\frac{du}2\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\csc u\cot u\cdot\frac{du}2=\frac12\int\csc u\cot u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(\frac12(-\csc u)=-\frac12\csc u+C\).Where did cot go? The cot is not lost or cancelled. You don't integrate \(\csc u\) and \(\cot u\) separately. The pair \(\csc u\cot u\) is one whole thing: it is exactly what you get when you differentiate \(-\csc u\).
    \(\frac{d}{du}(-\csc u)=-(-\csc u\cot u)=\csc u\cot u\).
    So the cot only "appears" when you differentiate \(\csc u\). Integrating runs that backwards, so the cot "disappears". It's like \(\int 2x\,dx=x^2\): the \(2x\) came from differentiating \(x^2\), so it goes away when you integrate. See the rule
  7. Put x back: \(-\frac12\csc2x+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left(-\frac12\csc2x\right)=-\frac12\cdot(-\csc2x\cot2x)\cdot2=\csc2x\cot2x\) ✓

Answer\(-\frac12\csc2x+C\)

4·P5

\(\displaystyle\int x\sec^2(x^2)\,dx\)

Uses∫sec² / ∫csc² u-substitution

  1. Spot the form: \(\sec^2\) of \(x^2\). The derivative of \(x^2\) is \(2x\), and there is an \(x\) outside, so substitution works. Then use \(\int\sec^2u\,du=\tan u\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\tan u=\sec^2u\), so going backwards \(\int\sec^2u\,du=\tan u\). See the rule
  2. Name the angle: \(u=x^2\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=2x\), so \(du=2x\,dx\).
  4. Match: the integral has \(x\,dx\). Divide by 2: \(x\,dx=\frac{du}2\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\sec^2(x^2)\,x\,dx=\int\sec^2u\cdot\frac{du}2=\frac12\int\sec^2u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(\frac12\tan u+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\frac12\tan(x^2)+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12\tan(x^2)=\frac12\cdot\sec^2(x^2)\cdot2x=x\sec^2(x^2)\) ✓

Answer\(\frac12\tan(x^2)+C\)

4·P6

\(\displaystyle\int\tan5x\,dx\)

Uses∫tan / ∫cot

  1. Spot the form: tangent of \(5x\). Use \(\int\tan u\,du=\ln|\sec u|\).Why? \(\tan u=\frac{\sin u}{\cos u}\). With \(w=\cos u\), \(dw=-\sin u\,du\), so it becomes \(-\int\frac{dw}w=-\ln|\cos u|\). Since \(-\ln a=\ln\frac1a\) and \(\frac1{\cos u}=\sec u\), that equals \(\ln|\sec u|\). See the rule
  2. Name the angle: \(u=5x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=5\), so \(du=5\,dx\).
  4. Match: divide by 5: \(dx=\frac{du}5\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\tan u\cdot\frac{du}5=\frac15\int\tan u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(\frac15\ln|\sec u|+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\frac15\ln|\sec5x|+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac15\ln|\sec5x|=\frac15\cdot\frac{\sec5x\tan5x\cdot5}{\sec5x}=\tan5x\) ✓ (the derivative of \(\sec u\) is \(\sec u\tan u\))

Answer\(\frac15\ln|\sec5x|+C\)

4·P7

\(\displaystyle\int\cot2x\,dx\)

Uses∫tan / ∫cot

  1. Spot the form: cotangent of \(2x\). Use \(\int\cot u\,du=\ln|\sin u|\).Why? \(\cot u=\frac{\cos u}{\sin u}\), and the top is the derivative of the bottom, so the log rule gives \(\ln|\sin u|\). See the rule
  2. Name the angle: \(u=2x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=2\), so \(du=2\,dx\).
  4. Match: divide by 2: \(dx=\frac{du}2\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\cot u\cdot\frac{du}2=\frac12\int\cot u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(\frac12\ln|\sin u|+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\frac12\ln|\sin2x|+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12\ln|\sin2x|=\frac12\cdot\frac{\cos2x\cdot2}{\sin2x}=\frac{\cos2x}{\sin2x}=\cot2x\) ✓

Answer\(\frac12\ln|\sin2x|+C\)

4·P8

\(\displaystyle\int\sec3x\,dx\)

Uses∫sec / ∫csc

  1. Spot the form: plain secant of \(3x\). Use \(\int\sec u\,du=\ln|\sec u+\tan u|\).Why? Multiply \(\sec u\) by \(\frac{\sec u+\tan u}{\sec u+\tan u}\). The top becomes \(\sec^2u+\sec u\tan u\), which is exactly the derivative of the bottom, so the log rule finishes it. See the rule
  2. Name the angle: \(u=3x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=3\), so \(du=3\,dx\).
  4. Match: divide by 3: \(dx=\frac{du}3\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\sec u\cdot\frac{du}3=\frac13\int\sec u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(\frac13\ln|\sec u+\tan u|+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\frac13\ln|\sec3x+\tan3x|+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{du}\ln|\sec u+\tan u|=\frac{\sec u\tan u+\sec^2u}{\sec u+\tan u}=\frac{\sec u(\tan u+\sec u)}{\sec u+\tan u}=\sec u\). With \(u=3x\): \(\frac13\cdot\sec3x\cdot3=\sec3x\) ✓

Answer\(\frac13\ln|\sec3x+\tan3x|+C\)

4·P9

\(\displaystyle\int\csc\frac x2\,dx\)

Uses∫sec / ∫csc

  1. Spot the form: plain cosecant of \(\frac x2\). Use \(\int\csc u\,du=\ln|\csc u-\cot u|\).Why? Multiply \(\csc u\) by \(\frac{\csc u-\cot u}{\csc u-\cot u}\). The top becomes \(\csc^2u-\csc u\cot u\), which is exactly the derivative of the bottom, so the log rule finishes it. See the rule
  2. Name the angle: \(u=\frac x2\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=\frac12\), so \(du=\frac12\,dx\).
  4. Match: multiply both sides by 2: \(dx=2\,du\).Why? Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip: \(a\div\frac bc=a\cdot\frac cb\). See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\csc u\cdot2\,du=2\int\csc u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(2\ln|\csc u-\cot u|+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(2\ln\left|\csc\frac x2-\cot\frac x2\right|+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{du}\ln|\csc u-\cot u|=\frac{-\csc u\cot u+\csc^2u}{\csc u-\cot u}=\frac{\csc u(\csc u-\cot u)}{\csc u-\cot u}=\csc u\). With \(u=\frac x2\): \(2\cdot\csc\frac x2\cdot\frac12=\csc\frac x2\) ✓

Answer\(2\ln\left|\csc\frac x2-\cot\frac x2\right|+C\)

Formula practice · identities & product-to-sum

4·P10

\(\displaystyle\int\sin^2x\,dx\)

UsesHalf-angle

  1. Why an identity?: there is no basic formula for \(\sin^2x\). A u-substitution with \(u=\sin x\) fails too, because \(du=\cos x\,dx\) is not in the integral. So lower the power first.
  2. Half-angle: \(\sin^2x=\frac12(1-\cos2x)=\frac12-\frac12\cos2x\).Why? This comes from rearranging \(\cos2\theta=1-2\sin^2\theta=2\cos^2\theta-1\). See the rule
  3. Split: \(\displaystyle\int\sin^2x\,dx=\int\frac12\,dx-\frac12\int\cos2x\,dx\).Why? Sum rule: integrate each term separately, then add the results. See the rule
  4. First term: \(\int\frac12\,dx=\frac x2\).
  5. Second term: with \(u=2x\), \(du=2\,dx\), so \(dx=\frac{du}2\): \(\int\cos2x\,dx=\frac12\int\cos u\,du=\frac12\sin2x\). Times the \(\frac12\) in front: \(\frac14\sin2x\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. Combine: \(\frac x2-\frac14\sin2x+C\).
  7. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac x2-\frac14\sin2x\right)=\frac12-\frac14\cdot2\cos2x=\frac12-\frac12\cos2x=\frac12(1-\cos2x)=\sin^2x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac x2-\frac14\sin2x+C\)

4·P11

\(\displaystyle\int\cos^23x\,dx\)

UsesHalf-angle

  1. Why an identity?: there is no basic formula for \(\cos^2\), so lower the power with the half-angle identity.
  2. Half-angle with \( heta=3x\): \(\cos^23x=\frac12(1+\cos6x)\). The angle doubles from \(3x\) to \(6x\).Why? This comes from rearranging \(\cos2\theta=1-2\sin^2\theta=2\cos^2\theta-1\). See the rule
  3. Split: \(\displaystyle\int\cos^23x\,dx=\int\frac12\,dx+\frac12\int\cos6x\,dx\).Why? Sum rule: integrate each term separately, then add the results. See the rule
  4. First term: \(\int\frac12\,dx=\frac x2\).
  5. Second term: with \(u=6x\), \(du=6\,dx\), so \(dx=\frac{du}6\): \(\int\cos6x\,dx=\frac16\sin6x\). Times the \(\frac12\) in front: \(\frac1{12}\sin6x\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. Combine: \(\frac x2+\frac1{12}\sin6x+C\).
  7. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac x2+\frac1{12}\sin6x\right)=\frac12+\frac1{12}\cdot6\cos6x=\frac12+\frac12\cos6x=\cos^23x\) ✓

Answer\(\frac x2+\frac1{12}\sin6x+C\)

4·P12

\(\displaystyle\int\tan^2x\,dx\)

UsesPythagorean identity ∫sec² / ∫csc²

  1. Why an identity?: \(\tan^2x\) has no direct formula, but \(\sec^2x\) does (\(\int\sec^2x\,dx=\tan x\)). So rewrite tan² using sec².
  2. Pythagorean identity: \(1+\tan^2x=\sec^2x\), so \(\tan^2x=\sec^2x-1\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  3. Split: \(\displaystyle\int\tan^2x\,dx=\int\sec^2x\,dx-\int1\,dx\).Why? Sum rule: integrate each term separately, then add the results. See the rule
  4. First term: \(\int\sec^2x\,dx=\tan x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\tan u=\sec^2u\), so going backwards \(\int\sec^2u\,du=\tan u\). See the rule
  5. Second term: \(\int1\,dx=x\).Why? Integrating plain \(dx\) gives the variable itself. See the rule
  6. Combine: \(\tan x-x+C\).
  7. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}(\tan x-x)=\sec^2x-1=\tan^2x\) ✓

Answer\(\tan x-x+C\)

4·P13

\(\displaystyle\int\sin x\cos x\,dx\)

UsesDouble angle ∫sin / ∫cos

  1. Spot the form: sin times cos with the same angle. The double-angle identity turns the product into one function.
  2. Double angle: \(\sin2x=2\sin x\cos x\), so \(\sin x\cos x=\frac12\sin2x\).Why? This comes from \(\sin(A+B)=\sin A\cos B+\cos A\sin B\) with \(A=B=\theta\). See the rule
  3. Pull out the constant: \(\displaystyle\int\sin x\cos x\,dx=\frac12\int\sin2x\,dx\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  4. Substitute: \(u=2x\), \(du=2\,dx\), so \(dx=\frac{du}2\): \(\frac12\cdot\frac12\int\sin u\,du=\frac14(-\cos u)\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Put x back: \(-\frac14\cos2x+C\).
  6. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left(-\frac14\cos2x\right)=-\frac14\cdot(-\sin2x)\cdot2=\frac12\sin2x=\sin x\cos x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule
  7. Another way: with \(u=\sin x\), \(du=\cos x\,dx\), you get \(\int u\,du=\frac12\sin^2x\). Both answers are right: \(\frac12\sin^2x=\frac14-\frac14\cos2x\), which differs from \(-\frac14\cos2x\) only by the constant \(\frac14\). That constant hides inside C.Why? This comes from rearranging \(\cos2\theta=1-2\sin^2\theta=2\cos^2\theta-1\). See the rule

Answer\(-\frac14\cos2x+C\)

4·P14

\(\displaystyle\int\sin5x\cos2x\,dx\)

UsesProduct-to-sum ∫sin / ∫cos

  1. Why an identity?: a product of two trig functions with different angles has no basic formula. Product-to-sum turns it into a sum you can integrate term by term.
  2. Pick the formula: sin times cos, so \(\sin A\cos B=\frac12[\sin(A+B)+\sin(A-B)]\).Why? Product-to-sum formulas come from adding or subtracting the angle-sum formulas, e.g. \(\sin(A+B)+\sin(A-B)=2\sin A\cos B\). See the rule
  3. Plug in: \(A=5x\), \(B=2x\): \(A+B=7x\) and \(A-B=3x\), so the integrand is \(\frac12[\sin7x+\sin3x]\).
  4. First term: \(u=7x\), \(dx=\frac{du}7\): \(\int\sin7x\,dx=-\frac17\cos7x\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Second term: \(u=3x\), \(dx=\frac{du}3\): \(\int\sin3x\,dx=-\frac13\cos3x\).
  6. Multiply by ½: \(\frac12\left(-\frac17\cos7x-\frac13\cos3x\right)=-\frac1{14}\cos7x-\frac16\cos3x+C\).
  7. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left(-\frac1{14}\cos7x-\frac16\cos3x\right)=\frac7{14}\sin7x+\frac36\sin3x=\frac12[\sin7x+\sin3x]=\sin5x\cos2x\) ✓

Answer\(-\frac1{14}\cos7x-\frac16\cos3x+C\)

4·P15

\(\displaystyle\int\cos4x\cos x\,dx\)

UsesProduct-to-sum ∫sin / ∫cos

  1. Why an identity?: a product of two trig functions with different angles has no basic formula. Product-to-sum turns it into a sum you can integrate term by term.
  2. Pick the formula: cos times cos, so \(\cos A\cos B=\frac12[\cos(A+B)+\cos(A-B)]\).Why? Product-to-sum formulas come from adding or subtracting the angle-sum formulas, e.g. \(\sin(A+B)+\sin(A-B)=2\sin A\cos B\). See the rule
  3. Plug in: \(A=4x\), \(B=x\): \(A+B=5x\) and \(A-B=3x\), so the integrand is \(\frac12[\cos5x+\cos3x]\).
  4. First term: \(u=5x\), \(dx=\frac{du}5\): \(\int\cos5x\,dx=\frac15\sin5x\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Second term: \(u=3x\), \(dx=\frac{du}3\): \(\int\cos3x\,dx=\frac13\sin3x\).
  6. Multiply by ½: \(\frac12\left(\frac15\sin5x+\frac13\sin3x\right)=\frac1{10}\sin5x+\frac16\sin3x+C\).
  7. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac1{10}\sin5x+\frac16\sin3x\right)=\frac5{10}\cos5x+\frac36\cos3x=\frac12[\cos5x+\cos3x]=\cos4x\cos x\) ✓

Answer\(\frac1{10}\sin5x+\frac16\sin3x+C\)

4·P16

\(\displaystyle\int\sin3x\sin x\,dx\)

UsesProduct-to-sum ∫sin / ∫cos

  1. Why an identity?: a product of two trig functions with different angles has no basic formula. Product-to-sum turns it into a sum you can integrate term by term.
  2. Pick the formula: sin times sin, so \(\sin A\sin B=\frac12[\cos(A-B)-\cos(A+B)]\). Watch the order: the minus angle comes first.Why? Product-to-sum formulas come from adding or subtracting the angle-sum formulas, e.g. \(\sin(A+B)+\sin(A-B)=2\sin A\cos B\). See the rule
  3. Plug in: \(A=3x\), \(B=x\): \(A-B=2x\) and \(A+B=4x\), so the integrand is \(\frac12[\cos2x-\cos4x]\).
  4. First term: \(u=2x\), \(dx=\frac{du}2\): \(\int\cos2x\,dx=\frac12\sin2x\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Second term: \(u=4x\), \(dx=\frac{du}4\): \(\int\cos4x\,dx=\frac14\sin4x\).
  6. Multiply by ½: \(\frac12\left(\frac12\sin2x-\frac14\sin4x\right)=\frac14\sin2x-\frac18\sin4x+C\).
  7. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac14\sin2x-\frac18\sin4x\right)=\frac24\cos2x-\frac48\cos4x=\frac12[\cos2x-\cos4x]=\sin3x\sin x\) ✓

Answer\(\frac14\sin2x-\frac18\sin4x+C\)

Slide examples

4·E1

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(1+\sin x)\,dx}{\cos x}\)

Uses∫sec / ∫csc ∫tan / ∫cot

  1. Spot the form: a fraction with \(\cos x\) alone on the bottom. Split it, and each piece becomes a basic trig function you can integrate with a formula.
  2. Split the fraction: \(\frac{1+\sin x}{\cos x}=\frac1{\cos x}+\frac{\sin x}{\cos x}\).Why? A sum on top can be split into separate fractions, \(\frac{a+b}c=\frac ac+\frac bc\), and the sum rule lets you integrate each piece separately. See the rule
  3. Rewrite in trig names: \(\frac1{\cos x}=\sec x\) and \(\frac{\sin x}{\cos x}=\tan x\). The integral is \(\int(\sec x+\tan x)\,dx\).Why? \(\sec\theta=\frac1{\cos\theta}\) and \(\csc\theta=\frac1{\sin\theta}\) by definition. See the ruleWhy? \(\tan\theta=\frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta}\). See the rule
  4. Integrate sec: \(\int\sec x\,dx=\ln|\sec x+\tan x|\).Why? Multiply \(\sec u\) by \(\frac{\sec u+\tan u}{\sec u+\tan u}\). The top becomes \(\sec^2u+\sec u\tan u\), which is exactly the derivative of the bottom, so the log rule finishes it. See the rule
  5. Integrate tan: \(\int\tan x\,dx=\ln|\sec x|\).Why? \(\tan u=\frac{\sin u}{\cos u}\). With \(w=\cos u\), \(dw=-\sin u\,du\), so it becomes \(-\int\frac{dw}w=-\ln|\cos u|\). Since \(-\ln a=\ln\frac1a\) and \(\frac1{\cos u}=\sec u\), that equals \(\ln|\sec u|\). See the rule
  6. Combine: \(\ln|\sec x+\tan x|+\ln|\sec x|+C\).Why? Sum rule: integrate each term separately, then add the results. See the rule
  7. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|\sec x+\tan x|=\sec x\) and \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|\sec x|=\frac{\sec x\tan x}{\sec x}=\tan x\). The sum is \(\sec x+\tan x=\frac{1+\sin x}{\cos x}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\ln|\sec x+\tan x|+\ln|\sec x|+C\)

4·E2

\(\displaystyle\int(1+\csc x)^2\,dx\)

UsesSum rule ∫sec / ∫csc ∫sec² / ∫csc²

  1. Spot the form: a square of a sum. There is no u to substitute, so expand it and integrate each trig term with its formula.
  2. Expand the square: \((1+\csc x)^2=1+2\csc x+\csc^2x\).Why? Expanding a square: \((a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2\). See the rule
  3. Split: \(\int dx+2\int\csc x\,dx+\int\csc^2x\,dx\).Why? Sum rule: integrate each term separately, then add the results. See the ruleWhy? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  4. Constant term: \(\int dx=x\).Why? \(\int k\,dx=kx\): a constant integrates to that constant times x, because \(\frac{d}{dx}(kx)=k\). See the rule
  5. Integrate csc: \(\int\csc x\,dx=\ln|\csc x-\cot x|\), so \(2\int\csc x\,dx=2\ln|\csc x-\cot x|\).Why? Multiply \(\csc u\) by \(\frac{\csc u-\cot u}{\csc u-\cot u}\). The top becomes \(\csc^2u-\csc u\cot u\), which is exactly the derivative of the bottom, so the log rule finishes it. See the rule
  6. Integrate csc²: \(\int\csc^2x\,dx=-\cot x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\cot u=-\csc^2u\), so going backwards \(\int\csc^2u\,du=-\cot u\). See the rule
  7. Combine: \(x+2\ln|\csc x-\cot x|-\cot x+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left[x+2\ln|\csc x-\cot x|-\cot x\right]=1+2\csc x+\csc^2x=(1+\csc x)^2\) ✓ (the middle derivative is \(\frac{-\csc x\cot x+\csc^2x}{\csc x-\cot x}=\csc x\))Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(x+2\ln|\csc x-\cot x|-\cot x+C\)

4·E3

\(\displaystyle\int e^{4x}\sin(e^{4x}+1)\,dx\)

Uses∫sin / ∫cos u-substitution

  1. Spot the form: \(\sin\) of something, times \(e^{4x}\). The derivative of the inside \(e^{4x}+1\) is \(4e^{4x}\), so use \(\int\sin u\,du=-\cos u\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\cos u=-\sin u\), so the antiderivative of \(\sin u\) is \(-\cos u\). Forgetting that minus is the most common slip. See the rule
  2. Name the inside of sin: \(u=e^{4x}+1\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Differentiate: chain rule on \(e^{4x}\): \(\frac{du}{dx}=4e^{4x}\), so \(du=4e^{4x}dx\).Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule
  4. Match: the integral has \(e^{4x}dx\), so \(e^{4x}dx=\frac{du}4\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\frac14\int\sin u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(\frac14(-\cos u)=-\frac14\cos u+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(-\frac14\cos(e^{4x}+1)+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left[-\frac14\cos(e^{4x}+1)\right]=-\frac14\cdot(-\sin(e^{4x}+1))\cdot4e^{4x}=e^{4x}\sin(e^{4x}+1)\) ✓Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Answer\(-\frac14\cos(e^{4x}+1)+C\)

4·E4

\(\displaystyle\int xe^{\ln\cot x^2}\,dx\)

Usese/ln cancel ∫tan / ∫cot

  1. Spot the form: e to a power that contains ln. e and ln cancel, which leaves a plain trig integral.
  2. e and ln cancel: \(e^{\ln\cot x^2}=\cot x^2\). The integral becomes \(\int x\cot x^2\,dx\). (Here \(\cot x^2\) means \(\cot(x^2)\).)Why? \(e^x\) and \(\ln x\) are inverse functions, so they undo each other: \(e^{\ln u}=u\) and \(\ln e^u=u\). See the rule
  3. Name the angle: \(u=x^2\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  4. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=2x\), so \(du=2x\,dx\).
  5. Match: the integral has \(x\,dx\), so \(x\,dx=\frac{du}2\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(\frac12\int\cot u\,du\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  7. Integrate: \(\int\cot u\,du=\ln|\sin u|\), so \(\frac12\ln|\sin u|+C\).Why? \(\cot u=\frac{\cos u}{\sin u}\), and the top is the derivative of the bottom, so the log rule gives \(\ln|\sin u|\). See the rule
  8. Put x back: \(\frac12\ln|\sin x^2|+C\).
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12\ln|\sin x^2|=\frac12\cdot\frac{\cos x^2\cdot2x}{\sin x^2}=x\cot x^2\) ✓Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the rule

Answer\(\frac12\ln|\sin x^2|+C\)

4·E5

\(\displaystyle\int(\cos^42x-\sin^42x)\,dx\)

UsesPythagorean identity Double angle ∫sin / ∫cos

  1. Spot the form: 4th powers of cos and sin, subtracted. Powers that high are hard to integrate directly, so factor first.
  2. Difference of squares: \(a^4-b^4=(a^2)^2-(b^2)^2=(a^2+b^2)(a^2-b^2)\), so \(\cos^42x-\sin^42x=(\cos^22x+\sin^22x)(\cos^22x-\sin^22x)\).Why? Difference of squares: \(a^2-b^2=(a+b)(a-b)\). Multiply out the right side to check. See the rule
  3. Pythagorean identity: the first factor is \(\cos^22x+\sin^22x=1\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  4. Double angle: \(\cos^2\theta-\sin^2\theta=\cos2\theta\). With \(\theta=2x\), the second factor is \(\cos4x\).Why? This comes from \(\cos(A+B)=\cos A\cos B-\sin A\sin B\) with \(A=B=\theta\): \(\cos2\theta=\cos^2\theta-\sin^2\theta\). See the rule
  5. Rewrite the integral: \(\int1\cdot\cos4x\,dx=\int\cos4x\,dx\).
  6. Name the angle: \(u=4x\), so \(du=4\,dx\) and \(dx=\frac{du}4\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the ruleWhy? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  7. Integrate: \(\frac14\int\cos u\,du=\frac14\sin u\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\sin u=\cos u\), so going backwards \(\int\cos u\,du=\sin u\). No minus sign here. See the rule
  8. Put x back: \(\frac14\sin4x+C\).
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac14\sin4x=\cos4x=\cos^42x-\sin^42x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac14\sin4x+C\)

4·E6

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(\cos x-2\sin x)^2}{\cos x}\,dx\)

UsesPythagorean identity ∫sin / ∫cos ∫sec / ∫csc

  1. Spot the form: a squared top over \(\cos x\). Expand, divide term by term, and turn each piece into a trig function with a known integral.
  2. Expand the top: \((\cos x-2\sin x)^2=\cos^2x-4\sin x\cos x+4\sin^2x\).Why? Expanding a square: \((a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2\). See the rule
  3. Divide each term by \(\cos x\): \(\frac{\cos^2x}{\cos x}-\frac{4\sin x\cos x}{\cos x}+\frac{4\sin^2x}{\cos x}=\cos x-4\sin x+\frac{4\sin^2x}{\cos x}\).Why? A sum on top can be split into separate fractions, \(\frac{a+b}c=\frac ac+\frac bc\), and the sum rule lets you integrate each piece separately. See the rule
  4. Fix the last term: use \(\sin^2x=1-\cos^2x\): \(\frac{4(1-\cos^2x)}{\cos x}=\frac4{\cos x}-\frac{4\cos^2x}{\cos x}=4\sec x-4\cos x\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the ruleWhy? \(\sec\theta=\frac1{\cos\theta}\) and \(\csc\theta=\frac1{\sin\theta}\) by definition. See the rule
  5. Collect like terms: \(\cos x-4\cos x-4\sin x+4\sec x=-3\cos x-4\sin x+4\sec x\).
  6. Integrate cos: \(\int-3\cos x\,dx=-3\sin x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\sin u=\cos u\), so going backwards \(\int\cos u\,du=\sin u\). No minus sign here. See the rule
  7. Integrate sin: \(\int-4\sin x\,dx=-4(-\cos x)=4\cos x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\cos u=-\sin u\), so the antiderivative of \(\sin u\) is \(-\cos u\). Forgetting that minus is the most common slip. See the rule
  8. Integrate sec: \(\int4\sec x\,dx=4\ln|\sec x+\tan x|\).Why? Multiply \(\sec u\) by \(\frac{\sec u+\tan u}{\sec u+\tan u}\). The top becomes \(\sec^2u+\sec u\tan u\), which is exactly the derivative of the bottom, so the log rule finishes it. See the rule
  9. Combine: \(-3\sin x+4\cos x+4\ln|\sec x+\tan x|+C\).Why? Sum rule: integrate each term separately, then add the results. See the rule
  10. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}[\dots]=-3\cos x-4\sin x+4\sec x\), which is what you had after collecting terms ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(-3\sin x+4\cos x+4\ln|\sec x+\tan x|+C\)

4·E7

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{\cos^23x\,dx}{1-\sin3x}\)

UsesPythagorean identity ∫sin / ∫cos

  1. Spot the form: \(\cos^2\) on top and \(1-\sin\) on the bottom. Turn the top into sines so that it factors and cancels with the bottom.
  2. Pythagorean identity: \(\cos^23x=1-\sin^23x\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  3. Factor: as a difference of squares: \(1-\sin^23x=(1+\sin3x)(1-\sin3x)\).Why? Difference of squares: \(a^2-b^2=(a+b)(a-b)\). Multiply out the right side to check. See the rule
  4. Cancel: the \((1-\sin3x)\) cancels with the bottom, leaving \(\int(1+\sin3x)\,dx\).
  5. Constant term: \(\int dx=x\).Why? \(\int k\,dx=kx\): a constant integrates to that constant times x, because \(\frac{d}{dx}(kx)=k\). See the rule
  6. Integrate sin 3x: \(u=3x\), \(du=3\,dx\), so \(\int\sin3x\,dx=\frac13\int\sin u\,du=-\frac13\cos3x\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the ruleWhy? \(\frac{d}{du}\cos u=-\sin u\), so the antiderivative of \(\sin u\) is \(-\cos u\). Forgetting that minus is the most common slip. See the rule
  7. Combine: \(x-\frac13\cos3x+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left(x-\frac13\cos3x\right)=1+\sin3x\), and \(\frac{\cos^23x}{1-\sin3x}=1+\sin3x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(x-\frac13\cos3x+C\)

4·E8

\(\displaystyle\int\cos5x\cos3x\,dx\)

UsesProduct-to-sum ∫sin / ∫cos

  1. Spot the form: a product of two cosines with different angles. There is no u for this, so turn the product into a sum.
  2. Pick the formula: \(\cos A\cos B=\frac12[\cos(A+B)+\cos(A-B)]\).Why? Product-to-sum formulas come from adding or subtracting the angle-sum formulas, e.g. \(\sin(A+B)+\sin(A-B)=2\sin A\cos B\). See the rule
  3. Plug in: \(A=5x\), \(B=3x\): \(A+B=8x\) and \(A-B=2x\), so \(\cos5x\cos3x=\frac12[\cos8x+\cos2x]\).
  4. Pull out the ½: \(\frac12\left[\int\cos8x\,dx+\int\cos2x\,dx\right]\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the ruleWhy? Sum rule: integrate each term separately, then add the results. See the rule
  5. Integrate cos 8x: \(u=8x\), \(dx=\frac{du}8\): \(\int\cos8x\,dx=\frac18\sin8x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\sin u=\cos u\), so going backwards \(\int\cos u\,du=\sin u\). No minus sign here. See the rule
  6. Integrate cos 2x: \(u=2x\), \(dx=\frac{du}2\): \(\int\cos2x\,dx=\frac12\sin2x\).
  7. Multiply by ½: \(\frac12\left[\frac18\sin8x+\frac12\sin2x\right]=\frac1{16}\sin8x+\frac14\sin2x+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left[\frac1{16}\sin8x+\frac14\sin2x\right]=\frac12\cos8x+\frac12\cos2x=\cos5x\cos3x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac1{16}\sin8x+\frac14\sin2x+C\)

4·E9

\(\displaystyle\int\sin4x\cos3x\,dx\)

UsesProduct-to-sum ∫sin / ∫cos

  1. Spot the form: a product of sin and cos with different angles. Turn the product into a sum.
  2. Pick the formula: \(\sin A\cos B=\frac12[\sin(A+B)+\sin(A-B)]\).Why? Product-to-sum formulas come from adding or subtracting the angle-sum formulas, e.g. \(\sin(A+B)+\sin(A-B)=2\sin A\cos B\). See the rule
  3. Plug in: \(A=4x\), \(B=3x\): \(A+B=7x\) and \(A-B=x\), so \(\sin4x\cos3x=\frac12[\sin7x+\sin x]\). Put the bigger angle as A, so that \(A-B\) is positive.
  4. Pull out the ½: \(\frac12\left[\int\sin7x\,dx+\int\sin x\,dx\right]\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the ruleWhy? Sum rule: integrate each term separately, then add the results. See the rule
  5. Integrate sin 7x: \(u=7x\), \(dx=\frac{du}7\): \(\int\sin7x\,dx=-\frac17\cos7x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\cos u=-\sin u\), so the antiderivative of \(\sin u\) is \(-\cos u\). Forgetting that minus is the most common slip. See the rule
  6. Integrate sin x: \(\int\sin x\,dx=-\cos x\).
  7. Multiply by ½: \(\frac12\left[-\frac17\cos7x-\cos x\right]=-\frac1{14}\cos7x-\frac12\cos x+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left[-\frac1{14}\cos7x-\frac12\cos x\right]=\frac12\sin7x+\frac12\sin x=\sin4x\cos3x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(-\frac1{14}\cos7x-\frac12\cos x+C\)

Exercises · full solutions

4·X1

\(\displaystyle\int\sin2x\tan2x\,dx\)

UsesPythagorean identity ∫sec / ∫csc ∫sin / ∫cos

  1. Rewrite tan: \(\sin2x\tan2x=\sin2x\cdot\frac{\sin2x}{\cos2x}=\frac{\sin^22x}{\cos2x}\).Why? \(\tan\theta\) is defined as \(\frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta}\): height over width on the unit circle. See the rule
  2. Pythagorean: \(\sin^22x=1-\cos^22x\), so \(\frac{1-\cos^22x}{\cos2x}=\frac1{\cos2x}-\cos2x=\sec2x-\cos2x\).Why? \(\sec\theta\) is defined as \(\frac1{\cos\theta}\). Squaring both sides gives \(\frac1{\cos^2\theta}=\sec^2\theta\). See the ruleWhy? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  3. Integrate each (angle 2x gives \(\frac12\)): \(\frac12\ln|\sec2x+\tan2x|-\frac12\sin2x+C\).

Answer\(\frac12\ln|\sec2x+\tan2x|-\frac12\sin2x+C\)

4·X2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(\cos5x-\sin5x)\,dx}{\cos^25x}\)

Uses∫sec / ∫csc ∫sec tan / ∫csc cot

  1. Split the fraction: \(\frac{\cos5x}{\cos^25x}-\frac{\sin5x}{\cos^25x}\).Why? \(\tan\theta\) is defined as \(\frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta}\): height over width on the unit circle. See the ruleWhy? A sum on top can be split into separate fractions, \(\frac{a+b}c=\frac ac+\frac bc\), and the sum rule lets you integrate each piece separately. See the rule
  2. Simplify: \(\frac{\cos5x}{\cos^25x}=\sec5x\), and \(\frac{\sin5x}{\cos^25x}=\frac1{\cos5x}\cdot\frac{\sin5x}{\cos5x}=\sec5x\tan5x\).Why? \(\sec\theta\) is defined as \(\frac1{\cos\theta}\). Squaring both sides gives \(\frac1{\cos^2\theta}=\sec^2\theta\). See the ruleWhy? \(\tan\theta\) is defined as \(\frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta}\): height over width on the unit circle. See the rule
  3. Integrate each (angle 5x gives \(\frac15\)): \(\frac15\ln|\sec5x+\tan5x|-\frac15\sec5x+C\).

Answer\(\frac15\ln|\sec5x+\tan5x|-\frac15\sec5x+C\)

4·X3

\(\displaystyle\int\sqrt{\cos^22x+2\sin^22x-1}\,dx\)

UsesPythagorean identity ∫sin / ∫cos

  1. Regroup inside the root: \(\cos^22x+2\sin^22x-1=(\cos^22x+\sin^22x)+\sin^22x-1\).
  2. Pythagorean: the bracket is 1, so it's \(1+\sin^22x-1=\sin^22x\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  3. Take the root: \(\sqrt{\sin^22x}=|\sin2x|\). Where \(\sin2x\ge0\), this is just \(\sin2x\).Why? \(\sqrt{a^2}=|a|\): the square root of a square is the absolute value. See the rule
  4. Integrate: \(\int\sin2x\,dx=-\frac12\cos2x+C\).

Answer\(-\frac12\cos2x+C\) (where \(\sin2x\ge0\))

4·X4

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(1-\sin^43x)\,dx}{\cos^43x}\)

UsesPythagorean identity ∫sec² / ∫csc²

  1. Difference of squares on top: \(1-\sin^43x=(1-\sin^23x)(1+\sin^23x)\).Why? \(a^2-b^2=(a+b)(a-b)\). Multiply out the right side to check. See the rule
  2. Pythagorean: \(1-\sin^23x=\cos^23x\). So the top is \(\cos^23x(1+\sin^23x)\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  3. Cancel \(\cos^23x\) with the bottom: \(\frac{1+\sin^23x}{\cos^23x}=\sec^23x+\tan^23x\).
  4. Replace tan²: \(\tan^23x=\sec^23x-1\), giving \(2\sec^23x-1\).
  5. Integrate: \(2\cdot\frac13\tan3x-x=\frac23\tan3x-x+C\).

Answer\(\frac23\tan3x-x+C\)

4·X5

\(\displaystyle\int\sin2x\sin4x\,dx\)

UsesProduct-to-sum ∫sin / ∫cos

  1. Pick the formula: \(\sin A\sin B=\frac12[\cos(A-B)-\cos(A+B)]\), with \(A=4x\), \(B=2x\).Why? Product-to-sum formulas come from adding or subtracting the angle-sum formulas, e.g. \(\sin(A+B)+\sin(A-B)=2\sin A\cos B\). See the rule
  2. Plug in: \(\frac12[\cos2x-\cos6x]\).
  3. Integrate each: \(\frac12\sin2x\) and \(\frac16\sin6x\).
  4. Multiply by ½: \(\frac14\sin2x-\frac1{12}\sin6x+C\).

Answer\(\frac14\sin2x-\frac1{12}\sin6x+C\)

4·X6

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{e^x\sin^2(e^{-x})}\)

Uses∫sec² / ∫csc² u-substitution

  1. Rewrite: \(\frac1{e^x}=e^{-x}\) and \(\frac1{\sin^2}=\csc^2\), so the integral is \(\int e^{-x}\csc^2(e^{-x})\,dx\).Why? \(\csc\theta\) is defined as \(\frac1{\sin\theta}\). See the ruleWhy? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  2. Name the angle: \(u=e^{-x}\), so \(du=-e^{-x}dx\) and \(e^{-x}dx=-du\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  3. Substitute: \(-\int\csc^2u\,du\).
  4. Integrate (\(\int\csc^2u=-\cot u\)): \(-(-\cot u)=\cot(e^{-x})+C\).

Answer\(\cot(e^{-x})+C\)

LESSON 5

Integrals Leading to Inverse Trig Functions

Match the integral to one of the three inverse trig forms. Identify \(a^2\) (a constant) and \(u^2\) (the variable part), then check that \(du\) is present.

  1. Find \(a^2\) and \(u^2\). Take square roots to get \(a\) and \(u\), then compute \(du\) and fix the constant.
  2. For a quadratic \(ax^2+bx+c\), complete the square first.
  3. If the numerator has an \(x\) term, split it into (a multiple of the derivative of the quadratic) + (a constant). The first part gives \(\ln\), the second gives \(\arctan\) or \(\arcsin\).

Formula practice · inverse trig forms

5·P1

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{9-x^2}}\)

UsesArcsin form

  1. Match the form: \(\sqrt{\text{constant}-\text{variable}^2}\) on the bottom means arcsin: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2-u^2}}=\arcsin\frac ua\).Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  2. Find a: \(a^2=9\), so \(a=3\).
  3. Find u and du: \(u^2=x^2\), so \(u=x\) and \(du=dx\). That matches the \(dx\) on top, so the correction factor is 1.
  4. Apply the formula: \(\arcsin\frac ua=\arcsin\frac x3+C\). Arcsin has no number in front.
  5. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\arcsin\frac x3=\frac1{\sqrt{1-\frac{x^2}9}}\cdot\frac13=\frac1{3\cdot\frac{\sqrt{9-x^2}}3}=\frac1{\sqrt{9-x^2}}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\arcsin\frac x3+C\)

5·P2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{16+x^2}\)

UsesArctan form

  1. Match the form: constant + variable² on the bottom, with no root, means arctan: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{du}{a^2+u^2}=\frac1a\arctan\frac ua\).Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  2. Find a: \(a^2=16\), so \(a=4\).
  3. Find u and du: \(u=x\), \(du=dx\). The correction factor is 1.
  4. Apply the formula: \(\frac1a\arctan\frac ua=\frac14\arctan\frac x4+C\). Unlike arcsin, arctan does have \(\frac1a\) in front.
  5. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac14\arctan\frac x4=\frac14\cdot\frac1{1+\frac{x^2}{16}}\cdot\frac14=\frac1{16+x^2}\) ✓

Answer\(\frac14\arctan\frac x4+C\)

5·P3

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x\sqrt{x^2-25}}\)

UsesArcsec form

  1. Match the form: u outside times \(\sqrt{u^2-a^2}\) (variable² first, then minus) means arcsec: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{du}{u\sqrt{u^2-a^2}}=\frac1a\operatorname{arcsec}\frac{|u|}a\).Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  2. Find a: \(a^2=25\), so \(a=5\).
  3. Find u and du: \(u=x\), \(du=dx\). The \(x\) outside the root is the u the formula needs.
  4. Apply the formula: \(\frac15\operatorname{arcsec}\frac{|x|}5+C\).
  5. Check: for \(x>5\): \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac15\operatorname{arcsec}\frac x5=\frac15\cdot\frac1{\frac x5\sqrt{\frac{x^2}{25}-1}}\cdot\frac15=\frac15\cdot\frac{25}{x\sqrt{x^2-25}}\cdot\frac15=\frac1{x\sqrt{x^2-25}}\) ✓

Answer\(\frac15\operatorname{arcsec}\frac{|x|}5+C\)

5·P4

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1-4x^2}}\)

UsesArcsin form Correction factor

  1. Match the form: \(\sqrt{\text{constant}-\text{variable}^2}\): arcsin.Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  2. Find a: \(a^2=1\), so \(a=1\).
  3. Find u: \(u^2=4x^2=(2x)^2\), so \(u=2x\).Why? \((ab)^2=a^2b^2\), so \(4x^2=2^2x^2=(2x)^2\). Spotting the square tells you what u is. See the rule
  4. Differentiate: \(du=2\,dx\).
  5. Match: the integral has \(dx\). Divide by 2: \(dx=\frac{du}2\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1-4x^2}}=\frac12\int\frac{du}{\sqrt{1-u^2}}\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  7. Apply the formula: \(\frac12\arcsin\frac u1=\frac12\arcsin2x+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12\arcsin2x=\frac12\cdot\frac1{\sqrt{1-4x^2}}\cdot2=\frac1{\sqrt{1-4x^2}}\) ✓

Answer\(\frac12\arcsin2x+C\)

5·P5

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x^2+4x+13}\)

UsesCompleting the square Arctan form

  1. Spot the form: a quadratic on the bottom and only \(dx\) on top. Completing the square will turn it into \(u^2+a^2\), the arctan form.
  2. Complete the square: half of 4 is 2, and \(2^2=4\). Split 13 into \(4+9\): \(x^2+4x+13=(x^2+4x+4)+9=(x+2)^2+9\).Why? Take half the x-coefficient and square it, then add and subtract it: \(x^2+bx=\left(x+\frac b2\right)^2-\frac{b^2}4\). See the rule
  3. Match the form: variable² + constant, no root: arctan.Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  4. Find u, du and a: \(u=x+2\), \(du=dx\) (the correction factor is 1). \(a^2=9\), so \(a=3\).
  5. Apply the formula: \(\frac1a\arctan\frac ua=\frac13\arctan\frac{x+2}3+C\).
  6. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac13\arctan\frac{x+2}3=\frac13\cdot\frac1{1+\frac{(x+2)^2}9}\cdot\frac13=\frac1{9+(x+2)^2}=\frac1{x^2+4x+13}\) ✓

Answer\(\frac13\arctan\frac{x+2}3+C\)

5·P6

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{e^x\,dx}{1+e^{2x}}\)

UsesArctan form

  1. Spot the square: \(e^{2x}=(e^x)^2\).Why? Exponent law: \(e^{2x}=e^{x+x}=e^x\cdot e^x=(e^x)^2\). See the rule
  2. Match the form: the bottom is \(1+(e^x)^2\): constant + variable², no root, so arctan with \(a=1\).Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  3. Name u: \(u=e^x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  4. Differentiate: \(du=e^x\,dx\), which is exactly the top. The correction factor is 1.
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{e^x\,dx}{1+(e^x)^2}=\int\frac{du}{1+u^2}\).
  6. Apply the formula: with \(a=1\): \(\frac11\arctan\frac u1=\arctan u+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\arctan(e^x)+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\arctan(e^x)=\frac1{1+(e^x)^2}\cdot e^x=\frac{e^x}{1+e^{2x}}\) ✓

Answer\(\arctan(e^x)+C\)

Formula practice · log / inverse-hyperbolic forms

5·P7

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{x^2+9}}\)

Usesln(u+√(u²±a²)) form

  1. Look at the order: under the root it is variable² + constant (\(u^2+a^2\)). Arcsin needs constant − variable², so it is not arcsin. Use \(\displaystyle\int\frac{du}{\sqrt{u^2+a^2}}=\ln\left(u+\sqrt{u^2+a^2}\right)\).Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  2. Find u, du and a: \(u=x\), \(du=dx\). \(a^2=9\), so \(a=3\).
  3. Apply the formula: \(\ln\left(x+\sqrt{x^2+9}\right)+C\). No bars needed: \(\sqrt{x^2+9}>|x|\), so the inside is always positive.
  4. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln\left(x+\sqrt{x^2+9}\right)=\frac{1+\frac x{\sqrt{x^2+9}}}{x+\sqrt{x^2+9}}=\frac{\frac{\sqrt{x^2+9}+x}{\sqrt{x^2+9}}}{x+\sqrt{x^2+9}}=\frac1{\sqrt{x^2+9}}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\ln\left(x+\sqrt{x^2+9}\right)+C\)

5·P8

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{x^2-4}}\)

Usesln(u+√(u²±a²)) form

  1. Look at the order: \(u^2-a^2\) under the root, and no u outside the root, so it is not arcsec. Use \(\displaystyle\int\frac{du}{\sqrt{u^2-a^2}}=\ln\left(u+\sqrt{u^2-a^2}\right)\).Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  2. Find u, du and a: \(u=x\), \(du=dx\). \(a^2=4\), so \(a=2\).
  3. Apply the formula: \(\ln\left(x+\sqrt{x^2-4}\right)+C\). (For \(x<-2\) the inside is negative, so the fully general answer is \(\ln\left|x+\sqrt{x^2-4}\right|\).)
  4. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln\left(x+\sqrt{x^2-4}\right)=\frac{1+\frac x{\sqrt{x^2-4}}}{x+\sqrt{x^2-4}}=\frac{\frac{\sqrt{x^2-4}+x}{\sqrt{x^2-4}}}{x+\sqrt{x^2-4}}=\frac1{\sqrt{x^2-4}}\) ✓

Answer\(\ln\left(x+\sqrt{x^2-4}\right)+C\)

5·P9

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{9-x^2}\)

Uses1/(a²−u²) form

  1. Look at the sign: \(a^2-u^2\) with no root. It is a minus, so it is not arctan. Use \(\displaystyle\int\frac{du}{a^2-u^2}=\frac1{2a}\ln\left|\frac{a+u}{a-u}\right|\).Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  2. Find u, du and a: \(u=x\), \(du=dx\). \(a^2=9\), so \(a=3\).
  3. Apply the formula: \(\frac1{2a}=\frac1{2\cdot3}=\frac16\): \(\frac16\ln\left|\frac{3+x}{3-x}\right|+C\).
  4. Check: split the log first: \(\frac16\left[\ln|3+x|-\ln|3-x|\right]\). Its derivative is \(\frac16\left[\frac1{3+x}+\frac1{3-x}\right]=\frac16\cdot\frac{(3-x)+(3+x)}{(3+x)(3-x)}=\frac16\cdot\frac6{9-x^2}=\frac1{9-x^2}\) ✓ (the derivative of \(\ln|3-x|\) is \(\frac{-1}{3-x}\), so subtracting it gives a plus)Why? Log quotient rule: \(\ln\frac ab=\ln a-\ln b\). Splitting the log first makes it easier to differentiate. See the rule

Answer\(\frac16\ln\left|\frac{3+x}{3-x}\right|+C\)

5·P10

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x\sqrt{4+x^2}}\)

Uses1/(u√(a²±u²)) form

  1. Look at the order: u outside times \(\sqrt{a^2+u^2}\). The constant comes first and it is a plus, while arcsec needs \(\sqrt{u^2-a^2}\). Use \(\displaystyle\int\frac{du}{u\sqrt{a^2\pm u^2}}=-\frac1a\ln\frac{a+\sqrt{a^2\pm u^2}}{|u|}\).Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  2. Find u, du and a: \(u=x\), \(du=dx\). \(a^2=4\), so \(a=2\).
  3. Apply the formula: \(-\frac12\ln\dfrac{2+\sqrt{4+x^2}}{|x|}+C\).
  4. Check: let \(r=\sqrt{4+x^2}\), so \(r'=\frac xr\). Split the log: \(-\frac12\left[\ln(2+r)-\ln|x|\right]\). Its derivative is \(-\frac12\left[\frac{x}{r(2+r)}-\frac1x\right]=-\frac12\cdot\frac{x^2-r(2+r)}{xr(2+r)}\). Since \(r^2=4+x^2\), the top is \(x^2-2r-4-x^2=-2(r+2)\). So it equals \(-\frac12\cdot\frac{-2(r+2)}{xr(r+2)}=\frac1{xr}=\frac1{x\sqrt{4+x^2}}\) ✓Why? Log quotient rule: \(\ln\frac ab=\ln a-\ln b\). Splitting the log first makes it easier to differentiate. See the rule

Answer\(-\frac12\ln\dfrac{2+\sqrt{4+x^2}}{|x|}+C\)

Slide examples

5·E1

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{x\,dx}{\sqrt{25-9x^4}}\)

UsesArcsin form Correction factor

  1. Spot the form: \(\sqrt{\text{constant}-\text{variable}^2}\) on the bottom means arcsin: \(\int\frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2-u^2}}=\arcsin\frac ua\).Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  2. Read off a: \(a^2=25\), so \(a=5\).
  3. Read off u: \(u^2=9x^4\), so \(u=3x^2\).Why? To find u from \(u^2\), take the square root of each factor: \(\sqrt{9x^4}=\sqrt9\cdot\sqrt{x^4}=3x^2\). See the rule
  4. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=6x\), so \(du=6x\,dx\).
  5. Match: the top is \(x\,dx\), so \(x\,dx=\frac{du}6\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{x\,dx}{\sqrt{25-9x^4}}=\frac16\int\frac{du}{\sqrt{5^2-u^2}}\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  7. Apply arcsin: \(\frac16\arcsin\frac u5+C\).Why? \(\int\frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2-u^2}}=\arcsin\frac ua\). It comes from \((\arcsin x)'=\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\). Unlike arctan, there is no \(\frac1a\) in front. See the rule
  8. Put x back: \(\frac16\arcsin\frac{3x^2}5+C\).
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac16\arcsin\frac{3x^2}5=\frac16\cdot\frac{6x/5}{\sqrt{1-\frac{9x^4}{25}}}=\frac{x/5}{\frac15\sqrt{25-9x^4}}=\frac x{\sqrt{25-9x^4}}\) ✓Why? You need these derivatives for du: \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\) and \((\arcsin x)'=\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\). See the rule

Answer\(\frac16\arcsin\frac{3x^2}5+C\)

5·E2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x^2+10x+26}\)

UsesCompleting the square Arctan form

  1. Spot the form: 1 over a quadratic that does not factor. Complete the square to get \(u^2+a^2\), which is the arctan form.Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  2. Complete the square: half of 10 is 5, and \(5^2=25\): \(x^2+10x+26=(x^2+10x+25)+1=(x+5)^2+1\).Why? Take half the x-coefficient and square it, then add and subtract it: \(x^2+bx=\left(x+\frac b2\right)^2-\frac{b^2}4\). See the rule
  3. Read off u and a: \(u=x+5\) and \(a^2=1\), so \(a=1\).
  4. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=1\), so \(du=dx\). Exact match, so the correction factor is 1.
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{du}{u^2+1}\).
  6. Apply arctan: \(\frac11\arctan\frac u1=\arctan u+C\).Why? \(\int\frac{du}{a^2+u^2}=\frac1a\arctan\frac ua\). It comes from \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\). Factoring \(a^2\) out of the bottom is what puts the \(\frac1a\) in front. See the rule
  7. Put x back: \(\arctan(x+5)+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\arctan(x+5)=\frac1{1+(x+5)^2}=\frac1{x^2+10x+26}\) ✓Why? You need these derivatives for du: \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\) and \((\arcsin x)'=\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\). See the rule

Answer\(\arctan(x+5)+C\)

5·E3

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{\tan x\sqrt{\sin^2x-16}}\)

UsesArcsec form

  1. Spot the form: a square root of (variable² − constant) on the bottom, with an extra factor beside it. That is the arcsec shape \(\frac{du}{u\sqrt{u^2-a^2}}\).Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  2. Rewrite: \(\frac1{\tan x}=\frac{\cos x}{\sin x}\), so the integral is \(\displaystyle\int\frac{\cos x\,dx}{\sin x\sqrt{\sin^2x-16}}\).Why? cos and sec are reciprocals of each other, so \(\cos x=\frac1{\sec x}\). Likewise \(\frac1{\tan x}=\cot x=\frac{\cos x}{\sin x}\). See the rule
  3. Name u: \(u=\sin x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  4. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dx}=\cos x\), so \(du=\cos x\,dx\). This is exactly the top.
  5. Read off a: \(a^2=16\), so \(a=4\).
  6. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{du}{u\sqrt{u^2-4^2}}\).
  7. Apply arcsec: \(\frac14\operatorname{arcsec}\frac{|u|}4+C\).Why? \(\int\frac{du}{u\sqrt{u^2-a^2}}=\frac1a\operatorname{arcsec}\frac{|u|}a\). It comes from \((\operatorname{arcsec}x)'=\frac1{|x|\sqrt{x^2-1}}\). See the rule
  8. Put x back: \(\frac14\operatorname{arcsec}\frac{|\sin x|}4+C\).
  9. Check (formal): differentiating gives \(\frac14\cdot\frac{\cos x/4}{\frac{|\sin x|}4\sqrt{\frac{\sin^2x}{16}-1}}=\frac{\cos x}{|\sin x|\sqrt{\sin^2x-16}}\), which matches the integrand up to the sign of \(\sin x\) ✓ (see the note below)

Note: \(\sin^2x\le1\), so \(\sin^2x-16\) is always negative and this integral has no real values. Use it only to practice recognizing the arcsec form.

Answer\(\frac14\operatorname{arcsec}\left|\frac{\sin x}4\right|+C\) (formal)

5·E4

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dy}{\sqrt{15+4y-4y^2}}\)

UsesCompleting the square Arcsin form

  1. Spot the form: 1 over the square root of a quadratic with a negative \(y^2\) term. Complete the square to get \(\sqrt{a^2-u^2}\), which is the arcsin form.Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  2. Factor out the minus: \(15+4y-4y^2=15-(4y^2-4y)\).
  3. Complete the square inside: \(4y^2-4y=(2y)^2-2(2y)(1)\). Add 1 to make \((2y-1)^2\): \(4y^2-4y=(2y-1)^2-1\).Why? Take half the x-coefficient and square it, then add and subtract it: \(x^2+bx=\left(x+\frac b2\right)^2-\frac{b^2}4\). See the rule
  4. Put it back: \(15-[(2y-1)^2-1]=16-(2y-1)^2\). Since \((2y-1)^2=(1-2y)^2\), the root is \(\sqrt{16-(1-2y)^2}\).
  5. Read off a and u: \(a^2=16\), so \(a=4\). \(u=1-2y\).
  6. Differentiate: \(\frac{du}{dy}=-2\), so \(du=-2\,dy\) and \(dy=-\frac{du}2\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  7. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle-\frac12\int\frac{du}{\sqrt{4^2-u^2}}\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  8. Apply arcsin: \(-\frac12\arcsin\frac u4+C\).Why? \(\int\frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2-u^2}}=\arcsin\frac ua\). It comes from \((\arcsin x)'=\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\). Unlike arctan, there is no \(\frac1a\) in front. See the rule
  9. Put y back: \(-\frac12\arcsin\frac{1-2y}4+C\).
  10. Check: \(\frac{d}{dy}\left[-\frac12\arcsin\frac{1-2y}4\right]=-\frac12\cdot\frac{-\frac24}{\sqrt{1-\frac{(1-2y)^2}{16}}}=\frac{\frac14}{\frac14\sqrt{16-(1-2y)^2}}=\frac1{\sqrt{15+4y-4y^2}}\) ✓Why? You need these derivatives for du: \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\) and \((\arcsin x)'=\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\). See the rule

Answer\(-\frac12\arcsin\frac{1-2y}4+C\)

5·E5

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(4x-7)\,dx}{x^2-6x+13}\)

UsesCompleting the square Log rule Arctan form

  1. Spot the form: a linear top over a quadratic that does not factor. Split it into a log part (top = derivative of bottom) and an arctan part (number on top).
  2. Complete the square on the bottom: half of \(-6\) is \(-3\), and \((-3)^2=9\): \(x^2-6x+13=(x^2-6x+9)+4=(x-3)^2+4\).Why? Take half the x-coefficient and square it, then add and subtract it: \(x^2+bx=\left(x+\frac b2\right)^2-\frac{b^2}4\). See the rule
  3. Split the top to match: \(4x-7=4(x-3)+5\) (since \(4(x-3)=4x-12\), and \(-12+5=-7\)).Why? Rewrite the top as (a multiple of the derivative of the bottom) + (a leftover number), so one piece fits the log or power rule and the other fits a different rule. See the rule
  4. Split the integral: \(\displaystyle4\int\frac{(x-3)\,dx}{(x-3)^2+4}+5\int\frac{dx}{(x-3)^2+4}\).Why? A sum on top can be split into separate fractions, \(\frac{a+b}c=\frac ac+\frac bc\), and the sum rule lets you integrate each piece separately. See the rule
  5. First part: name the bottom: \(w=(x-3)^2+4\), so \(dw=2(x-3)\,dx\) and \((x-3)\,dx=\frac{dw}2\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the ruleWhy? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. First part: log rule: \(4\cdot\frac12\int\frac{dw}w=2\ln|w|=2\ln(x^2-6x+13)\). No bars needed, since \((x-3)^2+4\) is always positive.Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the ruleWhy? ln only accepts positive inputs. The bars in \(\ln|u|\) keep the answer valid when u is negative. If u can never be negative (like \(x^2+5\)), you can drop them. See the rule
  7. Second part: read off u and a: \(u=x-3\), \(du=dx\), and \(a^2=4\), so \(a=2\).
  8. Second part: arctan: \(5\int\frac{du}{u^2+2^2}=5\cdot\frac12\arctan\frac u2=\frac52\arctan\frac{x-3}2\).Why? \(\int\frac{du}{a^2+u^2}=\frac1a\arctan\frac ua\). It comes from \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\). Factoring \(a^2\) out of the bottom is what puts the \(\frac1a\) in front. See the rule
  9. Combine: \(2\ln(x^2-6x+13)+\frac52\arctan\frac{x-3}2+C\).Why? Sum rule: integrate each term separately, then add the results. See the rule
  10. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}[\dots]=\frac{2(2x-6)}{x^2-6x+13}+\frac52\cdot\frac{\frac12}{1+\frac{(x-3)^2}4}=\frac{4x-12}{x^2-6x+13}+\frac5{x^2-6x+13}=\frac{4x-7}{x^2-6x+13}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(2\ln(x^2-6x+13)+\frac52\arctan\frac{x-3}2+C\)

5·E6

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{\tan x\,dx}{\sec x+4\cos x}\)

UsesArctan form

  1. Spot the form: a mix of tan, sec and cos. Rewrite everything in sec and tan, because \((\sec x)'=\sec x\tan x\). That usually leads to an arctan form.
  2. Rewrite cos: \(\cos x=\frac1{\sec x}\), so the bottom is \(\sec x+\frac4{\sec x}\).Why? cos and sec are reciprocals of each other, so \(\cos x=\frac1{\sec x}\). Likewise \(\frac1{\tan x}=\cot x=\frac{\cos x}{\sin x}\). See the rule
  3. Combine the bottom: write \(\sec x\) as \(\frac{\sec^2x}{\sec x}\): \(\sec x+\frac4{\sec x}=\frac{\sec^2x+4}{\sec x}\).Why? To add a whole number to a fraction, write it over the same denominator, e.g. \(1=\frac{1+z^2}{1+z^2}\). Then add the tops: \(\frac ac+\frac bc=\frac{a+b}c\). See the rule
  4. Flip into the top: \(\dfrac{\tan x}{(\sec^2x+4)/\sec x}=\dfrac{\sec x\tan x}{\sec^2x+4}\).Why? Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip: \(a\div\frac bc=a\cdot\frac cb\). See the rule
