  5. Name u: \(u=\sec x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  6. Differentiate: \(\frac{d}{dx}\sec x=\sec x\tan x\), so \(du=\sec x\tan x\,dx\). This is exactly the top.
  7. Read off a: \(a^2=4\), so \(a=2\). The integral is \(\displaystyle\int\frac{du}{u^2+2^2}\).
  8. Apply arctan: \(\frac12\arctan\frac u2+C\).Why? \(\int\frac{du}{a^2+u^2}=\frac1a\arctan\frac ua\). It comes from \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\). Factoring \(a^2\) out of the bottom is what puts the \(\frac1a\) in front. See the rule
  9. Put x back: \(\frac12\arctan\frac{\sec x}2+C\).
  10. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12\arctan\frac{\sec x}2=\frac12\cdot\frac{\frac12\sec x\tan x}{1+\frac{\sec^2x}4}=\frac{\sec x\tan x}{\sec^2x+4}\) ✓Why? You need these derivatives for du: \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\) and \((\arcsin x)'=\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\). See the rule

Answer\(\frac12\arctan\frac{\sec x}2+C\)

Exercises · full solutions

5·X1

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{e^{2x}\,dx}{\sqrt{25-4e^{4x}}}\)

UsesArcsin form Correction factor

  1. Match the form: arcsin, since there's a root of constant − variable².Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the ruleWhy? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  2. Read off: \(a=5\). \(u^2=4e^{4x}\Rightarrow u=2e^{2x}\).Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  3. Find du: \(du=4e^{2x}dx\), so \(e^{2x}dx=\frac{du}4\).
  4. Apply: \(\frac14\arcsin\frac{2e^{2x}}5+C\).

Answer\(\frac14\arcsin\frac{2e^{2x}}5+C\)

5·X2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{x^4\,dx}{x^2+3}\)

UsesLong division Arctan form

  1. Improper (degree 4 over degree 2), so divide: \(x^4\div(x^2+3)=x^2-3\) with remainder 9.Why? When the top's degree is at least the bottom's, divide first, like \(\frac72=3+\frac12\): quotient + remainder ÷ divisor. See the rule
  2. Rewrite: \(x^2-3+\frac9{x^2+3}\).
  3. Polynomial part: \(\frac{x^3}3-3x\).
  4. Arctan part: \(a^2=3\Rightarrow a=\sqrt3\). \(9\cdot\frac1{\sqrt3}\arctan\frac x{\sqrt3}=3\sqrt3\arctan\frac x{\sqrt3}\), since \(\frac9{\sqrt3}=3\sqrt3\).
  5. Combine: \(\frac{x^3}3-3x+3\sqrt3\arctan\frac x{\sqrt3}+C\).

Answer\(\frac{x^3}3-3x+3\sqrt3\arctan\frac x{\sqrt3}+C\)

5·X3

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(x-3)\,dx}{\sqrt{4x-x^2}}\)

UsesCompleting the square Power formula Arcsin form

  1. Complete the square: \(4x-x^2=-(x^2-4x)=-(x^2-4x+4)+4=4-(x-2)^2\).Why? Take half the x-coefficient and square it, then add and subtract it: \(x^2+bx=\left(x+\frac b2\right)^2-\frac{b^2}4\). See the rule
  2. Split the top to match \(x-2\): \(x-3=(x-2)-1\).Why? Rewrite the top as (a multiple of the derivative of the bottom) + (a leftover number), so one piece fits the log or power rule and the other fits arctan or arcsin. See the rule
  3. First part, power formula: \(\int\frac{(x-2)\,dx}{\sqrt{4-(x-2)^2}}\). Let \(w=4-(x-2)^2\), so \(dw=-2(x-2)dx\). This gives \(-\frac12\int w^{-1/2}dw=-w^{1/2}=-\sqrt{4x-x^2}\).
  4. Second part, arcsin: \(-\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{4-(x-2)^2}}\), with \(a=2\), \(u=x-2\). This gives \(-\arcsin\frac{x-2}2\).
  5. Combine: \(-\sqrt{4x-x^2}-\arcsin\frac{x-2}2+C\).

Answer\(-\sqrt{4x-x^2}-\arcsin\frac{x-2}2+C\)

5·X4

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dy}{6y-2-9y^2}\)

UsesCompleting the square Arctan form

  1. Rearrange the bottom with the \(y^2\) first: \(6y-2-9y^2=-(9y^2-6y+2)\).
  2. Complete the square: \(9y^2-6y+1=(3y-1)^2\), so \(9y^2-6y+2=(3y-1)^2+1\).Why? Take half the x-coefficient and square it, then add and subtract it: \(x^2+bx=\left(x+\frac b2\right)^2-\frac{b^2}4\). See the rule
  3. Name u: \(u=3y-1\), \(du=3\,dy\), \(a=1\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  4. Apply arctan (keep the minus): \(-\frac13\int\frac{du}{u^2+1}=-\frac13\arctan(3y-1)+C\).

Answer\(-\frac13\arctan(3y-1)+C\)

5·X5

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(1+\tan x)\,dx}{\cos^2x\sqrt{4-9\tan^2x}}\)

UsesArcsin form Power formula

  1. Substitute first: \(u=\tan x\), so \(du=\sec^2x\,dx=\frac{dx}{\cos^2x}\). The integral becomes \(\int\frac{(1+u)\,du}{\sqrt{4-9u^2}}\).
  2. Split: \(\int\frac{du}{\sqrt{4-9u^2}}+\int\frac{u\,du}{\sqrt{4-9u^2}}\).Why? A sum on top can be split into separate fractions, \(\frac{a+b}c=\frac ac+\frac bc\), and the sum rule lets you integrate each piece separately. See the rule
  3. First part, arcsin: \(a=2\) and \(w=3u\), so \(du=\frac{dw}3\). This gives \(\frac13\arcsin\frac{3u}2\).
  4. Second part, power formula: \(v=4-9u^2\), \(dv=-18u\,du\). This gives \(-\frac1{18}\int v^{-1/2}dv=-\frac1{18}\cdot2v^{1/2}=-\frac19\sqrt{4-9u^2}\).
  5. Put x back: \(\frac13\arcsin\frac{3\tan x}2-\frac19\sqrt{4-9\tan^2x}+C\).

Answer\(\frac13\arcsin\frac{3\tan x}2-\frac19\sqrt{4-9\tan^2x}+C\)

5·X6

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{\cot x\,dx}{\csc x+16\sin x}\)

UsesArctan form

  1. Rewrite csc: \(\csc x=\frac1{\sin x}\), so the bottom is \(\frac1{\sin x}+16\sin x=\frac{1+16\sin^2x}{\sin x}\).Why? \(\csc\theta\) is defined as \(\frac1{\sin\theta}\). See the rule
  2. Flip into the top: \(\cot x\cdot\frac{\sin x}{1+16\sin^2x}\). And \(\cot x\sin x=\cos x\), so it's \(\frac{\cos x}{1+16\sin^2x}\).Why? \(\cot x\cdot\sin x=\frac{\cos x}{\sin x}\cdot\sin x=\cos x\). See the rule
  3. Name u: \(u^2=16\sin^2x\Rightarrow u=4\sin x\), \(du=4\cos x\,dx\). And \(a=1\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  4. Apply arctan: \(\frac14\arctan(4\sin x)+C\).

Answer\(\frac14\arctan(4\sin x)+C\)

LESSON 7

Integration by Parts

\(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). Choose \(u\) so that it gets simpler when differentiated, and \(dv\) so it's easy to integrate. LIATE tells you which factor to make \(u\).

  1. Pick \(u\) by LIATE (whichever comes first: Log, Inverse trig, Algebraic, Trig, Exponential). The rest is \(dv\).
  2. Compute \(du\) and \(v\) (no \(+C\) needed for \(v\)).
  3. Write \(uv-\int v\,du\). Repeat if the new integral still needs parts. The tabular method is faster for polynomial × (sin, cos, or \(e^{x}\)).
  4. Cycling integrals (\(e^x\sin x\), \(\sec^3x\)): when the original integral \(I\) shows up again, move it to the left and solve, e.g. \(2I=\dots\)

Formula practice · integration by parts

7·P1

\(\displaystyle\int xe^x\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts

  1. Spot the form: a product of two different kinds of function (algebraic \(x\) times exponential \(e^x\)), and neither is the derivative of the other's inside. Use integration by parts: \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\).Why? Integration by parts is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\). Integrate both sides and rearrange to get \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). See the rule
  2. Choose u by LIATE: x is Algebraic and \(e^x\) is Exponential. A comes before E, so \(u=x\) and \(dv=e^x\,dx\).Why? LIATE: make u the function that gets simpler when you differentiate it (ln x becomes 1/x, and powers of x drop a degree). dv should be easy to integrate. See the rule
  3. Find du: differentiate u: \(du=dx\).
  4. Find v: integrate dv: \(v=\int e^x\,dx=e^x\).Why? No +C is needed for v. Any constant you add there cancels out in \(uv-\int v\,du\), so add C once at the very end. See the rule
  5. Plug into \(uv-\int v\,du\): \(x\cdot e^x-\int e^x\,dx\). The new integral is easier than the original.
  6. Finish: \(\int e^x\,dx=e^x\), so the answer is \(xe^x-e^x+C\).
  7. Check: product rule: \(\frac{d}{dx}(xe^x)=e^x+xe^x\). Subtract \(\frac{d}{dx}e^x=e^x\): \(e^x+xe^x-e^x=xe^x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(xe^x-e^x+C\)

7·P2

\(\displaystyle\int x\sin x\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts

  1. Spot the form: algebraic \(x\) times trig \(\sin x\): integration by parts.Why? Integration by parts is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\). Integrate both sides and rearrange to get \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). See the rule
  2. Choose u by LIATE: A comes before T, so \(u=x\) and \(dv=\sin x\,dx\).Why? LIATE: make u the function that gets simpler when you differentiate it (ln x becomes 1/x, and powers of x drop a degree). dv should be easy to integrate. See the rule
  3. Find du: \(du=dx\).
  4. Find v: \(v=\int\sin x\,dx=-\cos x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\cos u=-\sin u\), so the antiderivative of \(\sin u\) is \(-\cos u\). Forgetting that minus is the most common slip. See the rule
  5. Plug into \(uv-\int v\,du\): \(x(-\cos x)-\int(-\cos x)\,dx=-x\cos x+\int\cos x\,dx\). Minus times minus gives plus.
  6. Finish: \(\int\cos x\,dx=\sin x\), so the answer is \(-x\cos x+\sin x+C\).
  7. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}(-x\cos x)=-\cos x+x\sin x\) (product rule), and \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin x=\cos x\). Total: \(-\cos x+x\sin x+\cos x=x\sin x\) ✓

Answer\(-x\cos x+\sin x+C\)

7·P3

\(\displaystyle\int x^2\ln x\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts

  1. Spot the form: algebraic \(x^2\) times logarithmic \(\ln x\): integration by parts.Why? Integration by parts is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\). Integrate both sides and rearrange to get \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). See the rule
  2. Choose u by LIATE: L comes first, so \(u=\ln x\) and \(dv=x^2\,dx\). (Choosing \(dv=\ln x\,dx\) would need \(\int\ln x\,dx\) first, which is harder.)Why? LIATE: make u the function that gets simpler when you differentiate it (ln x becomes 1/x, and powers of x drop a degree). dv should be easy to integrate. See the rule
  3. Find du: \(du=\frac1x\,dx\).
  4. Find v: \(v=\int x^2\,dx=\frac{x^3}3\).Why? No +C is needed for v. Any constant you add there cancels out in \(uv-\int v\,du\), so add C once at the very end. See the rule
  5. Plug into \(uv-\int v\,du\): \(\frac{x^3}3\ln x-\int\frac{x^3}3\cdot\frac1x\,dx\).
  6. Simplify the new integral: \(\frac{x^3}x=x^2\), so it is \(\frac13\int x^2\,dx\).Why? Exponent law: \(\frac{a^m}{a^n}=a^{m-n}\), so \(\frac{x^3}x=x^{3-1}=x^2\). See the rule
  7. Finish: \(\frac13\cdot\frac{x^3}3=\frac{x^3}9\), so the answer is \(\frac{x^3}3\ln x-\frac{x^3}9+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{x^3}3\ln x\right)=x^2\ln x+\frac{x^3}3\cdot\frac1x=x^2\ln x+\frac{x^2}3\). Subtract \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac{x^3}9=\frac{x^2}3\): \(x^2\ln x\) ✓

Answer\(\frac{x^3}3\ln x-\frac{x^3}9+C\)

7·P4

\(\displaystyle\int\arctan x\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts Log rule

  1. Spot the form: a single function, \(\arctan x\), with no basic antiderivative formula. Use parts with \(dv=dx\): its derivative is simple even though the function is not.Why? Integration by parts is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\). Integrate both sides and rearrange to get \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). See the rule
  2. Choose u: \(u=\arctan x\), \(dv=dx\).Why? LIATE: make u the function that gets simpler when you differentiate it (ln x becomes 1/x, and powers of x drop a degree). dv should be easy to integrate. See the rule
  3. Find du: \(du=\frac{dx}{1+x^2}\).Why? You need these derivatives for du: \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\) and \((\arcsin x)'=\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\). See the rule
  4. Find v: \(v=\int dx=x\).
  5. Plug into \(uv-\int v\,du\): \(x\arctan x-\displaystyle\int\frac{x\,dx}{1+x^2}\).
  6. Solve the new integral: it is a log-rule integral. Use the letter \(w\) because u is already taken: \(w=1+x^2\), \(dw=2x\,dx\), so \(x\,dx=\frac{dw}2\). Then \(\int\frac{x\,dx}{1+x^2}=\frac12\int\frac{dw}w=\frac12\ln(1+x^2)\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  7. Finish: \(x\arctan x-\frac12\ln(1+x^2)+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}(x\arctan x)=\arctan x+\frac x{1+x^2}\), and \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12\ln(1+x^2)=\frac x{1+x^2}\). Subtracting leaves \(\arctan x\) ✓

Answer\(x\arctan x-\frac12\ln(1+x^2)+C\)

7·P5

\(\displaystyle\int\arcsin x\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts Power formula

  1. Spot the form: a single function, \(\arcsin x\), with no basic antiderivative formula. Use parts with \(dv=dx\).Why? Integration by parts is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\). Integrate both sides and rearrange to get \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). See the rule
  2. Choose u: \(u=\arcsin x\), \(dv=dx\).Why? LIATE: make u the function that gets simpler when you differentiate it (ln x becomes 1/x, and powers of x drop a degree). dv should be easy to integrate. See the rule
  3. Find du: \(du=\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\).Why? You need these derivatives for du: \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\) and \((\arcsin x)'=\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\). See the rule
  4. Find v: \(v=\int dx=x\).
  5. Plug into \(uv-\int v\,du\): \(x\arcsin x-\displaystyle\int\frac{x\,dx}{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\).
  6. Set up the new integral: \(w=1-x^2\), \(dw=-2x\,dx\), so \(x\,dx=-\frac{dw}2\). Then \(\int\frac{x\,dx}{\sqrt{1-x^2}}=-\frac12\int w^{-1/2}\,dw\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the ruleWhy? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  7. Power formula: \(-\frac12\cdot\frac{w^{1/2}}{1/2}=-w^{1/2}=-\sqrt{1-x^2}\). Dividing by \(\frac12\) is multiplying by 2, which cancels the \(\frac12\) in front.Why? Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip: \(a\div\frac bc=a\cdot\frac cb\). See the rule
  8. Finish: \(x\arcsin x-\left(-\sqrt{1-x^2}\right)=x\arcsin x+\sqrt{1-x^2}+C\). Subtracting a negative gives a plus.
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}(x\arcsin x)=\arcsin x+\frac x{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\), and \(\frac{d}{dx}\sqrt{1-x^2}=\frac{-2x}{2\sqrt{1-x^2}}=-\frac x{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\). Adding leaves \(\arcsin x\) ✓

Answer\(x\arcsin x+\sqrt{1-x^2}+C\)

7·P6

\(\displaystyle\int e^{2x}\cos x\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts

  1. Spot the form: exponential times trig. Neither gets simpler when differentiated, so parts will bring the original integral back. Do parts twice, then solve for it.Why? Integration by parts is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\). Integrate both sides and rearrange to get \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). See the rule
  2. Call the integral I and choose u: \(I=\int e^{2x}\cos x\,dx\). Take \(u=\cos x\), \(dv=e^{2x}\,dx\).Why? LIATE puts T before E, so \(u=\cos x\). With exponential times trig, either choice works, as long as you make the same kind of choice in the second round. Otherwise you undo your first round. See the rule
  3. Find du and v: \(du=-\sin x\,dx\). For v, integrate \(e^{2x}\) with the correction factor: \(v=\frac12e^{2x}\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  4. First round: \(I=uv-\int v\,du=\frac12e^{2x}\cos x-\int\frac12e^{2x}(-\sin x)\,dx=\frac12e^{2x}\cos x+\frac12\int e^{2x}\sin x\,dx\).
  5. Second round: on \(\int e^{2x}\sin x\,dx\), again let the trig part be u: \(u=\sin x\), \(dv=e^{2x}dx\), \(du=\cos x\,dx\), \(v=\frac12e^{2x}\). So \(\int e^{2x}\sin x\,dx=\frac12e^{2x}\sin x-\frac12\int e^{2x}\cos x\,dx=\frac12e^{2x}\sin x-\frac12I\).
  6. Substitute back: \(I=\frac12e^{2x}\cos x+\frac12\left(\frac12e^{2x}\sin x-\frac12I\right)=\frac12e^{2x}\cos x+\frac14e^{2x}\sin x-\frac14I\).
  7. Collect the I terms: add \(\frac14I\) to both sides: \(I+\frac14I=\frac54I\), so \(\frac54I=\frac12e^{2x}\cos x+\frac14e^{2x}\sin x\).Why? When the original integral comes back, treat it like an unknown I in an equation, move it to one side, and solve with algebra. See the rule
  8. Solve for I: multiply both sides by \(\frac45\): \(I=\frac25e^{2x}\cos x+\frac15e^{2x}\sin x=\frac15e^{2x}(2\cos x+\sin x)+C\). Add C only now, at the end.
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left[\frac15e^{2x}(2\cos x+\sin x)\right]=\frac15\left[2e^{2x}(2\cos x+\sin x)+e^{2x}(-2\sin x+\cos x)\right]=\frac15e^{2x}(5\cos x)=e^{2x}\cos x\) ✓

Answer\(\frac15e^{2x}(2\cos x+\sin x)+C\)

7·P7

\(\displaystyle\int\ln3x\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts ∫ln x

  1. Spot the form: a single ln function with no basic formula. Use parts with \(dv=dx\).Why? Integration by parts is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\). Integrate both sides and rearrange to get \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). See the rule
  2. Choose u: \(u=\ln3x\), \(dv=dx\).Why? LIATE: make u the function that gets simpler when you differentiate it (ln x becomes 1/x, and powers of x drop a degree). dv should be easy to integrate. See the rule
  3. Find du: chain rule: \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln3x=\frac1{3x}\cdot3=\frac1x\), so \(du=\frac{dx}x\).Why? Another way to see it: \(\ln3x=\ln3+\ln x\), and \(\ln3\) is a constant whose derivative is 0. So \((\ln3x)'=\frac1x\). See the rule
  4. Find v: \(v=\int dx=x\).
  5. Plug into \(uv-\int v\,du\): \(x\ln3x-\int x\cdot\frac{dx}x=x\ln3x-\int dx\). The x's cancel.
  6. Finish: \(\int dx=x\), so the answer is \(x\ln3x-x+C\).
  7. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}(x\ln3x)=\ln3x+x\cdot\frac1x=\ln3x+1\). Subtract \(\frac{d}{dx}x=1\): \(\ln3x\) ✓

Answer\(x\ln3x-x+C\)

Slide examples

7·E1

\(\displaystyle\int x\cos x\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts

  1. Spot the form: a product of two different kinds of function (a power of x and a trig function), with no u-substitution that works. Use integration by parts.Why? Integration by parts: \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). It is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\), integrate both sides and rearrange. See the rule
  2. Choose u: \(u=x\) (A comes before T in LIATE), \(dv=\cos x\,dx\).Why? LIATE: make u the function that gets simpler when you differentiate it (ln x becomes 1/x, and powers of x drop a degree). dv should be easy to integrate. See the rule
  3. Find du: \(du=dx\).
  4. Find v: \(v=\int\cos x\,dx=\sin x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\sin u=\cos u\), so going backwards \(\int\cos u\,du=\sin u\). No minus sign here. See the ruleWhy? No +C is needed for v. Any constant you add there cancels out in \(uv-\int v\,du\), so add C once at the very end. See the rule
  5. Plug in: \(uv-\int v\,du=x\sin x-\int\sin x\,dx\).
  6. Finish the integral: \(\int\sin x\,dx=-\cos x\), so \(-\int\sin x\,dx=+\cos x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\cos u=-\sin u\), so the antiderivative of \(\sin u\) is \(-\cos u\). Forgetting that minus is the most common slip. See the rule
  7. Combine: \(x\sin x+\cos x+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}(x\sin x+\cos x)=\sin x+x\cos x-\sin x=x\cos x\) ✓ (product rule on \(x\sin x\))Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(x\sin x+\cos x+C\)

7·E2

\(\displaystyle\int\ln x\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts ∫ln x

  1. Spot the form: \(\ln x\) on its own has no basic formula, but its derivative \(\frac1x\) is simple. Use integration by parts with \(dv=dx\).Why? Integration by parts: \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). It is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\), integrate both sides and rearrange. See the rule
  2. Choose u: there's only one function, so \(u=\ln x\) and \(dv=dx\).Why? LIATE: make u the function that gets simpler when you differentiate it (ln x becomes 1/x, and powers of x drop a degree). dv should be easy to integrate. See the rule
  3. Find du: \(du=\frac1x\,dx\).
  4. Find v: \(v=\int dx=x\).Why? No +C is needed for v. Any constant you add there cancels out in \(uv-\int v\,du\), so add C once at the very end. See the rule
  5. Plug in: \(uv-\int v\,du=x\ln x-\int x\cdot\frac1x\,dx\).
  6. Simplify: \(x\cdot\frac1x=1\), so the integral left is \(\int dx=x\).
  7. Combine: \(x\ln x-x+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}(x\ln x-x)=\ln x+x\cdot\frac1x-1=\ln x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(x\ln x-x+C\)

7·E3

\(\displaystyle\int x\arctan x\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts Arctan form

  1. Spot the form: a power of x times an inverse trig function. Use integration by parts, since \(\arctan x\) becomes a simple fraction when you differentiate it.Why? Integration by parts: \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). It is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\), integrate both sides and rearrange. See the rule
  2. Choose u: I comes before A in LIATE, so \(u=\arctan x\) and \(dv=x\,dx\).Why? LIATE: make u the function that gets simpler when you differentiate it (ln x becomes 1/x, and powers of x drop a degree). dv should be easy to integrate. See the rule
  3. Find du: \(du=\frac{dx}{1+x^2}\).Why? You need these derivatives for du: \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\) and \((\arcsin x)'=\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\). See the rule
  4. Find v: \(v=\int x\,dx=\frac{x^2}2\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the ruleWhy? No +C is needed for v. Any constant you add there cancels out in \(uv-\int v\,du\), so add C once at the very end. See the rule
  5. Plug in: \(\frac{x^2}2\arctan x-\int\frac{x^2}2\cdot\frac{dx}{1+x^2}=\frac{x^2}2\arctan x-\frac12\int\frac{x^2}{1+x^2}dx\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  6. Simplify the fraction: the top has the same degree as the bottom, so rewrite it: \(\frac{x^2}{x^2+1}=\frac{(x^2+1)-1}{x^2+1}=1-\frac1{x^2+1}\).Why? Add and subtract the same thing on top to make it match the bottom: \(\frac{x^2}{x^2+1}=\frac{(x^2+1)-1}{x^2+1}=1-\frac1{x^2+1}\). This is a quick form of long division. See the rule
  7. Integrate: \(\int\left(1-\frac1{1+x^2}\right)dx=x-\arctan x\), so \(\frac12\int\frac{x^2}{1+x^2}dx=\frac12x-\frac12\arctan x\).Why? \(\int\frac{du}{a^2+u^2}=\frac1a\arctan\frac ua\). It comes from \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\). Factoring \(a^2\) out of the bottom is what puts the \(\frac1a\) in front. See the rule
  8. Subtract it: \(\frac{x^2}2\arctan x-\left(\frac12x-\frac12\arctan x\right)=\frac12x^2\arctan x-\frac12x+\frac12\arctan x+C\). The minus flips both signs inside the bracket.
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}[\dots]=x\arctan x+\frac{x^2}{2(1+x^2)}-\frac12+\frac1{2(1+x^2)}=x\arctan x+\frac{x^2+1}{2(1+x^2)}-\frac12=x\arctan x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac12x^2\arctan x-\frac12x+\frac12\arctan x+C\)

7·E4

\(\displaystyle\int\sec^3x\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts ∫sec³x

  1. Spot the form: an odd power of sec with nothing to substitute. Split off \(\sec^2x\) and use integration by parts. The original integral will come back, so you solve for it.Why? Integration by parts: \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). It is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\), integrate both sides and rearrange. See the rule
  2. Split: \(\sec^3x=\sec x\cdot\sec^2x\).Why? Splitting off one factor uses \(a^n=a^{n-1}\cdot a\). Here one \(\sec^2x\) is easy to integrate (it gives \(\tan x\)), so it becomes dv. See the rule
  3. Choose u and dv: \(u=\sec x\), \(dv=\sec^2x\,dx\).
  4. Find du and v: \(du=\sec x\tan x\,dx\), \(v=\int\sec^2x\,dx=\tan x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\tan u=\sec^2u\), so going backwards \(\int\sec^2u\,du=\tan u\). See the rule
  5. Plug in: call the integral I: \(I=\sec x\tan x-\int\tan x\cdot\sec x\tan x\,dx=\sec x\tan x-\int\sec x\tan^2x\,dx\).
  6. Pythagorean identity: \(\tan^2x=\sec^2x-1\), so \(\int\sec x\tan^2x\,dx=\int\sec x(\sec^2x-1)\,dx=\int\sec^3x\,dx-\int\sec x\,dx\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  7. Recognize I: \(\int\sec^3x\,dx\) is I itself, and \(\int\sec x\,dx=\ln|\sec x+\tan x|\). So the piece is \(I-\ln|\sec x+\tan x|\).Why? Multiply \(\sec u\) by \(\frac{\sec u+\tan u}{\sec u+\tan u}\). The top becomes \(\sec^2u+\sec u\tan u\), which is exactly the derivative of the bottom, so the log rule finishes it. See the rule
  8. Substitute back: \(I=\sec x\tan x-(I-\ln|\sec x+\tan x|)=\sec x\tan x-I+\ln|\sec x+\tan x|\).
  9. Solve for I: add I to both sides: \(2I=\sec x\tan x+\ln|\sec x+\tan x|\). Divide by 2: \(I=\frac12\sec x\tan x+\frac12\ln|\sec x+\tan x|+C\).Why? When the original integral comes back, treat it like an unknown I in an equation, move it to one side, and solve with algebra. See the rule
  10. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12\sec x\tan x=\frac12(\sec x\tan^2x+\sec^3x)\) and \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12\ln|\sec x+\tan x|=\frac12\sec x\). Using \(\tan^2x=\sec^2x-1\), the sum is \(\frac12(2\sec^3x-\sec x)+\frac12\sec x=\sec^3x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac12\sec x\tan x+\frac12\ln|\sec x+\tan x|+C\)

7·E5

\(\displaystyle\int(x^2+x)\sin x\,dx\)

tabular

UsesIntegration by parts Tabular method

SignDifferentiateIntegrate
+\(x^2+x\)\(\sin x\)
−\(2x+1\)\(-\cos x\)
+\(2\)\(-\sin x\)
\(0\)\(\cos x\)
  1. Spot the form: a polynomial times \(\sin x\). Integration by parts would need two rounds (the polynomial has degree 2), so use the tabular shortcut.Why? The tabular method is integration by parts repeated. Each diagonal product is one \(uv\) term, and the alternating signs come from the minus in \(uv-\int v\,du\) each round. See the rule
  2. Set up the table: left column: differentiate \(x^2+x\) until you reach 0 (\(x^2+x\to2x+1\to2\to0\)). Right column: integrate \(\sin x\) the same number of times (\(\sin x\to-\cos x\to-\sin x\to\cos x\)).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\cos u=-\sin u\), so the antiderivative of \(\sin u\) is \(-\cos u\). Forgetting that minus is the most common slip. See the ruleWhy? \(\frac{d}{du}\sin u=\cos u\), so going backwards \(\int\cos u\,du=\sin u\). No minus sign here. See the rule
  3. Signs alternate: \(+,-,+\), starting with +.
  4. First diagonal: \(+(x^2+x)\cdot(-\cos x)=-(x^2+x)\cos x\).
  5. Second diagonal: \(-(2x+1)\cdot(-\sin x)=+(2x+1)\sin x\).
  6. Third diagonal: \(+2\cdot\cos x=2\cos x\).
  7. Stop: the left column has reached 0, so there are no more terms.
  8. Combine: \(-(x^2+x)\cos x+(2x+1)\sin x+2\cos x+C\).
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}[\dots]=-(2x+1)\cos x+(x^2+x)\sin x+2\sin x+(2x+1)\cos x-2\sin x=(x^2+x)\sin x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(-(x^2+x)\cos x+(2x+1)\sin x+2\cos x+C\)

7·E6

\(\displaystyle\int e^x\sin x\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts ∫eˣ sin x

  1. Spot the form: \(e^x\) times a trig function. Neither one ever disappears when you differentiate, so do integration by parts twice and the original integral comes back. Then solve for it.Why? Integration by parts: \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). It is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\), integrate both sides and rearrange. See the rule
  2. Call the integral I: \(I=\int e^x\sin x\,dx\).
  3. First round: choose: \(u=\sin x\), \(dv=e^xdx\), so \(du=\cos x\,dx\) and \(v=e^x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}e^u=e^u\): e to a power is its own derivative, so it is also its own antiderivative. Only the correction factor changes. See the rule
  4. First round: plug in: \(I=e^x\sin x-\int e^x\cos x\,dx\).
  5. Second round: choose: on \(\int e^x\cos x\,dx\), keep the same kind of choice: \(u=\cos x\), \(dv=e^xdx\), so \(du=-\sin x\,dx\) and \(v=e^x\).Why? LIATE puts T before E, so \(u=\cos x\). With exponential times trig, either choice works, as long as you make the same kind of choice in the second round. Otherwise you undo your first round. See the rule
  6. Second round: plug in: \(\int e^x\cos x\,dx=e^x\cos x-\int e^x(-\sin x)\,dx=e^x\cos x+I\).
  7. Substitute back: \(I=e^x\sin x-(e^x\cos x+I)=e^x\sin x-e^x\cos x-I\).
  8. Solve for I: add I to both sides: \(2I=e^x\sin x-e^x\cos x\), so \(I=\frac12e^x\sin x-\frac12e^x\cos x+C\).Why? When the original integral comes back, treat it like an unknown I in an equation, move it to one side, and solve with algebra. See the rule
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12e^x(\sin x-\cos x)=\frac12e^x(\sin x-\cos x)+\frac12e^x(\cos x+\sin x)=e^x\sin x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac12e^x\sin x-\frac12e^x\cos x+C\)

7·E7

\(\displaystyle\int x^2\sin x\,dx\)

additional ex.

UsesIntegration by parts Tabular method

  1. Spot the form: \(x^2\) times \(\sin x\). Use integration by parts. The power drops by one each round, so it takes two rounds.Why? Integration by parts: \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). It is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\), integrate both sides and rearrange. See the rule
  2. First round: choose: \(u=x^2\), \(dv=\sin x\,dx\).Why? LIATE: make u the function that gets simpler when you differentiate it (ln x becomes 1/x, and powers of x drop a degree). dv should be easy to integrate. See the rule
  3. First round: du and v: \(du=2x\,dx\), \(v=\int\sin x\,dx=-\cos x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\cos u=-\sin u\), so the antiderivative of \(\sin u\) is \(-\cos u\). Forgetting that minus is the most common slip. See the rule
  4. First round: plug in: \(x^2(-\cos x)-\int(-\cos x)(2x)\,dx=-x^2\cos x+2\int x\cos x\,dx\).
  5. Second round: \(\int x\cos x\,dx=x\sin x+\cos x\). This is exactly 7·E1: \(u=x\), \(dv=\cos x\,dx\).
  6. Combine: \(-x^2\cos x+2(x\sin x+\cos x)=-x^2\cos x+2x\sin x+2\cos x+C\).
  7. Tabular shortcut: differentiate \(x^2\to2x\to2\to0\), integrate \(\sin x\to-\cos x\to-\sin x\to\cos x\), signs \(+,-,+\). The diagonals give \(-x^2\cos x+2x\sin x+2\cos x\), the same result.Why? The tabular method is integration by parts repeated. Each diagonal product is one \(uv\) term, and the alternating signs come from the minus in \(uv-\int v\,du\) each round. See the rule
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}[\dots]=-2x\cos x+x^2\sin x+2\sin x+2x\cos x-2\sin x=x^2\sin x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(-x^2\cos x+2x\sin x+2\cos x+C\)

7·E8

\(\displaystyle\int e^x\cos x\,dx\)

additional ex.

UsesIntegration by parts

  1. Spot the form: \(e^x\) times \(\cos x\). Do integration by parts twice and the original integral comes back. Then solve for it.Why? Integration by parts: \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). It is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\), integrate both sides and rearrange. See the rule
  2. Call the integral I: \(I=\int e^x\cos x\,dx\).
  3. First round: choose: \(u=e^x\), \(dv=\cos x\,dx\), so \(du=e^xdx\) and \(v=\sin x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\sin u=\cos u\), so going backwards \(\int\cos u\,du=\sin u\). No minus sign here. See the rule
  4. First round: plug in: \(I=e^x\sin x-\int e^x\sin x\,dx\).
  5. Second round: choose: keep \(u=e^x\) again: \(dv=\sin x\,dx\), so \(du=e^xdx\) and \(v=-\cos x\).Why? LIATE puts T before E, so \(u=\cos x\). With exponential times trig, either choice works, as long as you make the same kind of choice in the second round. Otherwise you undo your first round. See the ruleWhy? \(\frac{d}{du}\cos u=-\sin u\), so the antiderivative of \(\sin u\) is \(-\cos u\). Forgetting that minus is the most common slip. See the rule
  6. Second round: plug in: \(\int e^x\sin x\,dx=-e^x\cos x-\int(-\cos x)e^x\,dx=-e^x\cos x+I\).
  7. Substitute back: \(I=e^x\sin x-(-e^x\cos x+I)=e^x\sin x+e^x\cos x-I\).
  8. Solve for I: \(2I=e^x\sin x+e^x\cos x\), so \(I=\frac12e^x\cos x+\frac12e^x\sin x+C\).Why? When the original integral comes back, treat it like an unknown I in an equation, move it to one side, and solve with algebra. See the rule
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12e^x(\cos x+\sin x)=\frac12e^x(\cos x+\sin x)+\frac12e^x(-\sin x+\cos x)=e^x\cos x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac12e^x\cos x+\frac12e^x\sin x+C\)

Exercises · full solutions

7·X1

\(\displaystyle\int x\ln x\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts

  1. Choose u: L comes before A, so \(u=\ln x\) and \(dv=x\,dx\).Why? LIATE: make u the function that gets simpler when you differentiate it (ln x becomes 1/x, and powers of x drop a degree). dv should be easy to integrate. See the rule
  2. Find du and v: \(du=\frac{dx}x\), \(v=\frac{x^2}2\).
  3. Plug in: \(\frac{x^2}2\ln x-\int\frac{x^2}2\cdot\frac1x\,dx=\frac{x^2}2\ln x-\frac12\int x\,dx\).
  4. Finish: \(\frac{x^2}2\ln x-\frac{x^2}4+C\).

Answer\(\frac{x^2}2\ln x-\frac{x^2}4+C\)

7·X2

\(\displaystyle\int(2x^3+x+4)\sin5x\,dx\)

tabular

UsesIntegration by parts Tabular method

SignDifferentiateIntegrate
+\(2x^3+x+4\)\(\sin5x\)
−\(6x^2+1\)\(-\frac15\cos5x\)
+\(12x\)\(-\frac1{25}\sin5x\)
−\(12\)\(\frac1{125}\cos5x\)
\(0\)\(\frac1{625}\sin5x\)
  1. Set up the table: differentiate \(2x^3+x+4\to6x^2+1\to12x\to12\to0\). Integrate \(\sin5x\) four times, with each step adding a factor of \(\frac15\).
  2. Signs alternate \(+,-,+,-\).
  3. Multiply diagonally: \(+(2x^3+x+4)\left(-\frac15\cos5x\right)\), \(-(6x^2+1)\left(-\frac1{25}\sin5x\right)\), \(+12x\left(\frac1{125}\cos5x\right)\), \(-12\left(\frac1{625}\sin5x\right)\).
  4. Simplify the signs and add C.

Answer\(-\frac15(2x^3+x+4)\cos5x+\frac1{25}(6x^2+1)\sin5x+\frac{12}{125}x\cos5x-\frac{12}{625}\sin5x+C\)

7·X3

\(\displaystyle\int x\sin4x\cos4x\,dx\)

UsesDouble angle Integration by parts

  1. Simplify first with the double angle: \(\sin4x\cos4x=\frac12\sin8x\). The integral is \(\frac12\int x\sin8x\,dx\).
  2. Choose u: \(u=x\), \(dv=\sin8x\,dx\), so \(du=dx\), \(v=-\frac18\cos8x\).Why? LIATE: make u the function that gets simpler when you differentiate it (ln x becomes 1/x, and powers of x drop a degree). dv should be easy to integrate. See the rule
  3. Plug in: \(\frac12\left[-\frac x8\cos8x+\frac18\int\cos8x\,dx\right]=\frac12\left[-\frac x8\cos8x+\frac1{64}\sin8x\right]\).
  4. Multiply by ½: \(-\frac x{16}\cos8x+\frac1{128}\sin8x+C\).

Answer\(-\frac x{16}\cos8x+\frac1{128}\sin8x+C\)

7·X4

\(\displaystyle\int\sin(\ln x)\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts

  1. Call the integral I. First round: \(u=\sin(\ln x)\), \(dv=dx\). Then \(du=\cos(\ln x)\cdot\frac1x\,dx\), \(v=x\), so \(I=x\sin(\ln x)-\int\cos(\ln x)\,dx\). The x and \(\frac1x\) cancel.Why? When the original integral comes back, treat it like an unknown I in an equation, move it to one side, and solve with algebra. See the rule
  2. Second round: \(u=\cos(\ln x)\), \(dv=dx\): \(\int\cos(\ln x)\,dx=x\cos(\ln x)+\int\sin(\ln x)\,dx=x\cos(\ln x)+I\).
  3. Substitute back: \(I=x\sin(\ln x)-x\cos(\ln x)-I\).
  4. Solve: \(2I=x[\sin(\ln x)-\cos(\ln x)]\), so \(I=\frac x2[\sin(\ln x)-\cos(\ln x)]+C\).Why? When the original integral comes back, treat it like an unknown I in an equation, move it to one side, and solve with algebra. See the rule

Answer\(\frac x2\left[\sin(\ln x)-\cos(\ln x)\right]+C\)

7·X5

\(\displaystyle\int x^3e^{2x}\,dx\)

tabular

UsesIntegration by parts Tabular method

SignDifferentiateIntegrate
+\(x^3\)\(e^{2x}\)
−\(3x^2\)\(\frac12e^{2x}\)
+\(6x\)\(\frac14e^{2x}\)
−\(6\)\(\frac18e^{2x}\)
\(0\)\(\frac1{16}e^{2x}\)
  1. Set up the table: differentiate \(x^3\to3x^2\to6x\to6\to0\). Integrate \(e^{2x}\to\frac12e^{2x}\to\frac14e^{2x}\to\frac18e^{2x}\to\frac1{16}e^{2x}\).
  2. Signs alternate \(+,-,+,-\).
  3. Multiply diagonally: \(\frac12x^3e^{2x}-\frac34x^2e^{2x}+\frac68xe^{2x}-\frac6{16}e^{2x}\).
  4. Factor out \(e^{2x}\) and simplify: \(e^{2x}\left(\frac{x^3}2-\frac{3x^2}4+\frac{3x}4-\frac38\right)+C\).

Answer\(e^{2x}\left(\frac{x^3}2-\frac{3x^2}4+\frac{3x}4-\frac38\right)+C\)

7·X6

\(\displaystyle\int x(2x+3)^7\,dx\)

UsesIntegration by parts Power formula

  1. Choose u: \(u=x\), \(dv=(2x+3)^7dx\).Why? LIATE: make u the function that gets simpler when you differentiate it (ln x becomes 1/x, and powers of x drop a degree). dv should be easy to integrate. See the rule
  2. Find v (power formula with \(w=2x+3\), \(dw=2\,dx\)): \(v=\frac12\cdot\frac{(2x+3)^8}8=\frac{(2x+3)^8}{16}\).
  3. Plug in: \(\frac{x(2x+3)^8}{16}-\int\frac{(2x+3)^8}{16}dx\).
  4. Integrate the rest: \(\frac1{16}\cdot\frac12\cdot\frac{(2x+3)^9}9=\frac{(2x+3)^9}{288}\).
  5. Finish: \(\frac{x(2x+3)^8}{16}-\frac{(2x+3)^9}{288}+C\).

Answer\(\frac{x(2x+3)^8}{16}-\frac{(2x+3)^9}{288}+C\)

LESSON 8

Transformation by Trigonometric Formulas

Powers of trig functions. Use the trig powers table in the formula sheet: split off the factor that becomes \(du\), convert the rest with a Pythagorean identity, then use the power formula. Examples start at 3 because examples 1–2 aren't in your PDF.

Slide examples

8·E3

\(\displaystyle\int\sin^72x\cos^32x\,dx\)

UsesTrig powers table Power formula

  1. Spot the form: a product of powers of sin and cos with the same angle \(2x\). The cos power (3) is odd, so split off one \(\cos2x\) for du and use \(u=\sin2x\).Why? The trig powers table tells you what to split off based on which power is odd or even, so the rest can be converted with \(\sin^2+\cos^2=1\) or \(1+\tan^2=\sec^2\). See the rule
  2. Split: \(\cos^32x=\cos^22x\cdot\cos2x\), so the integrand is \(\sin^72x\cos^22x\cdot\cos2x\).Why? Splitting off one factor uses \(a^n=a^{n-1}\cdot a\), e.g. \(\sin^5x=\sin^4x\cdot\sin x\). The single factor becomes part of du, and the even power left over can be converted with an identity. See the rule
  3. Convert: \(\cos^22x=1-\sin^22x\), giving \(\sin^72x(1-\sin^22x)\cos2x\). Now everything except the lone \(\cos2x\) is in sines.Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  4. Name u: \(u=\sin2x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  5. Differentiate: chain rule: \(du=2\cos2x\,dx\), so \(\cos2x\,dx=\frac{du}2\).Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the ruleWhy? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(\frac12\int u^7(1-u^2)\,du=\frac12\int(u^7-u^9)\,du\).Why? Multiply each term in the bracket by the outside factor, adding exponents: \(u^7(1-u^2)=u^7-u^9\). See the rule
  7. Power formula: \(\frac12\left(\frac{u^8}8-\frac{u^{10}}{10}\right)=\frac{u^8}{16}-\frac{u^{10}}{20}\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  8. Put x back: \(\frac1{16}\sin^82x-\frac1{20}\sin^{10}2x+C\).
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}[\dots]=\frac8{16}\sin^72x\cdot2\cos2x-\frac{10}{20}\sin^92x\cdot2\cos2x=\sin^72x\cos2x(1-\sin^22x)=\sin^72x\cos^32x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac1{16}\sin^82x-\frac1{20}\sin^{10}2x+C\)

8·E4

\(\displaystyle\int\sin^5(3x+1)\,dx\)

UsesTrig powers table Pythagorean identity Power formula

  1. Spot the form: an odd power of sine on its own. Split off one sine for du and use \(u=\cos(3x+1)\).Why? The trig powers table tells you what to split off based on which power is odd or even, so the rest can be converted with \(\sin^2+\cos^2=1\) or \(1+\tan^2=\sec^2\). See the rule
  2. Split: \(\sin^5(3x+1)=\sin^4(3x+1)\cdot\sin(3x+1)=[\sin^2(3x+1)]^2\sin(3x+1)\).Why? Splitting off one factor uses \(a^n=a^{n-1}\cdot a\), e.g. \(\sin^5x=\sin^4x\cdot\sin x\). The single factor becomes part of du, and the even power left over can be converted with an identity. See the ruleWhy? A power of a power multiplies the exponents: \((a^m)^n=a^{mn}\). See the rule
  3. Convert: \(\sin^2=1-\cos^2\), so \([\sin^2(3x+1)]^2=[1-\cos^2(3x+1)]^2\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  4. Expand: \([1-\cos^2(3x+1)]^2=1-2\cos^2(3x+1)+\cos^4(3x+1)\).Why? Expanding a square: \((a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2\). See the rule
  5. Name u: \(u=\cos(3x+1)\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  6. Differentiate: \(du=-\sin(3x+1)\cdot3\,dx=-3\sin(3x+1)\,dx\), so \(\sin(3x+1)\,dx=-\frac{du}3\).Why? Chain rule: the derivative of the outside times the derivative of the inside, e.g. \(\frac{d}{dx}\sin3x=3\cos3x\). See the ruleWhy? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  7. Substitute everything: \(-\frac13\int(1-2u^2+u^4)\,du\).
  8. Power formula: \(-\frac13\left(u-\frac23u^3+\frac15u^5\right)=-\frac13u+\frac29u^3-\frac1{15}u^5\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  9. Put x back: \(-\frac13\cos(3x+1)+\frac29\cos^3(3x+1)-\frac1{15}\cos^5(3x+1)+C\).
  10. Check: with \(c=\cos(3x+1)\), \(s=\sin(3x+1)\): the derivative is \(-\frac13(1-2c^2+c^4)\cdot(-3s)=(1-c^2)^2s=s^4\cdot s=s^5\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(-\frac13\cos(3x+1)+\frac29\cos^3(3x+1)-\frac1{15}\cos^5(3x+1)+C\)

8·E5

\(\displaystyle\int\sin^5x\cos^{10}x\,dx\)

UsesTrig powers table Power formula

  1. Spot the form: powers of sin and cos with the same angle. The sin power (5) is odd, so split off one \(\sin x\) for du and use \(u=\cos x\).Why? The trig powers table tells you what to split off based on which power is odd or even, so the rest can be converted with \(\sin^2+\cos^2=1\) or \(1+\tan^2=\sec^2\). See the rule
  2. Split: \(\sin^5x=\sin^4x\cdot\sin x=(\sin^2x)^2\sin x\).Why? Splitting off one factor uses \(a^n=a^{n-1}\cdot a\), e.g. \(\sin^5x=\sin^4x\cdot\sin x\). The single factor becomes part of du, and the even power left over can be converted with an identity. See the rule
  3. Convert: \(\sin^2x=1-\cos^2x\), so the integrand is \((1-\cos^2x)^2\cos^{10}x\sin x\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  4. Expand: \((1-\cos^2x)^2=1-2\cos^2x+\cos^4x\). Multiply by \(\cos^{10}x\): \(\cos^{10}x-2\cos^{12}x+\cos^{14}x\).Why? Expanding a square: \((a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2\). See the ruleWhy? Multiplying powers with the same base adds the exponents: \(a^m\cdot a^n=a^{m+n}\). See the rule
  5. Name u: \(u=\cos x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  6. Differentiate: \(du=-\sin x\,dx\), so \(\sin x\,dx=-du\).Why? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  7. Substitute everything: \(-\int(u^{10}-2u^{12}+u^{14})\,du\).
  8. Power formula: \(-\left(\frac{u^{11}}{11}-\frac{2u^{13}}{13}+\frac{u^{15}}{15}\right)\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  9. Put x back: \(-\frac1{11}\cos^{11}x+\frac2{13}\cos^{13}x-\frac1{15}\cos^{15}x+C\).
  10. Check: with \(c=\cos x\): the derivative is \(-(c^{10}-2c^{12}+c^{14})\cdot(-\sin x)=c^{10}(1-c^2)^2\sin x=\cos^{10}x\sin^5x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(-\frac1{11}\cos^{11}x+\frac2{13}\cos^{13}x-\frac1{15}\cos^{15}x+C\)

8·E6

\(\displaystyle\int\cos^4x\,dx\)

UsesTrig powers table Half-angle

  1. Spot the form: an even power of cos on its own. There is no lone factor to use for du, so lower the power with the half-angle identity.Why? The trig powers table tells you what to split off based on which power is odd or even, so the rest can be converted with \(\sin^2+\cos^2=1\) or \(1+\tan^2=\sec^2\). See the rule
  2. First half-angle: \(\cos^4x=(\cos^2x)^2=\left(\frac{1+\cos2x}2\right)^2\).Why? This comes from rearranging \(\cos2\theta=1-2\sin^2\theta=2\cos^2\theta-1\). See the rule
  3. Expand: \(\left(\frac{1+\cos2x}2\right)^2=\frac14(1+2\cos2x+\cos^22x)\).Why? Expanding a square: \((a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2\). See the rule
  4. Second half-angle: there is still a square, \(\cos^22x\). Use the identity again with angle \(2x\): \(\cos^22x=\frac{1+\cos4x}2\).Why? This comes from rearranging \(\cos2\theta=1-2\sin^2\theta=2\cos^2\theta-1\). See the rule
  5. Collect: \(\frac14\left(1+2\cos2x+\frac12+\frac12\cos4x\right)=\frac14\left(\frac32+2\cos2x+\frac12\cos4x\right)=\frac38+\frac12\cos2x+\frac18\cos4x\).
  6. Integrate the constant: \(\int\frac38\,dx=\frac38x\).Why? \(\int k\,dx=kx\): a constant integrates to that constant times x, because \(\frac{d}{dx}(kx)=k\). See the rule
  7. Integrate the cosines: \(\int\cos kx\,dx=\frac1k\sin kx\), so \(\frac12\cdot\frac12\sin2x=\frac14\sin2x\) and \(\frac18\cdot\frac14\sin4x=\frac1{32}\sin4x\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\sin u=\cos u\), so going backwards \(\int\cos u\,du=\sin u\). No minus sign here. See the rule
  8. Combine: \(\frac38x+\frac14\sin2x+\frac1{32}\sin4x+C\).Why? Sum rule: integrate each term separately, then add the results. See the rule
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}[\dots]=\frac38+\frac12\cos2x+\frac18\cos4x\), which is the collected form of \(\cos^4x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac38x+\frac14\sin2x+\frac1{32}\sin4x+C\)

8·E7

\(\displaystyle\int\tan^5x\sec^4x\,dx\)

UsesTrig powers table Power formula

  1. Spot the form: powers of tan and sec. The sec power (4) is even, so split off \(\sec^2x\) for du and use \(u=\tan x\).Why? The trig powers table tells you what to split off based on which power is odd or even, so the rest can be converted with \(\sin^2+\cos^2=1\) or \(1+\tan^2=\sec^2\). See the rule
  2. Split: \(\sec^4x=\sec^2x\cdot\sec^2x\), so the integrand is \(\tan^5x\sec^2x\cdot\sec^2x\).Why? Splitting off one factor uses \(a^n=a^{n-1}\cdot a\), e.g. \(\sin^5x=\sin^4x\cdot\sin x\). The single factor becomes part of du, and the even power left over can be converted with an identity. See the rule
  3. Convert: change the leftover \(\sec^2x\) to \(1+\tan^2x\): \(\tan^5x(1+\tan^2x)\sec^2x\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  4. Name u: \(u=\tan x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  5. Differentiate: \(du=\sec^2x\,dx\). Exact match.Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\tan u=\sec^2u\), so going backwards \(\int\sec^2u\,du=\tan u\). See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(\int u^5(1+u^2)\,du=\int(u^5+u^7)\,du\).Why? Multiply each term in the bracket by the outside factor, adding exponents: \(u^7(1-u^2)=u^7-u^9\). See the rule
  7. Power formula: \(\frac{u^6}6+\frac{u^8}8\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  8. Put x back: \(\frac16\tan^6x+\frac18\tan^8x+C\).
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}[\dots]=\tan^5x\sec^2x+\tan^7x\sec^2x=\tan^5x\sec^2x(1+\tan^2x)=\tan^5x\sec^4x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac16\tan^6x+\frac18\tan^8x+C\)

8·E8

\(\displaystyle\int\tan^32x\sec^72x\,dx\)

UsesTrig powers table Power formula

  1. Spot the form: powers of tan and sec. The tan power (3) is odd, so split off \(\sec2x\tan2x\) for du and use \(u=\sec2x\).Why? The trig powers table tells you what to split off based on which power is odd or even, so the rest can be converted with \(\sin^2+\cos^2=1\) or \(1+\tan^2=\sec^2\). See the rule
  2. Split: \(\tan^32x\sec^72x=\tan^22x\sec^62x\cdot\sec2x\tan2x\).Why? Splitting off one factor uses \(a^n=a^{n-1}\cdot a\), e.g. \(\sin^5x=\sin^4x\cdot\sin x\). The single factor becomes part of du, and the even power left over can be converted with an identity. See the rule
  3. Convert: \(\tan^22x=\sec^22x-1\): \((\sec^22x-1)\sec^62x\cdot\sec2x\tan2x\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  4. Name u: \(u=\sec2x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  5. Differentiate: \(du=\sec2x\tan2x\cdot2\,dx\), so \(\sec2x\tan2x\,dx=\frac{du}2\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\sec u=\sec u\tan u\), so \(\sec u\tan u\,du\) is exactly what \(d(\sec u)\) needs. See the ruleWhy? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(\frac12\int(u^2-1)u^6\,du=\frac12\int(u^8-u^6)\,du\).Why? Multiply each term in the bracket by the outside factor, adding exponents: \(u^7(1-u^2)=u^7-u^9\). See the rule
  7. Power formula: \(\frac12\left(\frac{u^9}9-\frac{u^7}7\right)=\frac{u^9}{18}-\frac{u^7}{14}\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  8. Put x back: \(\frac1{18}\sec^92x-\frac1{14}\sec^72x+C\).
  9. Check: with \(u=\sec2x\), \(u'=2\sec2x\tan2x\): the derivative is \(\left(\frac{u^8}2-\frac{u^6}2\right)\cdot2\sec2x\tan2x=u^6(u^2-1)\sec2x\tan2x=\sec^72x\tan^32x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac1{18}\sec^92x-\frac1{14}\sec^72x+C\)

8·E9

\(\displaystyle\int\tan^2x\sec x\,dx\)

UsesTrig powers table ∫sec³x ∫sec / ∫csc

  1. Spot the form: an even tan power with an odd sec power. Neither split works, so convert everything to secants.Why? The trig powers table tells you what to split off based on which power is odd or even, so the rest can be converted with \(\sin^2+\cos^2=1\) or \(1+\tan^2=\sec^2\). See the rule
  2. Convert: \(\tan^2x=\sec^2x-1\), so \(\tan^2x\sec x=(\sec^2x-1)\sec x=\sec^3x-\sec x\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  3. Split the integral: \(\int\sec^3x\,dx-\int\sec x\,dx\).Why? Sum rule: integrate each term separately, then add the results. See the rule
  4. Use the sec³ result: \(\int\sec^3x\,dx=\frac12\sec x\tan x+\frac12\ln|\sec x+\tan x|\). This is 7·E4, done with integration by parts.Why? Integration by parts: \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). It is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\), integrate both sides and rearrange. See the rule
  5. Use the sec result: \(\int\sec x\,dx=\ln|\sec x+\tan x|\).Why? Multiply \(\sec u\) by \(\frac{\sec u+\tan u}{\sec u+\tan u}\). The top becomes \(\sec^2u+\sec u\tan u\), which is exactly the derivative of the bottom, so the log rule finishes it. See the rule
  6. Subtract: \(\frac12\sec x\tan x+\frac12\ln|\sec x+\tan x|-\ln|\sec x+\tan x|\).
  7. Simplify: \(\frac12\ln-\ln=-\frac12\ln\), so \(\frac12\sec x\tan x-\frac12\ln|\sec x+\tan x|+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac12\sec x\tan x=\frac12(\sec x\tan^2x+\sec^3x)\) and \(\frac{d}{dx}\left(-\frac12\ln|\sec x+\tan x|\right)=-\frac12\sec x\). With \(\sec^3x=\sec x\tan^2x+\sec x\), the sum is \(\sec x\tan^2x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac12\sec x\tan x-\frac12\ln|\sec x+\tan x|+C\)

8·E10

\(\displaystyle\int\sec^6x\,dx\)

UsesTrig powers table Power formula

  1. Spot the form: an even power of sec on its own. Split off \(\sec^2x\) for du and use \(u=\tan x\).Why? The trig powers table tells you what to split off based on which power is odd or even, so the rest can be converted with \(\sin^2+\cos^2=1\) or \(1+\tan^2=\sec^2\). See the rule
  2. Split: \(\sec^6x=\sec^4x\cdot\sec^2x=(\sec^2x)^2\cdot\sec^2x\).Why? Splitting off one factor uses \(a^n=a^{n-1}\cdot a\), e.g. \(\sin^5x=\sin^4x\cdot\sin x\). The single factor becomes part of du, and the even power left over can be converted with an identity. See the ruleWhy? A power of a power multiplies the exponents: \((a^m)^n=a^{mn}\). See the rule
  3. Convert: \(\sec^2x=1+\tan^2x\), so \((\sec^2x)^2=(1+\tan^2x)^2\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  4. Expand: \((1+\tan^2x)^2=1+2\tan^2x+\tan^4x\).Why? Expanding a square: \((a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2\). See the rule
  5. Name u: \(u=\tan x\), so \(du=\sec^2x\,dx\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the ruleWhy? \(\frac{d}{du}\tan u=\sec^2u\), so going backwards \(\int\sec^2u\,du=\tan u\). See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(\int(1+2u^2+u^4)\,du\).
  7. Power formula: \(u+\frac23u^3+\frac15u^5\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  8. Put x back: \(\tan x+\frac23\tan^3x+\frac15\tan^5x+C\).
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}[\dots]=(1+2\tan^2x+\tan^4x)\sec^2x=(1+\tan^2x)^2\sec^2x=\sec^6x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\tan x+\frac23\tan^3x+\frac15\tan^5x+C\)

8·E11

\(\displaystyle\int\cot^33x\csc^43x\,dx\)

UsesTrig powers table Power formula

  1. Spot the form: powers of cot and csc. The csc power (4) is even, so split off \(\csc^23x\) for du and use \(u=\cot3x\).Why? The trig powers table tells you what to split off based on which power is odd or even, so the rest can be converted with \(\sin^2+\cos^2=1\) or \(1+\tan^2=\sec^2\). See the rule
  2. Split: \(\csc^43x=\csc^23x\cdot\csc^23x\), so the integrand is \(\cot^33x\csc^23x\cdot\csc^23x\).Why? Splitting off one factor uses \(a^n=a^{n-1}\cdot a\), e.g. \(\sin^5x=\sin^4x\cdot\sin x\). The single factor becomes part of du, and the even power left over can be converted with an identity. See the rule
  3. Convert: change the leftover \(\csc^23x\) to \(\cot^23x+1\): \(\cot^33x(\cot^23x+1)\csc^23x\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  4. Name u: \(u=\cot3x\).Why? u-substitution: pick u as the inside piece whose derivative also shows up in the integral (up to a constant). Then everything can be written in terms of u. See the rule
  5. Differentiate: \(du=-\csc^23x\cdot3\,dx\), so \(\csc^23x\,dx=-\frac{du}3\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\cot u=-\csc^2u\), so \(\csc^2u\,du\) is what \(d(\cot u)\) needs, with a minus sign. See the ruleWhy? Solve the du equation for the exact piece in the integral. Any leftover number becomes the correction factor in front. See the rule
  6. Substitute everything: \(-\frac13\int u^3(u^2+1)\,du=-\frac13\int(u^5+u^3)\,du\).Why? Multiply each term in the bracket by the outside factor, adding exponents: \(u^7(1-u^2)=u^7-u^9\). See the rule
  7. Power formula: \(-\frac13\left(\frac{u^6}6+\frac{u^4}4\right)=-\frac{u^6}{18}-\frac{u^4}{12}\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  8. Put x back: \(-\frac1{18}\cot^63x-\frac1{12}\cot^43x+C\).
  9. Check: with \(u=\cot3x\), \(u'=-3\csc^23x\): the derivative is \(\left(-\frac{u^5}3-\frac{u^3}3\right)(-3\csc^23x)=u^3(u^2+1)\csc^23x=\cot^33x\csc^43x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(-\frac1{18}\cot^63x-\frac1{12}\cot^43x+C\)

Exercises · full solutions

8·X1

\(\displaystyle\int\sin^2x\cos^2x\,dx\)

UsesDouble angle Half-angle

  1. Pick the case: both powers are even, so use identities to lower them.Why? The trig powers table tells you what to split off based on which power is odd or even, so the rest can be converted with \(\sin^2+\cos^2=1\) or \(1+\tan^2=\sec^2\). See the rule
  2. Double angle: \(\sin x\cos x=\frac12\sin2x\), so \(\sin^2x\cos^2x=\frac14\sin^22x\).Why? This comes from \(\sin(A+B)=\sin A\cos B+\cos A\sin B\) with \(A=B=\theta\). See the rule
  3. Half-angle: \(\sin^22x=\frac12(1-\cos4x)\), so the integrand is \(\frac18(1-\cos4x)\).Why? This comes from rearranging \(\cos2\theta=1-2\sin^2\theta=2\cos^2\theta-1\). See the rule
  4. Integrate: \(\frac18\left(x-\frac14\sin4x\right)=\frac x8-\frac1{32}\sin4x+C\).

Answer\(\frac x8-\frac1{32}\sin4x+C\)

8·X2

\(\displaystyle\int\sin^52x\cos^{15}2x\,dx\)

UsesTrig powers table Power formula

  1. Pick the case: the sine power 5 is odd, so split off one \(\sin2x\) and use \(u=\cos2x\).Why? The trig powers table tells you what to split off based on which power is odd or even, so the rest can be converted with \(\sin^2+\cos^2=1\) or \(1+\tan^2=\sec^2\). See the rule
  2. Split and convert: \(\sin^42x\cos^{15}2x\sin2x=(1-\cos^22x)^2\cos^{15}2x\sin2x\).Why? Splitting off one factor uses \(a^n=a^{n-1}\cdot a\), e.g. \(\sin^5x=\sin^4x\cdot\sin x\). The single factor becomes part of du, and the even power left over can be converted with an identity. See the rule
  3. Expand: \((1-2\cos^22x+\cos^42x)\cos^{15}2x=\cos^{15}2x-2\cos^{17}2x+\cos^{19}2x\).Why? Expanding means multiplying every term in one bracket by every term in the other, e.g. \((a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2\). See the rule
  4. Substitute: \(u=\cos2x\), \(du=-2\sin2x\,dx\), so \(-\frac12\int(u^{15}-2u^{17}+u^{19})\,du\).
  5. Integrate: \(-\frac12\left(\frac{u^{16}}{16}-\frac{u^{18}}9+\frac{u^{20}}{20}\right)\).
  6. Put x back: \(-\frac1{32}\cos^{16}2x+\frac1{18}\cos^{18}2x-\frac1{40}\cos^{20}2x+C\).

Answer\(-\frac1{32}\cos^{16}2x+\frac1{18}\cos^{18}2x-\frac1{40}\cos^{20}2x+C\)

8·X3

\(\displaystyle\int\tan^53x\,dx\)

UsesTrig powers table Pythagorean identity ∫tan / ∫cot

  1. Peel off tan²: \(\tan^53x=\tan^33x\tan^23x=\tan^33x(\sec^23x-1)=\tan^33x\sec^23x-\tan^33x\).Why? Peeling off \(\tan^2\) (or \(\cot^2\)) and replacing it with \(\sec^2-1\) (or \(\csc^2-1\)) creates a \(\sec^2\) factor, which is exactly the du of \(\tan\). See the rule
  2. Peel again on \(\tan^33x\): \(\tan3x(\sec^23x-1)=\tan3x\sec^23x-\tan3x\).Why? Peeling off \(\tan^2\) (or \(\cot^2\)) and replacing it with \(\sec^2-1\) (or \(\csc^2-1\)) creates a \(\sec^2\) factor, which is exactly the du of \(\tan\). See the rule
  3. So the integrand is \(\tan^33x\sec^23x-\tan3x\sec^23x+\tan3x\).
  4. First two terms: \(u=\tan3x\), \(du=3\sec^23x\,dx\), giving \(\frac13\cdot\frac{u^4}4-\frac13\cdot\frac{u^2}2\).
  5. Last term: \(\int\tan3x\,dx=\frac13\ln|\sec3x|\).
  6. Combine: \(\frac1{12}\tan^43x-\frac16\tan^23x+\frac13\ln|\sec3x|+C\).

Answer\(\frac1{12}\tan^43x-\frac16\tan^23x+\frac13\ln|\sec3x|+C\)

8·X4

\(\displaystyle\int\sin7x\sin4x\,dx\)

UsesProduct-to-sum

  1. Pick the formula: \(\sin A\sin B=\frac12[\cos(A-B)-\cos(A+B)]\), with \(A=7x\), \(B=4x\).Why? Product-to-sum formulas come from adding or subtracting the angle-sum formulas, e.g. \(\sin(A+B)+\sin(A-B)=2\sin A\cos B\). See the rule
  2. Plug in: \(\frac12[\cos3x-\cos11x]\).
  3. Integrate each: \(\frac13\sin3x\) and \(\frac1{11}\sin11x\).
  4. Multiply by ½: \(\frac16\sin3x-\frac1{22}\sin11x+C\).

Answer\(\frac16\sin3x-\frac1{22}\sin11x+C\)

8·X5

\(\displaystyle\int\cot^4x\,dx\)

UsesTrig powers table Pythagorean identity ∫sec² / ∫csc²

  1. Peel off cot²: \(\cot^4x=\cot^2x\cdot\cot^2x=\cot^2x(\csc^2x-1)=\cot^2x\csc^2x-\cot^2x\).Why? Peeling off \(\tan^2\) (or \(\cot^2\)) and replacing it with \(\sec^2-1\) (or \(\csc^2-1\)) creates a \(\sec^2\) factor, which is exactly the du of \(\tan\). See the rule
  2. Convert again: \(\cot^2x=\csc^2x-1\), so the integrand is \(\cot^2x\csc^2x-\csc^2x+1\).
  3. First term: \(u=\cot x\), \(du=-\csc^2x\,dx\), giving \(-\int u^2du=-\frac{\cot^3x}3\).
  4. Other terms: \(-\int\csc^2x\,dx=\cot x\), and \(\int dx=x\).
  5. Combine: \(-\frac13\cot^3x+\cot x+x+C\).

Answer\(-\frac13\cot^3x+\cot x+x+C\)

8·X6

\(\displaystyle\int\cot^5x\csc^7x\,dx\)

UsesTrig powers table Power formula

  1. Pick the case: the cotangent power 5 is odd, so split off \(\csc x\cot x\) and use \(u=\csc x\).Why? The trig powers table tells you what to split off based on which power is odd or even, so the rest can be converted with \(\sin^2+\cos^2=1\) or \(1+\tan^2=\sec^2\). See the rule
  2. Split: \(\cot^4x\csc^6x\cdot\csc x\cot x\).Why? Splitting off one factor uses \(a^n=a^{n-1}\cdot a\), e.g. \(\sin^5x=\sin^4x\cdot\sin x\). The single factor becomes part of du, and the even power left over can be converted with an identity. See the rule
  3. Convert: \(\cot^4x=(\csc^2x-1)^2=\csc^4x-2\csc^2x+1\).
  4. Substitute: \(u=\csc x\), \(du=-\csc x\cot x\,dx\), so \(-\int(u^4-2u^2+1)u^6\,du=-\int(u^{10}-2u^8+u^6)\,du\).
  5. Integrate and put x back: \(-\frac1{11}\csc^{11}x+\frac29\csc^9x-\frac17\csc^7x+C\).

Answer\(-\frac1{11}\csc^{11}x+\frac29\csc^9x-\frac17\csc^7x+C\)

LESSON 9

Integration by Substitution

Algebraic substitution gets rid of radicals: \(x=z^n\) (n = LCD of the exponents) or \(ax+b=z^n\). Trig substitution handles \(a^2\pm u^2\) and \(u^2-a^2\) using the table in the formula sheet. After a trig substitution, use a reference triangle to convert θ back to x.

  1. Choose the substitution and write x and dx in terms of the new variable.
  2. Simplify the integrand. If you get an improper fraction in z, use long division.
  3. Integrate, then substitute back. For trig substitution, read sinθ, cosθ, tanθ off the triangle.

Slide examples · algebraic

9·E1

\(\displaystyle\int\sin\sqrt x\,dx\)

UsesAlgebraic substitution Integration by parts

  1. Spot the form: a root inside sin. No formula fits \(\sin\sqrt x\) directly, so remove the root with an algebraic substitution first.Why? Algebraic substitution: set the root (or the expression under it) equal to a new variable so the radical disappears, then rewrite x and dx in terms of it. See the rule
  2. Choose the substitution: let \(x=z^2\), so \(\sqrt x=z\).
  3. Find dx: differentiate \(x=z^2\): \(dx=2z\,dz\).
  4. Substitute everything: \(\int\sin z\cdot2z\,dz=2\int z\sin z\,dz\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  5. Choose u and dv: this is a product of z and a trig function, so use integration by parts: \(u=z\), \(dv=\sin z\,dz\).Why? Integration by parts: \(\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du\). It is the product rule backwards: \((uv)'=u'v+uv'\), integrate both sides and rearrange. See the ruleWhy? LIATE: make u the function that gets simpler when you differentiate it (ln x becomes 1/x, and powers of x drop a degree). dv should be easy to integrate. See the rule
  6. Find du and v: \(du=dz\), \(v=-\cos z\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\cos u=-\sin u\), so the antiderivative of \(\sin u\) is \(-\cos u\). Forgetting that minus is the most common slip. See the rule
  7. Plug in: \(\int z\sin z\,dz=-z\cos z-\int(-\cos z)\,dz=-z\cos z+\int\cos z\,dz=-z\cos z+\sin z\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\sin u=\cos u\), so going backwards \(\int\cos u\,du=\sin u\). No minus sign here. See the rule
  8. Multiply by 2: \(2(-z\cos z+\sin z)=-2z\cos z+2\sin z\).
  9. Put x back: \(z=\sqrt x\): \(-2\sqrt x\cos\sqrt x+2\sin\sqrt x+C\).
  10. Check: in z: \(\frac{d}{dz}(-2z\cos z+2\sin z)=-2\cos z+2z\sin z+2\cos z=2z\sin z\), which is the z-integrand ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(-2\sqrt x\cos\sqrt x+2\sin\sqrt x+C\)

9·E2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{1+\sqrt x}\)

UsesAlgebraic substitution Log rule

  1. Spot the form: a root on the bottom with no derivative to match. Remove the root with an algebraic substitution.Why? Algebraic substitution: set the root (or the expression under it) equal to a new variable so the radical disappears, then rewrite x and dx in terms of it. See the rule
  2. Choose the substitution: let \(x=z^2\), so \(\sqrt x=z\).
  3. Find dx: \(dx=2z\,dz\).
  4. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{2z\,dz}{1+z}=2\int\frac{z}{z+1}dz\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  5. Divide: the top and bottom have the same degree, so divide: \(\frac z{z+1}=\frac{(z+1)-1}{z+1}=1-\frac1{z+1}\).Why? When the top's degree is at least the bottom's, divide first, like \(\frac72=3+\frac12\): quotient + remainder ÷ divisor. See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(2\int\left(1-\frac1{z+1}\right)dz=2z-2\ln|z+1|\).Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  7. Put x back: \(z=\sqrt x\): \(2\sqrt x-2\ln(\sqrt x+1)+C\). No bars needed, since \(\sqrt x+1\) is always positive.Why? ln only accepts positive inputs. The bars in \(\ln|u|\) keep the answer valid when u is negative. If u can never be negative (like \(x^2+5\)), you can drop them. See the rule
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\left[2\sqrt x-2\ln(\sqrt x+1)\right]=\frac1{\sqrt x}-\frac{2}{\sqrt x+1}\cdot\frac1{2\sqrt x}=\frac1{\sqrt x}\left(1-\frac1{\sqrt x+1}\right)=\frac1{\sqrt x}\cdot\frac{\sqrt x}{\sqrt x+1}=\frac1{1+\sqrt x}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(2\sqrt x-2\ln(\sqrt x+1)+C\)

9·E3

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(5y+4)\,dy}{\sqrt{5y-1}}\)

UsesAlgebraic substitution Power formula

  1. Spot the form: a root of a linear expression on the bottom, with another linear expression on top. Remove the root with an algebraic substitution.Why? Algebraic substitution: set the root (or the expression under it) equal to a new variable so the radical disappears, then rewrite x and dx in terms of it. See the rule
  2. Choose the substitution: let \(5y-1=z^2\), so \(\sqrt{5y-1}=z\).
  3. Solve for y: \(5y=z^2+1\), so \(y=\frac{z^2+1}5\).
  4. Find dy: \(dy=\frac{2z}5dz\).
  5. Rewrite the top: \(5y+4=(z^2+1)+4=z^2+5\).
  6. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{z^2+5}{z}\cdot\frac{2z}5dz\). The z's cancel, leaving \(\frac25\int(z^2+5)\,dz\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  7. Integrate: \(\frac25\left(\frac{z^3}3+5z\right)=\frac2{15}z^3+2z\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  8. Put y back: \(z=\sqrt{5y-1}\), so \(z^3=(5y-1)^{3/2}\): \(\frac2{15}(5y-1)^{3/2}+2\sqrt{5y-1}+C\).Why? A root is a fractional power: \(\sqrt[n]{a^m}=a^{m/n}\). A square root is the power ½. See the rule
  9. Check: \(\frac{d}{dy}[\dots]=\frac2{15}\cdot\frac32(5y-1)^{1/2}\cdot5+2\cdot\frac{5}{2\sqrt{5y-1}}=\sqrt{5y-1}+\frac5{\sqrt{5y-1}}=\frac{(5y-1)+5}{\sqrt{5y-1}}=\frac{5y+4}{\sqrt{5y-1}}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac2{15}(5y-1)^{3/2}+2\sqrt{5y-1}+C\)

9·E4

\(\displaystyle\int\sqrt{e^x-9}\,dx\)

UsesAlgebraic substitution Arctan form

  1. Spot the form: a root of \(e^x-9\) with nothing else to use for du. Remove the root with an algebraic substitution.Why? Algebraic substitution: set the root (or the expression under it) equal to a new variable so the radical disappears, then rewrite x and dx in terms of it. See the rule
  2. Choose the substitution: let \(z^2=e^x-9\), so \(\sqrt{e^x-9}=z\) and \(e^x=z^2+9\).
  3. Find dx: differentiate both sides of \(e^x=z^2+9\): \(e^xdx=2z\,dz\), so \(dx=\frac{2z\,dz}{e^x}=\frac{2z\,dz}{z^2+9}\).
  4. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int z\cdot\frac{2z\,dz}{z^2+9}=2\int\frac{z^2}{z^2+9}dz\).Why? After substituting, the integral must contain only u and du, with no x left over. If an x is still there, the substitution is not finished. See the rule
  5. Divide: same degree on top and bottom: \(\frac{z^2}{z^2+9}=\frac{(z^2+9)-9}{z^2+9}=1-\frac9{z^2+9}\).Why? When the top's degree is at least the bottom's, divide first, like \(\frac72=3+\frac12\): quotient + remainder ÷ divisor. See the rule
  6. Integrate the 1: \(2\int dz=2z\).
  7. Integrate the arctan piece: \(2\cdot9\int\frac{dz}{z^2+3^2}=18\cdot\frac13\arctan\frac z3=6\arctan\frac z3\).Why? \(\int\frac{du}{a^2+u^2}=\frac1a\arctan\frac ua\). It comes from \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\). Factoring \(a^2\) out of the bottom is what puts the \(\frac1a\) in front. See the rule
  8. Combine: \(2z-6\arctan\frac z3+C\).
  9. Put x back: \(2\sqrt{e^x-9}-6\arctan\frac{\sqrt{e^x-9}}3+C\).
  10. Check: in z: \(\frac{d}{dz}\left(2z-6\arctan\frac z3\right)=2-\frac{6\cdot\frac13}{1+\frac{z^2}9}=2-\frac{18}{z^2+9}=\frac{2z^2}{z^2+9}\), which is the z-integrand ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Slide typo: the note says "divide \(z^2\) by \(z^2+1\)". It should be \(z^2+9\). The final answer on the slide is correct.

Answer\(2\sqrt{e^x-9}-6\arctan\frac{\sqrt{e^x-9}}3+C\)

9·E5

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt x+\sqrt[3]x}\)

UsesAlgebraic substitution Log rule

  1. Spot the form: two different roots of x on the bottom. Pick one substitution that removes both roots at once.Why? Algebraic substitution: set the root (or the expression under it) equal to a new variable so the radical disappears, then rewrite x and dx in terms of it. See the rule
  2. Pick n: the roots are \(x^{1/2}\) and \(x^{1/3}\). The LCD of 2 and 3 is 6, so let \(x=z^6\).Why? Using the LCD of the root indices makes every root a whole power of z at the same time: \(\sqrt{z^6}=z^3\) and \(\sqrt[3]{z^6}=z^2\). See the rule
  3. Convert the roots: \(\sqrt x=(z^6)^{1/2}=z^3\) and \(\sqrt[3]x=(z^6)^{1/3}=z^2\).Why? A power of a power multiplies the exponents: \((a^m)^n=a^{mn}\). See the rule
  4. Find dx: \(dx=6z^5dz\).
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{6z^5dz}{z^3+z^2}\).
  6. Factor and cancel: \(z^3+z^2=z^2(z+1)\), so \(\frac{6z^5}{z^2(z+1)}=\frac{6z^3}{z+1}\).Why? Dividing powers with the same base subtracts the exponents: \(\frac{a^m}{a^n}=a^{m-n}\). See the rule
  7. Divide: \(\frac{z^3}{z+1}=z^2-z+1-\frac1{z+1}\) (top degree 3 is bigger than bottom degree 1).Why? When the top's degree is at least the bottom's, divide first, like \(\frac72=3+\frac12\): quotient + remainder ÷ divisor. See the ruleWhy? The bottom is \(z+1=z-(-1)\), so synthetic division with \(-1\) works: coefficients \(1,0,0,0\) give \(1,-1,1\) with remainder \(-1\). See the rule
  8. Integrate: \(6\left(\frac{z^3}3-\frac{z^2}2+z-\ln|z+1|\right)=2z^3-3z^2+6z-6\ln|z+1|\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the ruleWhy? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  9. Put x back: \(z=\sqrt[6]x\), so \(z^3=\sqrt x\) and \(z^2=\sqrt[3]x\): \(2\sqrt x-3\sqrt[3]x+6\sqrt[6]x-6\ln(\sqrt[6]x+1)+C\).
  10. Check: in z: \(\frac{d}{dz}[\dots]=6z^2-6z+6-\frac6{z+1}=6\cdot\frac{(z^2-z+1)(z+1)-1}{z+1}=\frac{6z^3}{z+1}\), which is the z-integrand ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(2\sqrt x-3\sqrt[3]x+6\sqrt[6]x-6\ln(\sqrt[6]x+1)+C\)

Slide examples · trigonometric

9·T1

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{x^3\,dx}{\sqrt{4-x^2}}\)

UsesTrig substitution table Trig powers table

  1. Spot the form: a root of (constant − \(x^2\)). That is \(\sqrt{a^2-u^2}\), so use the trig substitution \(x=a\sin\theta\).Why? Each substitution turns the expression under the root into a perfect square via a Pythagorean identity, e.g. \(4-4\sin^2\theta=4\cos^2\theta\). See the rule
  2. Read off a: \(4-x^2=2^2-x^2\), so \(a=2\) and \(x=2\sin\theta\).
  3. Find dx: \(dx=2\cos\theta\,d\theta\).
  4. Simplify the root: \(\sqrt{4-x^2}=\sqrt{4-4\sin^2\theta}=\sqrt{4(1-\sin^2\theta)}=\sqrt{4\cos^2\theta}=2\cos\theta\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the ruleWhy? \(\sqrt{k^2A^2}=kA\): take the root of each factor. (We assume \(\theta\) is in the range where that trig value is positive.) See the rule
  5. Convert the top: \(x^3=(2\sin\theta)^3=8\sin^3\theta\).
  6. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{8\sin^3\theta\cdot2\cos\theta\,d\theta}{2\cos\theta}=8\int\sin^3\theta\,d\theta\). The \(2\cos\theta\) cancels.
  7. Odd power of sine: split off one sine: \(\sin^3\theta=\sin^2\theta\sin\theta=(1-\cos^2\theta)\sin\theta\).Why? The trig powers table tells you what to split off based on which power is odd or even, so the rest can be converted with \(\sin^2+\cos^2=1\) or \(1+\tan^2=\sec^2\). See the ruleWhy? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  8. Substitute again: \(w=\cos\theta\), \(dw=-\sin\theta\,d\theta\): \(8\int(1-w^2)(-dw)=-8\left(w-\frac{w^3}3\right)=-8\cos\theta+\frac83\cos^3\theta\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  9. Draw the triangle: \(\sin\theta=\frac x2\): opposite \(x\), hypotenuse 2, so adjacent \(=\sqrt{4-x^2}\) and \(\cos\theta=\frac{\sqrt{4-x^2}}2\).Why? Reference triangle: the substitution gives two sides (e.g. \(x=2\sin\theta\) means \(\sin\theta=\frac x2=\frac{\text{opp}}{\text{hyp}}\)). Find the third side with Pythagoras, then read off any trig value with SOH-CAH-TOA. See the rule
  10. Put x back: \(-8\cdot\frac{\sqrt{4-x^2}}2+\frac83\cdot\frac{(4-x^2)^{3/2}}8=-4\sqrt{4-x^2}+\frac13(4-x^2)^{3/2}+C\).
  11. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}[\dots]=\frac{4x}{\sqrt{4-x^2}}-x\sqrt{4-x^2}=\frac{4x-x(4-x^2)}{\sqrt{4-x^2}}=\frac{x^3}{\sqrt{4-x^2}}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(-4\sqrt{4-x^2}+\frac13(4-x^2)^{3/2}+C\)

9·T2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{(4x^2+9)^2}\)

UsesTrig substitution table Half-angle Arctan form

  1. Spot the form: a power of (\(u^2\) + constant) on the bottom. That is \(u^2+a^2\), so use the trig substitution \(u=a\tan\theta\).Why? Each substitution turns the expression under the root into a perfect square via a Pythagorean identity, e.g. \(4-4\sin^2\theta=4\cos^2\theta\). See the rule
  2. Read off u and a: \(4x^2+9=(2x)^2+3^2\), so \(u=2x\), \(a=3\), and \(2x=3\tan\theta\).Why? \((ab)^2=a^2b^2\), so \(4x^2=2^2x^2=(2x)^2\). Spotting the square tells you what u is. See the rule
  3. Find dx: \(x=\frac32\tan\theta\), so \(dx=\frac32\sec^2\theta\,d\theta\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\tan u=\sec^2u\), so going backwards \(\int\sec^2u\,du=\tan u\). See the rule
  4. Simplify the bottom: \(4x^2+9=9\tan^2\theta+9=9(\tan^2\theta+1)=9\sec^2\theta\), so \((4x^2+9)^2=81\sec^4\theta\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{\frac32\sec^2\theta\,d\theta}{81\sec^4\theta}=\frac3{162}\int\frac{d\theta}{\sec^2\theta}=\frac1{54}\int\cos^2\theta\,d\theta\).Why? \(\frac1{\sec^2\theta}=\cos^2\theta\), because sec and cos are reciprocals. See the rule
  6. Half-angle: \(\cos^2\theta=\frac12(1+\cos2\theta)\), so \(\frac1{54}\cdot\frac12\int(1+\cos2\theta)\,d\theta=\frac1{108}\left(\theta+\frac12\sin2\theta\right)\).Why? This comes from rearranging \(\cos2\theta=1-2\sin^2\theta=2\cos^2\theta-1\). See the rule
