  7. Double angle: \(\frac12\sin2\theta=\sin\theta\cos\theta\), so the result is \(\frac1{108}(\theta+\sin\theta\cos\theta)\).Why? This comes from \(\sin(A+B)=\sin A\cos B+\cos A\sin B\) with \(A=B=\theta\). See the rule
  8. Draw the triangle: \(\tan\theta=\frac{2x}3\): opposite \(2x\), adjacent 3, hypotenuse \(\sqrt{4x^2+9}\). So \(\theta=\arctan\frac{2x}3\), \(\sin\theta=\frac{2x}{\sqrt{4x^2+9}}\), \(\cos\theta=\frac3{\sqrt{4x^2+9}}\).Why? Reference triangle: the substitution gives two sides (e.g. \(x=2\sin\theta\) means \(\sin\theta=\frac x2=\frac{\text{opp}}{\text{hyp}}\)). Find the third side with Pythagoras, then read off any trig value with SOH-CAH-TOA. See the rule
  9. Put x back: \(\sin\theta\cos\theta=\frac{6x}{4x^2+9}\), so \(\frac1{108}\arctan\frac{2x}3+\frac{6x}{108(4x^2+9)}=\frac1{108}\arctan\frac{2x}3+\frac{x}{18(4x^2+9)}+C\).
  10. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac1{108}\arctan\frac{2x}3=\frac1{54}\cdot\frac{3}{4x^2+9}\cdot\frac12\cdot2=\frac1{18(4x^2+9)}\) and \(\frac{d}{dx}\frac x{18(4x^2+9)}=\frac{9-4x^2}{18(4x^2+9)^2}\). Adding over \(18(4x^2+9)^2\): \(\frac{(4x^2+9)+(9-4x^2)}{18(4x^2+9)^2}=\frac{18}{18(4x^2+9)^2}=\frac1{(4x^2+9)^2}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac1{108}\arctan\frac{2x}3+\dfrac{x}{18(4x^2+9)}+C\)

9·T3

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{\sqrt{9x^2-16}}{x}\,dx\)

UsesTrig substitution table Pythagorean identity Arcsec form

  1. Spot the form: a root of (\(u^2\) − constant). That is \(\sqrt{u^2-a^2}\), so use the trig substitution \(u=a\sec\theta\).Why? Each substitution turns the expression under the root into a perfect square via a Pythagorean identity, e.g. \(4-4\sin^2\theta=4\cos^2\theta\). See the rule
  2. Read off u and a: \(9x^2-16=(3x)^2-4^2\), so \(u=3x\), \(a=4\), and \(3x=4\sec\theta\).Why? \((ab)^2=a^2b^2\), so \(4x^2=2^2x^2=(2x)^2\). Spotting the square tells you what u is. See the rule
  3. Find dx: \(x=\frac43\sec\theta\), so \(dx=\frac43\sec\theta\tan\theta\,d\theta\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\sec u=\sec u\tan u\), so \(\sec u\tan u\,du\) is exactly what \(d(\sec u)\) needs. See the rule
  4. Simplify the root: \(\sqrt{9x^2-16}=\sqrt{16\sec^2\theta-16}=\sqrt{16(\sec^2\theta-1)}=\sqrt{16\tan^2\theta}=4\tan\theta\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the ruleWhy? \(\sqrt{k^2A^2}=kA\): take the root of each factor. (We assume \(\theta\) is in the range where that trig value is positive.) See the rule
  5. Substitute everything: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{4\tan\theta}{\frac43\sec\theta}\cdot\frac43\sec\theta\tan\theta\,d\theta=4\int\tan^2\theta\,d\theta\). The \(\frac43\sec\theta\) cancels.
  6. Pythagorean identity: \(\tan^2\theta=\sec^2\theta-1\), so \(4\int(\sec^2\theta-1)\,d\theta\).Why? \(\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1\) is Pythagoras on the unit circle (legs sin and cos, hypotenuse 1). Divide it by \(\cos^2\theta\) to get \(\tan^2\theta+1=\sec^2\theta\), or by \(\sin^2\theta\) to get \(1+\cot^2\theta=\csc^2\theta\). See the rule
  7. Integrate: \(4(\tan\theta-\theta)=4\tan\theta-4\theta\).Why? \(\frac{d}{du}\tan u=\sec^2u\), so going backwards \(\int\sec^2u\,du=\tan u\). See the rule
  8. Draw the triangle: \(\sec\theta=\frac{3x}4\): hypotenuse \(3x\), adjacent 4, so opposite \(=\sqrt{9x^2-16}\) and \(\tan\theta=\frac{\sqrt{9x^2-16}}4\). Also \(\theta=\operatorname{arcsec}\frac{3x}4\).Why? Reference triangle: the substitution gives two sides (e.g. \(x=2\sin\theta\) means \(\sin\theta=\frac x2=\frac{\text{opp}}{\text{hyp}}\)). Find the third side with Pythagoras, then read off any trig value with SOH-CAH-TOA. See the rule
  9. Put x back: \(4\tan\theta=\sqrt{9x^2-16}\), so \(\sqrt{9x^2-16}-4\operatorname{arcsec}\frac{3x}4+C\).
  10. Check: for \(x>0\): \(\frac{d}{dx}\sqrt{9x^2-16}=\frac{9x}{\sqrt{9x^2-16}}\) and \(\frac{d}{dx}4\operatorname{arcsec}\frac{3x}4=\frac{4\cdot\frac34}{\frac{3x}4\sqrt{\frac{9x^2}{16}-1}}=\frac{16}{x\sqrt{9x^2-16}}\). The difference is \(\frac{9x^2-16}{x\sqrt{9x^2-16}}=\frac{\sqrt{9x^2-16}}x\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\sqrt{9x^2-16}-4\operatorname{arcsec}\frac{3x}4+C\)

Slide examples · Weierstrass (marked "not included")

9·W1

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{1+\sin x+\cos x}\)

UsesWeierstrass Log rule

  1. Spot the form: a fraction built only from \(\sin x\) and \(\cos x\) (no powers, no products). Use the Weierstrass substitution \(z=\tan\frac x2\).Why? The Weierstrass formulas come from the double-angle identities written in terms of \(\tan\frac x2\). They turn sin and cos into plain fractions in z. See the rule
  2. Write the substitutions: \(\sin x=\frac{2z}{1+z^2}\), \(\cos x=\frac{1-z^2}{1+z^2}\), \(dx=\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\).
  3. Simplify the bottom: write 1 as \(\frac{1+z^2}{1+z^2}\): \(1+\frac{2z}{1+z^2}+\frac{1-z^2}{1+z^2}=\frac{(1+z^2)+2z+(1-z^2)}{1+z^2}=\frac{2z+2}{1+z^2}\).Why? To add a whole number to a fraction, write it over the same denominator, e.g. \(1=\frac{1+z^2}{1+z^2}\). Then add the tops: \(\frac ac+\frac bc=\frac{a+b}c\). See the rule
  4. Assemble: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\div\frac{2z+2}{1+z^2}\).
  5. Divide by multiplying by the flip: \(\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\cdot\frac{1+z^2}{2(z+1)}=\frac{dz}{z+1}\). The 2s cancel and the \(1+z^2\) cancels.Why? Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip: \(a\div\frac bc=a\cdot\frac cb\). See the rule
  6. Log rule: \(\int\frac{dz}{z+1}=\ln|z+1|+C\).Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  7. Put x back: \(\ln\left|\tan\frac x2+1\right|+C\).
  8. Check: in z: \(\frac{d}{dz}\ln|z+1|=\frac1{z+1}\), which is the z-integrand ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\ln\left|\tan\frac x2+1\right|+C\)

9·W2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{3+\cos2x}\)

UsesWeierstrass Arctan form

  1. Spot the form: a fraction built only from \(\cos2x\). Use Weierstrass, but with half of the angle \(2x\).Why? The Weierstrass formulas come from the double-angle identities written in terms of \(\tan\frac x2\). They turn sin and cos into plain fractions in z. See the rule
  2. Choose z: the angle is \(2x\), so \(z=\tan\frac{2x}2=\tan x\). Then \(\cos2x=\frac{1-z^2}{1+z^2}\).
  3. Find dx: \(x=\arctan z\), so \(dx=\frac{dz}{1+z^2}\). (Not \(\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\). That's only for \(z=\tan\frac x2\).)Why? You need these derivatives for du: \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\) and \((\arcsin x)'=\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\). See the rule
  4. Simplify the bottom: write 3 as \(\frac{3(1+z^2)}{1+z^2}\): \(3+\frac{1-z^2}{1+z^2}=\frac{3+3z^2+1-z^2}{1+z^2}=\frac{4+2z^2}{1+z^2}\).Why? To add a whole number to a fraction, write it over the same denominator, e.g. \(1=\frac{1+z^2}{1+z^2}\). Then add the tops: \(\frac ac+\frac bc=\frac{a+b}c\). See the rule
  5. Divide by multiplying by the flip: \(\frac{dz}{1+z^2}\cdot\frac{1+z^2}{4+2z^2}=\frac{dz}{2(z^2+2)}\).Why? Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip: \(a\div\frac bc=a\cdot\frac cb\). See the rule
  6. Match the arctan form: \(\frac12\int\frac{dz}{z^2+(\sqrt2)^2}\), so \(a=\sqrt2\).Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  7. Integrate: \(\frac12\cdot\frac1{\sqrt2}\arctan\frac z{\sqrt2}=\frac1{2\sqrt2}\arctan\frac z{\sqrt2}+C\).Why? \(\int\frac{du}{a^2+u^2}=\frac1a\arctan\frac ua\). It comes from \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\). Factoring \(a^2\) out of the bottom is what puts the \(\frac1a\) in front. See the rule
  8. Put x back: \(\frac1{2\sqrt2}\arctan\frac{\tan x}{\sqrt2}+C\).
  9. Check: in z: \(\frac{d}{dz}\frac1{2\sqrt2}\arctan\frac z{\sqrt2}=\frac1{2\sqrt2}\cdot\frac{1/\sqrt2}{1+\frac{z^2}2}=\frac1{2(z^2+2)}\), which is the z-integrand ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Slide mistake: the slide uses \(dx=\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\), which is only true for \(z=\tan\frac x2\). Here \(z=\tan x\), so its answer \(\frac{\sqrt2}2\arctan\left(\frac{\sqrt2}2\tan x\right)\) is twice too big. Differentiating confirms the corrected answer.

Answer\(\dfrac{1}{2\sqrt2}\arctan\dfrac{\tan x}{\sqrt2}+C\)

Formula practice · Weierstrass (not included)

9·P1

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{1+\cos x}\)

UsesWeierstrass

  1. Spot the form: a fraction built only from \(\cos x\). The Weierstrass substitution \(z=\tan\frac x2\) turns any such fraction into an ordinary fraction in z.
  2. Write the substitutions: \(\cos x=\frac{1-z^2}{1+z^2}\) and \(dx=\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\).Why? The Weierstrass formulas come from the double-angle identities written in terms of \(\tan\frac x2\). They turn sin and cos into plain fractions in z. See the rule
  3. Simplify the bottom: write 1 as \(\frac{1+z^2}{1+z^2}\): \(1+\frac{1-z^2}{1+z^2}=\frac{(1+z^2)+(1-z^2)}{1+z^2}=\frac2{1+z^2}\).Why? To add a whole number to a fraction, write it over the same denominator, e.g. \(1=\frac{1+z^2}{1+z^2}\). Then add the tops: \(\frac ac+\frac bc=\frac{a+b}c\). See the rule
  4. Assemble: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{1+\cos x}=\int\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\div\frac2{1+z^2}\).
  5. Divide by multiplying by the flip: \(\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\cdot\frac{1+z^2}2=dz\). Everything cancels.Why? Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip: \(a\div\frac bc=a\cdot\frac cb\). See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(\int dz=z+C\).
  7. Put x back: \(\tan\frac x2+C\).
  8. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\tan\frac x2=\frac12\sec^2\frac x2=\frac1{2\cos^2\frac x2}\). By the half-angle identity, \(2\cos^2\frac x2=1+\cos x\), so this equals \(\frac1{1+\cos x}\) ✓Why? This comes from rearranging \(\cos2\theta=1-2\sin^2\theta=2\cos^2\theta-1\). See the rule

Answer\(\tan\frac x2+C\)

9·P2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{2+\cos x}\)

UsesWeierstrass Arctan form

  1. Spot the form: a fraction built only from \(\cos x\): Weierstrass, \(z=\tan\frac x2\).
  2. Write the substitutions: \(\cos x=\frac{1-z^2}{1+z^2}\) and \(dx=\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\).Why? The Weierstrass formulas come from the double-angle identities written in terms of \(\tan\frac x2\). They turn sin and cos into plain fractions in z. See the rule
  3. Simplify the bottom: write 2 as \(\frac{2(1+z^2)}{1+z^2}\): \(2+\frac{1-z^2}{1+z^2}=\frac{2+2z^2+1-z^2}{1+z^2}=\frac{3+z^2}{1+z^2}\).Why? To add a whole number to a fraction, write it over the same denominator, e.g. \(1=\frac{1+z^2}{1+z^2}\). Then add the tops: \(\frac ac+\frac bc=\frac{a+b}c\). See the rule
  4. Divide by multiplying by the flip: \(\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\cdot\frac{1+z^2}{3+z^2}=\frac{2\,dz}{z^2+3}\). The \(1+z^2\) cancels.Why? Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip: \(a\div\frac bc=a\cdot\frac cb\). See the rule
  5. Match the arctan form: \(\int\frac{du}{a^2+u^2}=\frac1a\arctan\frac ua\) with \(u=z\) and \(a^2=3\), so \(a=\sqrt3\).Why? Compare the integrand to the formula shape. \(a^2\) is the constant part and \(u^2\) is the variable part, and the sign and order decide which formula applies. See the rule
  6. Integrate: \(2\cdot\frac1{\sqrt3}\arctan\frac z{\sqrt3}=\frac2{\sqrt3}\arctan\frac z{\sqrt3}+C\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  7. Put x back: \(\dfrac2{\sqrt3}\arctan\dfrac{\tan\frac x2}{\sqrt3}+C\).
  8. Check: in z: \(\frac{d}{dz}\left[\frac2{\sqrt3}\arctan\frac z{\sqrt3}\right]=\frac2{\sqrt3}\cdot\frac1{1+\frac{z^2}3}\cdot\frac1{\sqrt3}=\frac23\cdot\frac3{3+z^2}=\frac2{z^2+3}\), which is the z-integrand ✓

Answer\(\dfrac2{\sqrt3}\arctan\dfrac{\tan\frac x2}{\sqrt3}+C\)

9·P3

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{\sin x}\)

UsesWeierstrass Log rule

  1. Spot the form: a fraction built only from \(\sin x\): Weierstrass, \(z=\tan\frac x2\).
  2. Write the substitutions: \(\sin x=\frac{2z}{1+z^2}\) and \(dx=\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\).Why? The Weierstrass formulas come from the double-angle identities written in terms of \(\tan\frac x2\). They turn sin and cos into plain fractions in z. See the rule
  3. Assemble: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{\sin x}=\int\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\div\frac{2z}{1+z^2}\).
  4. Divide by multiplying by the flip: \(\frac{2\,dz}{1+z^2}\cdot\frac{1+z^2}{2z}=\frac{dz}z\). The 2s cancel and the \(1+z^2\) cancels.Why? Dividing by a fraction is the same as multiplying by its flip: \(a\div\frac bc=a\cdot\frac cb\). See the rule
  5. Log rule: \(\int\frac{dz}z=\ln|z|+C\).Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  6. Put x back: \(\ln\left|\tan\frac x2\right|+C\).
  7. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln\left|\tan\frac x2\right|=\frac{\frac12\sec^2\frac x2}{\tan\frac x2}=\frac1{2\sin\frac x2\cos\frac x2}=\frac1{\sin x}\) ✓ (the last step is the double angle \(\sin x=2\sin\frac x2\cos\frac x2\))Why? This comes from \(\sin(A+B)=\sin A\cos B+\cos A\sin B\) with \(A=B=\theta\). See the rule
  8. Note: \(\tan\frac x2=\csc x-\cot x\), so this matches the \(\int\csc x\,dx\) formula.

Answer\(\ln\left|\tan\frac x2\right|+C\)

Exercises · algebraic substitution

9·X1

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{(x+1)\sqrt{x+3}}\)

UsesAlgebraic substitution Partial fractions table

  1. Remove the root: \(z^2=x+3\), so \(x=z^2-3\) and \(dx=2z\,dz\).Why? Algebraic substitution: set the root (or the expression under it) equal to a new variable so the radical disappears, then rewrite x and dx in terms of it. See the rule
  2. Rewrite the other factor: \(x+1=z^2-3+1=z^2-2\).
  3. Substitute: \(\int\frac{2z\,dz}{(z^2-2)z}=2\int\frac{dz}{z^2-2}\).
  4. Use the \(u^2-a^2\) form (\(a=\sqrt2\)). By partial fractions, \(\int\frac{du}{u^2-a^2}=\frac1{2a}\ln\left|\frac{u-a}{u+a}\right|\).
  5. Apply: \(2\cdot\frac1{2\sqrt2}\ln\left|\frac{z-\sqrt2}{z+\sqrt2}\right|=\frac1{\sqrt2}\ln\left|\frac{\sqrt{x+3}-\sqrt2}{\sqrt{x+3}+\sqrt2}\right|+C\).

Answer\(\frac1{\sqrt2}\ln\left|\dfrac{\sqrt{x+3}-\sqrt2}{\sqrt{x+3}+\sqrt2}\right|+C\)

9·X2

\(\displaystyle\int\sqrt{4+\sqrt x}\,dx\)

UsesAlgebraic substitution Power formula

  1. Remove the outer root: \(z^2=4+\sqrt x\). Then \(\sqrt x=z^2-4\), so \(x=(z^2-4)^2\).Why? Algebraic substitution: set the root (or the expression under it) equal to a new variable so the radical disappears, then rewrite x and dx in terms of it. See the rule
  2. Find dx: \(dx=2(z^2-4)\cdot2z\,dz=4z(z^2-4)\,dz\).
  3. Substitute: \(\int z\cdot4z(z^2-4)\,dz=4\int(z^4-4z^2)\,dz\).
  4. Integrate: \(4\left(\frac{z^5}5-\frac{4z^3}3\right)=\frac45z^5-\frac{16}3z^3\).
  5. Put x back (\(z=\sqrt{4+\sqrt x}\)): \(\frac45(4+\sqrt x)^{5/2}-\frac{16}3(4+\sqrt x)^{3/2}+C\).

Answer\(\frac45(4+\sqrt x)^{5/2}-\frac{16}3(4+\sqrt x)^{3/2}+C\)

9·X3

\(\displaystyle\int(5x-1)\sqrt{x+2}\,dx\)

UsesAlgebraic substitution Power formula

  1. Remove the root: \(z^2=x+2\), so \(x=z^2-2\) and \(dx=2z\,dz\).Why? Algebraic substitution: set the root (or the expression under it) equal to a new variable so the radical disappears, then rewrite x and dx in terms of it. See the rule
  2. Rewrite the other factor: \(5x-1=5(z^2-2)-1=5z^2-11\).
  3. Substitute: \(\int(5z^2-11)\cdot z\cdot2z\,dz=\int(10z^4-22z^2)\,dz\).
  4. Integrate: \(2z^5-\frac{22}3z^3\).
  5. Put x back: \(2(x+2)^{5/2}-\frac{22}3(x+2)^{3/2}+C\).

Answer\(2(x+2)^{5/2}-\frac{22}3(x+2)^{3/2}+C\)

9·X4

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{\left(x^{1/3}-x^{1/4}\right)dx}{4x^{1/2}}\)

UsesPower formula

  1. Divide each term by \(4x^{1/2}\): \(\frac{x^{1/3}}{4x^{1/2}}=\frac14x^{\frac13-\frac12}=\frac14x^{-1/6}\), and \(\frac{x^{1/4}}{4x^{1/2}}=\frac14x^{-1/4}\).Why? Dividing powers with the same base subtracts the exponents: \(\frac{a^m}{a^n}=a^{m-n}\). See the ruleWhy? A sum on top can be split into separate fractions, \(\frac{a+b}c=\frac ac+\frac bc\), and the sum rule lets you integrate each piece separately. See the rule
  2. Power formula: \(\frac14\left(\frac{x^{5/6}}{5/6}-\frac{x^{3/4}}{3/4}\right)=\frac14\left(\frac65x^{5/6}-\frac43x^{3/4}\right)\).
  3. Simplify: \(\frac3{10}x^{5/6}-\frac13x^{3/4}+C\).
  4. Note: the lesson's method (\(x=z^{12}\), since the LCD of 3, 4 and 2 is 12) gives the same result.

Answer\(\frac3{10}x^{5/6}-\frac13x^{3/4}+C\)

9·X5

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{e^{2x}\,dx}{\sqrt{e^x-1}}\)

UsesAlgebraic substitution Power formula

  1. Remove the root: \(z^2=e^x-1\), so \(e^x=z^2+1\).Why? Algebraic substitution: set the root (or the expression under it) equal to a new variable so the radical disappears, then rewrite x and dx in terms of it. See the rule
  2. Find \(e^xdx\): differentiate both sides: \(e^xdx=2z\,dz\).
  3. Split the top: \(e^{2x}dx=e^x\cdot e^xdx=(z^2+1)\cdot2z\,dz\).Why? Exponent law: \(e^{2x}=e^{x+x}=e^x\cdot e^x=(e^x)^2\). See the ruleWhy? Rewrite the top as (a multiple of the derivative of the bottom) + (a leftover number), so one piece fits the log or power rule and the other fits arctan or arcsin. See the rule
  4. Substitute: \(\int\frac{(z^2+1)2z\,dz}{z}=2\int(z^2+1)\,dz=\frac23z^3+2z\).
  5. Put x back: \(\frac23(e^x-1)^{3/2}+2\sqrt{e^x-1}+C\).

Answer\(\frac23(e^x-1)^{3/2}+2\sqrt{e^x-1}+C\)

9·X6

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dy}{\sqrt{y+4}-2}\)

UsesAlgebraic substitution Log rule

  1. Remove the root: \(z^2=y+4\), so \(dy=2z\,dz\).Why? Algebraic substitution: set the root (or the expression under it) equal to a new variable so the radical disappears, then rewrite x and dx in terms of it. See the rule
  2. Substitute: \(\int\frac{2z\,dz}{z-2}\).
  3. Divide: \(\frac z{z-2}=1+\frac2{z-2}\).
  4. Integrate: \(2\int\left(1+\frac2{z-2}\right)dz=2z+4\ln|z-2|\).
  5. Put y back: \(2\sqrt{y+4}+4\ln\left|\sqrt{y+4}-2\right|+C\).

Answer\(2\sqrt{y+4}+4\ln\left|\sqrt{y+4}-2\right|+C\)

Exercises · trigonometric substitution

9·Y1

\(\displaystyle\int x^3\sqrt{4-x^2}\,dx\)

UsesTrig substitution table Trig powers table

  1. Pick the substitution: \(4-x^2\) is \(a^2-u^2\) with \(a=2\), so \(x=2\sin\theta\), \(dx=2\cos\theta\,d\theta\), \(\sqrt{4-x^2}=2\cos\theta\).Why? Each substitution turns the expression under the root into a perfect square via a Pythagorean identity, e.g. \(4-4\sin^2\theta=4\cos^2\theta\). See the rule
  2. Substitute: \(\int8\sin^3\theta\cdot2\cos\theta\cdot2\cos\theta\,d\theta=32\int\sin^3\theta\cos^2\theta\,d\theta\).
  3. Odd power of sine: \(\sin^3\theta=(1-\cos^2\theta)\sin\theta\). With \(w=\cos\theta\), \(dw=-\sin\theta\,d\theta\): \(-32\int(1-w^2)w^2dw=32\left(-\frac{w^3}3+\frac{w^5}5\right)\).
  4. Back to x: \(\cos\theta=\frac{\sqrt{4-x^2}}2\), so \(\cos^3\theta=\frac{(4-x^2)^{3/2}}8\) and \(\cos^5\theta=\frac{(4-x^2)^{5/2}}{32}\).Why? A root is a fractional power: \(\sqrt[n]{a^m}=a^{m/n}\). A square root is the power ½. See the ruleWhy? Reference triangle: the substitution gives two sides (e.g. \(x=2\sin\theta\) means \(\sin\theta=\frac x2=\frac{\text{opp}}{\text{hyp}}\)). Find the third side with Pythagoras, then read off any trig value with SOH-CAH-TOA. See the rule
  5. Simplify: \(-\frac{32}{3\cdot8}(4-x^2)^{3/2}+\frac{32}{5\cdot32}(4-x^2)^{5/2}=-\frac43(4-x^2)^{3/2}+\frac15(4-x^2)^{5/2}+C\).

Answer\(-\frac43(4-x^2)^{3/2}+\frac15(4-x^2)^{5/2}+C\)

9·Y2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{(25+16x^2)^{3/2}}\)

UsesTrig substitution table

  1. Pick the substitution: \(25+16x^2=5^2+(4x)^2\), so \(4x=5\tan\theta\).Why? Each substitution turns the expression under the root into a perfect square via a Pythagorean identity, e.g. \(4-4\sin^2\theta=4\cos^2\theta\). See the rule
  2. Convert: \(dx=\frac54\sec^2\theta\,d\theta\). \(25+16x^2=25\sec^2\theta\), so \((25+16x^2)^{3/2}=125\sec^3\theta\).
  3. Substitute: \(\int\frac{\frac54\sec^2\theta}{125\sec^3\theta}d\theta=\frac1{100}\int\cos\theta\,d\theta=\frac1{100}\sin\theta\).
  4. Back to x with the triangle (opposite 4x, adjacent 5, hypotenuse \(\sqrt{25+16x^2}\)): \(\sin\theta=\frac{4x}{\sqrt{25+16x^2}}\).Why? Reference triangle: the substitution gives two sides (e.g. \(x=2\sin\theta\) means \(\sin\theta=\frac x2=\frac{\text{opp}}{\text{hyp}}\)). Find the third side with Pythagoras, then read off any trig value with SOH-CAH-TOA. See the rule
  5. Simplify: \(\frac{4x}{100\sqrt{25+16x^2}}=\frac{x}{25\sqrt{25+16x^2}}+C\).

Answer\(\dfrac{x}{25\sqrt{25+16x^2}}+C\)

9·Y3

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{(4x^2-1)^{3/2}}\)

UsesTrig substitution table

  1. Pick the substitution: \(4x^2-1=(2x)^2-1^2\) is \(u^2-a^2\), so \(2x=\sec\theta\).Why? Each substitution turns the expression under the root into a perfect square via a Pythagorean identity, e.g. \(4-4\sin^2\theta=4\cos^2\theta\). See the rule
  2. Convert: \(dx=\frac12\sec\theta\tan\theta\,d\theta\). \(4x^2-1=\tan^2\theta\), so \((4x^2-1)^{3/2}=\tan^3\theta\).
  3. Substitute: \(\frac12\int\frac{\sec\theta\tan\theta}{\tan^3\theta}d\theta=\frac12\int\frac{\sec\theta}{\tan^2\theta}d\theta\).
  4. Rewrite in sin and cos: \(\frac{\sec\theta}{\tan^2\theta}=\frac1{\cos\theta}\cdot\frac{\cos^2\theta}{\sin^2\theta}=\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin^2\theta}\).Why? \(\sec\theta\) is defined as \(\frac1{\cos\theta}\). Squaring both sides gives \(\frac1{\cos^2\theta}=\sec^2\theta\). See the ruleWhy? \(\cot\theta=\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}\), the flip of tan. See the rule
  5. Integrate (\(w=\sin\theta\)): \(\frac12\int w^{-2}dw=-\frac1{2\sin\theta}=-\frac12\csc\theta\).
  6. Back to x: \(\csc\theta=\frac{\sec\theta}{\tan\theta}=\frac{2x}{\sqrt{4x^2-1}}\), giving \(-\frac{x}{\sqrt{4x^2-1}}+C\).Why? Reference triangle: the substitution gives two sides (e.g. \(x=2\sin\theta\) means \(\sin\theta=\frac x2=\frac{\text{opp}}{\text{hyp}}\)). Find the third side with Pythagoras, then read off any trig value with SOH-CAH-TOA. See the rule

Answer\(-\dfrac{x}{\sqrt{4x^2-1}}+C\)

9·Y4

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x^2\sqrt{4x^2-9}}\)

UsesTrig substitution table

  1. Pick the substitution: \(4x^2-9=(2x)^2-3^2\), so \(2x=3\sec\theta\).Why? Each substitution turns the expression under the root into a perfect square via a Pythagorean identity, e.g. \(4-4\sin^2\theta=4\cos^2\theta\). See the rule
  2. Convert: \(x=\frac32\sec\theta\), \(dx=\frac32\sec\theta\tan\theta\,d\theta\), \(x^2=\frac94\sec^2\theta\), \(\sqrt{4x^2-9}=3\tan\theta\).
  3. Substitute: \(\int\frac{\frac32\sec\theta\tan\theta}{\frac94\sec^2\theta\cdot3\tan\theta}d\theta=\frac{3/2}{27/4}\int\frac{d\theta}{\sec\theta}=\frac29\int\cos\theta\,d\theta\).
  4. Integrate: \(\frac29\sin\theta\).
  5. Back to x: \(\sin\theta=\frac{\tan\theta}{\sec\theta}=\frac{\sqrt{4x^2-9}/3}{2x/3}=\frac{\sqrt{4x^2-9}}{2x}\).Why? Reference triangle: the substitution gives two sides (e.g. \(x=2\sin\theta\) means \(\sin\theta=\frac x2=\frac{\text{opp}}{\text{hyp}}\)). Find the third side with Pythagoras, then read off any trig value with SOH-CAH-TOA. See the rule
  6. Simplify: \(\frac29\cdot\frac{\sqrt{4x^2-9}}{2x}=\frac{\sqrt{4x^2-9}}{9x}+C\).

Answer\(\dfrac{\sqrt{4x^2-9}}{9x}+C\)

9·Y5

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{x^2\,dx}{\sqrt{9-25x^2}}\)

UsesTrig substitution table Half-angle Arcsin form

  1. Pick the substitution: \(9-25x^2=3^2-(5x)^2\), so \(5x=3\sin\theta\).Why? Each substitution turns the expression under the root into a perfect square via a Pythagorean identity, e.g. \(4-4\sin^2\theta=4\cos^2\theta\). See the rule
  2. Convert: \(x=\frac35\sin\theta\), \(dx=\frac35\cos\theta\,d\theta\), \(x^2=\frac9{25}\sin^2\theta\), \(\sqrt{9-25x^2}=3\cos\theta\).
  3. Substitute: \(\int\frac{\frac9{25}\sin^2\theta\cdot\frac35\cos\theta}{3\cos\theta}d\theta=\frac9{125}\int\sin^2\theta\,d\theta\).
  4. Half-angle: \(\int\sin^2\theta\,d\theta=\frac12(\theta-\sin\theta\cos\theta)\), giving \(\frac9{250}(\theta-\sin\theta\cos\theta)\).Why? This comes from rearranging \(\cos2\theta=1-2\sin^2\theta=2\cos^2\theta-1\). See the rule
  5. Back to x: \(\sin\theta=\frac{5x}3\), \(\cos\theta=\frac{\sqrt{9-25x^2}}3\), \(\theta=\arcsin\frac{5x}3\).Why? Reference triangle: the substitution gives two sides (e.g. \(x=2\sin\theta\) means \(\sin\theta=\frac x2=\frac{\text{opp}}{\text{hyp}}\)). Find the third side with Pythagoras, then read off any trig value with SOH-CAH-TOA. See the rule
  6. Simplify: \(\frac9{250}\cdot\frac{5x\sqrt{9-25x^2}}9=\frac{x\sqrt{9-25x^2}}{50}\), so the answer is \(\frac9{250}\arcsin\frac{5x}3-\frac{x}{50}\sqrt{9-25x^2}+C\).

Answer\(\frac9{250}\arcsin\frac{5x}3-\frac{x}{50}\sqrt{9-25x^2}+C\)

9·Y6

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(2x+3)\,dx}{\sqrt{x^2+2x+5}}\)

UsesCompleting the square Power formula Trig substitution table ∫sec / ∫csc ln(u+√(u²±a²)) form

  1. Complete the square: \(x^2+2x+5=(x+1)^2+4\).Why? Take half the x-coefficient and square it, then add and subtract it: \(x^2+bx=\left(x+\frac b2\right)^2-\frac{b^2}4\). See the rule
  2. Split the top to match the bottom's derivative \(2x+2\): \(2x+3=(2x+2)+1\).Why? Rewrite the top as (a multiple of the derivative of the bottom) + (a leftover number), so one piece fits the log or power rule and the other fits arctan or arcsin. See the rule
  3. First part, power formula: \(w=x^2+2x+5\), \(dw=(2x+2)dx\). \(\int w^{-1/2}dw=2\sqrt w=2\sqrt{x^2+2x+5}\).
  4. Second part, trig substitution: \(\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{(x+1)^2+4}}\). With \(x+1=2\tan\theta\), the root is \(2\sec\theta\) and \(dx=2\sec^2\theta\,d\theta\), leaving \(\int\sec\theta\,d\theta=\ln|\sec\theta+\tan\theta|\).
  5. Back to x: \(\sec\theta=\frac{\sqrt{x^2+2x+5}}2\), \(\tan\theta=\frac{x+1}2\). So \(\ln\frac{x+1+\sqrt{x^2+2x+5}}2\), and the \(-\ln2\) goes into C.Why? Reference triangle: the substitution gives two sides (e.g. \(x=2\sin\theta\) means \(\sin\theta=\frac x2=\frac{\text{opp}}{\text{hyp}}\)). Find the third side with Pythagoras, then read off any trig value with SOH-CAH-TOA. See the rule
  6. Combine: \(2\sqrt{x^2+2x+5}+\ln\left(x+1+\sqrt{x^2+2x+5}\right)+C\).

Answer\(2\sqrt{x^2+2x+5}+\ln\left(x+1+\sqrt{x^2+2x+5}\right)+C\)

LESSON 10

Partial Fractions

Break a proper rational function into simple fractions, one group per factor of the denominator. Then each piece integrates to a \(\ln\), a power, or an \(\arctan\).

  1. If it's improper, do long division first.
  2. Factor the denominator completely and write the forms from the partial fractions table.
  3. Multiply through by the denominator. Plug in the roots to get some constants quickly, then equate coefficients for the rest.
  4. Integrate each piece. For a quadratic piece \(\frac{A(2ax+b)+B}{ax^2+bx+c}\), the A part gives \(A\ln\), and the B part gives an arctan after completing the square.

Slide examples

10·E1

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(8x-1)\,dx}{2x^2-3x-2}\)

UsesPartial fractions table Log rule

  1. Spot the form: a rational function whose bottom factors into two different linear pieces, and the top has a lower degree. Use partial fractions.Why? Each factor of the denominator gets its own fraction: a constant over a linear factor, one term per power for repeated factors, and \(A(\text{derivative})+B\) over an irreducible quadratic. See the rule
  2. Factor the bottom: \(2x^2-3x-2=(2x+1)(x-2)\). Check: \(2x^2-4x+x-2=2x^2-3x-2\) ✓
  3. Set up the pieces: two linear factors, so one constant over each: \(\dfrac{8x-1}{(2x+1)(x-2)}=\dfrac A{2x+1}+\dfrac B{x-2}\).Why? Each factor of the denominator gets its own fraction: a constant over a linear factor, one term per power for repeated factors, and \(A(\text{derivative})+B\) over an irreducible quadratic. See the rule
  4. Clear the fractions: \(8x-1=A(x-2)+B(2x+1)\).Why? Multiply both sides by the full denominator so every fraction disappears and only polynomials are left. See the rule
  5. Plug in x = 2: the A term vanishes: \(16-1=B(5)\), so \(15=5B\) and \(B=3\).Why? Choosing x to make a factor equal 0 wipes out every term containing that factor, leaving one unknown to solve for. See the rule
  6. Plug in x = −½: the B term vanishes: \(-4-1=A\left(-\frac52\right)\), so \(-5=-\frac52A\) and \(A=2\).
  7. Rewrite the integral: \(\displaystyle\int\frac{2\,dx}{2x+1}+\int\frac{3\,dx}{x-2}\).Why? Sum rule: integrate each term separately, then add the results. See the rule
  8. First piece: \(u=2x+1\), \(du=2\,dx\). The top \(2\,dx\) is exactly du, so \(\int\frac{du}u=\ln|2x+1|\).Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  9. Second piece: \(3\int\frac{dx}{x-2}=3\ln|x-2|\).Why? Constant multiple rule: a number multiplying the integrand can be pulled out front. Integrate what is left, then multiply the number back in. See the rule
  10. Combine: \(\ln|2x+1|+3\ln|x-2|+C\). Optional: \(=\ln|(2x+1)(x-2)^3|+C\).Why? Log rules: \(k\ln a=\ln a^k\) and \(\ln a+\ln b=\ln ab\). Combining is optional; both forms are correct. See the rule
  11. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}[\dots]=\frac2{2x+1}+\frac3{x-2}=\frac{2(x-2)+3(2x+1)}{(2x+1)(x-2)}=\frac{8x-1}{2x^2-3x-2}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\ln|2x+1|+3\ln|x-2|+C=\ln\left|(2x+1)(x-2)^3\right|+C\)

10·E2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(3x^2-5x-4)\,dx}{x^3-5x^2+7x-3}\)

UsesPartial fractions table Log rule Power formula

  1. Spot the form: a rational function with a cubic on the bottom and a lower-degree top. Factor the bottom and use partial fractions.Why? Each factor of the denominator gets its own fraction: a constant over a linear factor, one term per power for repeated factors, and \(A(\text{derivative})+B\) over an irreducible quadratic. See the rule
  2. Find a root: try \(x=1\): \(1-5+7-3=0\) ✓, so \((x-1)\) is a factor.Why? To find a root of a cubic, try small whole numbers that divide the constant term (here \(\pm1,\pm3\)). Each root r gives a factor \((x-r)\). See the rule
  3. Factor the bottom: divide by \((x-1)\): \(x^3-5x^2+7x-3=(x-1)(x^2-4x+3)=(x-1)(x-1)(x-3)=(x-3)(x-1)^2\).Why? Synthetic division with 1 on the coefficients \(1,-5,7,-3\) gives \(1,-4,3\) with remainder 0. See the rule
  4. Set up the pieces: \((x-1)\) is repeated, so it needs one term per power: \(\dfrac A{x-3}+\dfrac B{x-1}+\dfrac C{(x-1)^2}\).Why? Each factor of the denominator gets its own fraction: a constant over a linear factor, one term per power for repeated factors, and \(A(\text{derivative})+B\) over an irreducible quadratic. See the rule
  5. Clear the fractions: \(3x^2-5x-4=A(x-1)^2+B(x-3)(x-1)+C(x-3)\).Why? Multiply both sides by the full denominator so every fraction disappears and only polynomials are left. See the rule
  6. Plug in x = 1: only the C term survives: \(3-5-4=C(-2)\), so \(-6=-2C\) and \(C=3\).Why? Choosing x to make a factor equal 0 wipes out every term containing that factor, leaving one unknown to solve for. See the rule
  7. Plug in x = 3: only the A term survives: \(27-15-4=A(4)\), so \(8=4A\) and \(A=2\).
  8. Find B: there is no root left, so match the \(x^2\) coefficients: on the right, \(x^2\) comes from \(A\) and \(B\), so \(3=A+B\) and \(B=1\).Why? Two polynomials are equal for every x only if each power of x has the same coefficient on both sides, so you can match \(x^3\), \(x^2\), \(x\) and the constants. See the rule
  9. Log pieces: \(\int\frac{2\,dx}{x-3}+\int\frac{dx}{x-1}=2\ln|x-3|+\ln|x-1|\).Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  10. Power piece: \(3\int(x-1)^{-2}dx=3\cdot\frac{(x-1)^{-1}}{-1}=-\frac3{x-1}\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  11. Combine: \(2\ln|x-3|+\ln|x-1|-\frac3{x-1}+C=\ln|(x-1)(x-3)^2|-\frac3{x-1}+C\).Why? Log rules: \(k\ln a=\ln a^k\) and \(\ln a+\ln b=\ln ab\). Combining is optional; both forms are correct. See the rule
  12. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}[\dots]=\frac2{x-3}+\frac1{x-1}+\frac3{(x-1)^2}\). Over \((x-3)(x-1)^2\) the top is \(2(x-1)^2+(x-3)(x-1)+3(x-3)=3x^2-5x-4\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\ln\left|(x-1)(x-3)^2\right|-\dfrac3{x-1}+C\)

10·E3

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(3x^2+4x-7)\,dx}{x^3+x-10}\)

UsesPartial fractions table Completing the square Log rule Arctan form

  1. Spot the form: a rational function with a cubic on the bottom and a lower-degree top. Factor the bottom and use partial fractions.Why? Each factor of the denominator gets its own fraction: a constant over a linear factor, one term per power for repeated factors, and \(A(\text{derivative})+B\) over an irreducible quadratic. See the rule
  2. Find a root: try \(x=2\): \(8+2-10=0\) ✓, so \((x-2)\) is a factor.Why? To find a root of a cubic, try small whole numbers that divide the constant term (here \(\pm1,\pm3\)). Each root r gives a factor \((x-r)\). See the rule
  3. Factor the bottom: \(x^3+x-10=(x-2)(x^2+2x+5)\).Why? Synthetic division with 2 on the coefficients \(1,0,1,-10\) gives \(1,2,5\) with remainder 0. See the rule
  4. Check the quadratic: \(b^2-4ac=4-20=-16<0\), so \(x^2+2x+5\) cannot be factored.Why? A quadratic \(ax^2+bx+c\) factors over the real numbers only if \(b^2-4ac\ge0\). If it is negative, keep it as one irreducible factor. See the rule
  5. Set up the pieces: \(\dfrac A{x-2}+\dfrac{B(2x+2)+C}{x^2+2x+5}\). Writing the top as B·(derivative) + C makes the log part easy later.Why? Each factor of the denominator gets its own fraction: a constant over a linear factor, one term per power for repeated factors, and \(A(\text{derivative})+B\) over an irreducible quadratic. See the rule
  6. Clear the fractions: \(3x^2+4x-7=A(x^2+2x+5)+[B(2x+2)+C](x-2)\).Why? Multiply both sides by the full denominator so every fraction disappears and only polynomials are left. See the rule
  7. Plug in x = 2: \(12+8-7=A(4+4+5)\), so \(13=13A\) and \(A=1\).Why? Choosing x to make a factor equal 0 wipes out every term containing that factor, leaving one unknown to solve for. See the rule
  8. Match the x² terms: \(3=A+2B\), so \(B=1\).Why? Two polynomials are equal for every x only if each power of x has the same coefficient on both sides, so you can match \(x^3\), \(x^2\), \(x\) and the constants. See the rule
  9. Match the x terms: \(4=2A+(C-2B)=2+C-2\), so \(C=4\).
  10. Linear piece: \(\int\frac{dx}{x-2}=\ln|x-2|\).Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the rule
  11. Log piece: \(\int\frac{(2x+2)\,dx}{x^2+2x+5}=\ln(x^2+2x+5)\): the top is the derivative of the bottom. No bars needed, since the quadratic is always positive.Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the ruleWhy? ln only accepts positive inputs. The bars in \(\ln|u|\) keep the answer valid when u is negative. If u can never be negative (like \(x^2+5\)), you can drop them. See the rule
  12. Arctan piece: complete the square: \(x^2+2x+5=(x+1)^2+4\). With \(u=x+1\), \(a=2\): \(4\int\frac{dx}{(x+1)^2+2^2}=4\cdot\frac12\arctan\frac{x+1}2=2\arctan\frac{x+1}2\).Why? Take half the x-coefficient and square it, then add and subtract it: \(x^2+bx=\left(x+\frac b2\right)^2-\frac{b^2}4\). See the ruleWhy? \(\int\frac{du}{a^2+u^2}=\frac1a\arctan\frac ua\). It comes from \((\arctan x)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\). Factoring \(a^2\) out of the bottom is what puts the \(\frac1a\) in front. See the rule
  13. Combine: \(\ln|x-2|+\ln(x^2+2x+5)+2\arctan\frac{x+1}2+C=\ln|(x-2)(x^2+2x+5)|+2\arctan\frac{x+1}2+C\).Why? Log rules: \(k\ln a=\ln a^k\) and \(\ln a+\ln b=\ln ab\). Combining is optional; both forms are correct. See the rule
  14. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}[\dots]=\frac1{x-2}+\frac{2x+2}{x^2+2x+5}+\frac{4}{x^2+2x+5}=\frac{(x^2+2x+5)+(2x+6)(x-2)}{(x-2)(x^2+2x+5)}=\frac{3x^2+4x-7}{x^3+x-10}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\ln\left|(x-2)(x^2+2x+5)\right|+2\arctan\frac{x+1}2+C\)

10·E4

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{x^5\,dx}{(x^2+4)^2}\)

UsesLong division Partial fractions table Log rule Power formula

  1. Spot the form: a rational function where the top (degree 5) is bigger than the bottom (degree 4). Divide first, then use partial fractions on the remainder.Why? When the top's degree is at least the bottom's, divide first, like \(\frac72=3+\frac12\): quotient + remainder ÷ divisor. See the rule
  2. Expand the bottom: \((x^2+4)^2=x^4+8x^2+16\).Why? Expanding a square: \((a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2\). See the rule
  3. Divide: \(x^5\div x^4=x\). Multiply: \(x(x^4+8x^2+16)=x^5+8x^3+16x\). Subtract: \(x^5-(x^5+8x^3+16x)=-8x^3-16x\). The degree 3 is less than 4, so stop.Why? When the top's degree is at least the bottom's, divide first, like \(\frac72=3+\frac12\): quotient + remainder ÷ divisor. See the rule
  4. Write the result: \(\dfrac{x^5}{(x^2+4)^2}=x-\dfrac{8x^3+16x}{(x^2+4)^2}\).
  5. Set up the pieces: repeated quadratic, so one term per power: \(\dfrac{8x^3+16x}{(x^2+4)^2}=\dfrac{A(2x)+B}{x^2+4}+\dfrac{C(2x)+D}{(x^2+4)^2}\).Why? Each factor of the denominator gets its own fraction: a constant over a linear factor, one term per power for repeated factors, and \(A(\text{derivative})+B\) over an irreducible quadratic. See the rule
  6. Clear the fractions: \(8x^3+16x=(2Ax+B)(x^2+4)+2Cx+D=2Ax^3+Bx^2+(8A+2C)x+(4B+D)\).Why? Multiply both sides by the full denominator so every fraction disappears and only polynomials are left. See the rule
  7. Match coefficients: \(x^3\): \(2A=8\Rightarrow A=4\). \(x^2\): \(B=0\). \(x\): \(8A+2C=16\Rightarrow32+2C=16\Rightarrow C=-8\). Constant: \(4B+D=0\Rightarrow D=0\).Why? Two polynomials are equal for every x only if each power of x has the same coefficient on both sides, so you can match \(x^3\), \(x^2\), \(x\) and the constants. See the rule
  8. Rewrite the integral: \(\displaystyle\int x\,dx-4\int\frac{2x\,dx}{x^2+4}+8\int\frac{2x\,dx}{(x^2+4)^2}\). The signs flip because of the minus in front of the remainder.
  9. First piece: \(\int x\,dx=\frac{x^2}2\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the rule
  10. Log piece: \(u=x^2+4\), \(du=2x\,dx\): \(-4\int\frac{du}u=-4\ln(x^2+4)\). No bars needed, since \(x^2+4>0\).Why? The log rule comes from \(\frac{d}{dx}\ln|u|=\frac{u'}{u}\). So any fraction whose top is the derivative of its bottom integrates to ln of the bottom. See the ruleWhy? ln only accepts positive inputs. The bars in \(\ln|u|\) keep the answer valid when u is negative. If u can never be negative (like \(x^2+5\)), you can drop them. See the rule
  11. Power piece: same u: \(8\int u^{-2}du=8\cdot\frac{u^{-1}}{-1}=-\frac8{x^2+4}\).Why? Power formula: add 1 to the exponent, then divide by the new exponent, \(\int u^n\,du=\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\). It works because differentiating \(\frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1}\) brings the \(n+1\) down and cancels it. (Not for \(n=-1\): that is the log rule.) See the ruleWhy? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  12. Combine: \(\frac{x^2}2-4\ln(x^2+4)-\frac8{x^2+4}+C\).
  13. Check: \(\frac{d}{dx}[\dots]=x-\frac{8x}{x^2+4}+\frac{16x}{(x^2+4)^2}=\frac{x(x^2+4)^2-8x(x^2+4)+16x}{(x^2+4)^2}=\frac{x^5}{(x^2+4)^2}\) ✓Why? Differentiating undoes integrating. If the derivative of your answer gives back the original integrand, the answer is right. See the rule

Answer\(\frac{x^2}2-4\ln(x^2+4)-\dfrac8{x^2+4}+C\)

Exercises · full solutions

10·X1

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(x^2-8)\,dx}{x^3+5x^2+6x}\)

UsesPartial fractions table Log rule

  1. Factor the bottom: \(x^3+5x^2+6x=x(x^2+5x+6)=x(x+2)(x+3)\).
  2. Set up the pieces: \(\frac Ax+\frac B{x+2}+\frac C{x+3}\).Why? Each factor of the denominator gets its own fraction: a constant over a linear factor, one term per power for repeated factors, and \(A(\text{derivative})+B\) over an irreducible quadratic. See the rule
  3. Clear the fractions: \(x^2-8=A(x+2)(x+3)+Bx(x+3)+Cx(x+2)\).Why? Multiply both sides by the full denominator so every fraction disappears and only polynomials are left. See the rule
  4. Plug in the roots: \(x=0\): \(-8=6A\Rightarrow A=-\frac43\). \(x=-2\): \(-4=-2B\Rightarrow B=2\). \(x=-3\): \(1=3C\Rightarrow C=\frac13\).Why? Choosing x to make a factor equal 0 wipes out every term containing that factor, leaving one unknown to solve for. See the rule
  5. Integrate each (all log rule): \(-\frac43\ln|x|+2\ln|x+2|+\frac13\ln|x+3|+C\).

Answer\(-\frac43\ln|x|+2\ln|x+2|+\frac13\ln|x+3|+C\)

10·X2

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(x^2-14x+5)\,dx}{x(x^2-6x+5)}\)

UsesPartial fractions table Log rule

  1. Factor the bottom: \(x(x^2-6x+5)=x(x-1)(x-5)\).
  2. Set up the pieces: \(\frac Ax+\frac B{x-1}+\frac C{x-5}\).Why? Each factor of the denominator gets its own fraction: a constant over a linear factor, one term per power for repeated factors, and \(A(\text{derivative})+B\) over an irreducible quadratic. See the rule
  3. Clear the fractions: \(x^2-14x+5=A(x-1)(x-5)+Bx(x-5)+Cx(x-1)\).Why? Multiply both sides by the full denominator so every fraction disappears and only polynomials are left. See the rule
  4. Plug in the roots: \(x=0\): \(5=5A\Rightarrow A=1\). \(x=1\): \(-8=-4B\Rightarrow B=2\). \(x=5\): \(-40=20C\Rightarrow C=-2\).Why? Choosing x to make a factor equal 0 wipes out every term containing that factor, leaving one unknown to solve for. See the rule
  5. Integrate each: \(\ln|x|+2\ln|x-1|-2\ln|x-5|+C\).

Answer\(\ln|x|+2\ln|x-1|-2\ln|x-5|+C\)

10·X3

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(5x-4)\,dx}{x^4+2x^3}\)

UsesPartial fractions table Log rule Power formula

  1. Factor the bottom: \(x^4+2x^3=x^3(x+2)\).
  2. Set up the pieces: \(x\) is repeated 3 times, so \(\frac Ax+\frac B{x^2}+\frac C{x^3}+\frac D{x+2}\).Why? Each factor of the denominator gets its own fraction: a constant over a linear factor, one term per power for repeated factors, and \(A(\text{derivative})+B\) over an irreducible quadratic. See the rule
  3. Clear the fractions: \(5x-4=Ax^2(x+2)+Bx(x+2)+C(x+2)+Dx^3\).Why? Multiply both sides by the full denominator so every fraction disappears and only polynomials are left. See the rule
  4. Plug in the roots: \(x=0\): \(-4=2C\Rightarrow C=-2\). \(x=-2\): \(-14=-8D\Rightarrow D=\frac74\).Why? Choosing x to make a factor equal 0 wipes out every term containing that factor, leaving one unknown to solve for. See the rule
  5. Equate coefficients: \(x^3\): \(0=A+D\Rightarrow A=-\frac74\). \(x^2\): \(0=2A+B\Rightarrow B=\frac72\).Why? Two polynomials are equal for every x only if each power of x has the same coefficient on both sides, so you can match \(x^3\), \(x^2\), \(x\) and the constants. See the rule
  6. Integrate each: \(-\frac74\ln|x|+\frac72\cdot\frac{x^{-1}}{-1}-2\cdot\frac{x^{-2}}{-2}+\frac74\ln|x+2|=-\frac74\ln|x|-\frac7{2x}+\frac1{x^2}+\frac74\ln|x+2|\).Why? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule
  7. Combine the logs: \(\frac74\ln\left|\frac{x+2}x\right|-\frac7{2x}+\frac1{x^2}+C\).Why? A power in the denominator moves to the top with a negative exponent, \(\frac1{a^n}=a^{-n}\), and back again. See the rule

Answer\(\frac74\ln\left|\frac{x+2}{x}\right|-\frac7{2x}+\frac1{x^2}+C\)

10·X4

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(x^2-12x+4)\,dx}{(x^2-3x+2)^2}\)

UsesPartial fractions table Log rule Power formula

  1. Factor the bottom: \(x^2-3x+2=(x-1)(x-2)\), so it's \((x-1)^2(x-2)^2\).
  2. Set up the pieces (two repeated factors): \(\frac A{x-1}+\frac B{(x-1)^2}+\frac C{x-2}+\frac D{(x-2)^2}\).Why? Each factor of the denominator gets its own fraction: a constant over a linear factor, one term per power for repeated factors, and \(A(\text{derivative})+B\) over an irreducible quadratic. See the rule
  3. Clear the fractions: \(x^2-12x+4=A(x-1)(x-2)^2+B(x-2)^2+C(x-2)(x-1)^2+D(x-1)^2\).Why? Multiply both sides by the full denominator so every fraction disappears and only polynomials are left. See the rule
  4. Plug in the roots: \(x=1\): \(-7=B\). \(x=2\): \(-16=D\).Why? Choosing x to make a factor equal 0 wipes out every term containing that factor, leaving one unknown to solve for. See the rule
  5. Find A and C: \(x^3\) terms: \(0=A+C\). \(x=0\): \(4=-4A+4B-2C+D=-4A-28-2C-16\). With \(C=-A\), that's \(-2A=48\), so \(A=-24\), \(C=24\).Why? Two polynomials are equal for every x only if each power of x has the same coefficient on both sides, so you can match \(x^3\), \(x^2\), \(x\) and the constants. See the rule
  6. Integrate each: \(-24\ln|x-1|+\frac7{x-1}+24\ln|x-2|+\frac{16}{x-2}\). Note that \(\int\frac{-7}{(x-1)^2}dx=\frac7{x-1}\).
  7. Combine the logs: \(24\ln\left|\frac{x-2}{x-1}\right|+\frac7{x-1}+\frac{16}{x-2}+C\).

Answer\(24\ln\left|\frac{x-2}{x-1}\right|+\frac7{x-1}+\frac{16}{x-2}+C\)

10·X5

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{(y^2+10)\,dy}{y^3-2y^2+5y}\)

UsesPartial fractions table Completing the square Log rule Arctan form

  1. Factor the bottom: \(y(y^2-2y+5)\). The quadratic can't be factored, because \(4-20<0\).
  2. Set up the pieces: \(\frac Ay+\frac{B(2y-2)+C}{y^2-2y+5}\).Why? Each factor of the denominator gets its own fraction: a constant over a linear factor, one term per power for repeated factors, and \(A(\text{derivative})+B\) over an irreducible quadratic. See the rule
  3. Clear the fractions: \(y^2+10=A(y^2-2y+5)+[B(2y-2)+C]y\).Why? Multiply both sides by the full denominator so every fraction disappears and only polynomials are left. See the rule
  4. Plug in \(y=0\): \(10=5A\Rightarrow A=2\).Why? Choosing x to make a factor equal 0 wipes out every term containing that factor, leaving one unknown to solve for. See the rule
  5. Equate coefficients: \(y^2\): \(1=A+2B\Rightarrow B=-\frac12\). \(y\): \(0=-2A-2B+C\Rightarrow C=3\).Why? Two polynomials are equal for every x only if each power of x has the same coefficient on both sides, so you can match \(x^3\), \(x^2\), \(x\) and the constants. See the rule
  6. Integrate each: \(2\ln|y|-\frac12\ln(y^2-2y+5)+3\int\frac{dy}{(y-1)^2+4}\). The last one is arctan with \(a=2\): \(\frac32\arctan\frac{y-1}2\).
  7. Answer: \(2\ln|y|-\frac12\ln(y^2-2y+5)+\frac32\arctan\frac{y-1}2+C\).

Answer\(2\ln|y|-\frac12\ln(y^2-2y+5)+\frac32\arctan\frac{y-1}2+C\)

10·X6

\(\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{(x^2+2x+10)^2}\)

UsesCompleting the square Trig substitution table Half-angle Arctan form

  1. Note: the slide writes dy, but the integrand is in x, so integrate in x.
  2. Complete the square: \(x^2+2x+10=(x+1)^2+9\).Why? Take half the x-coefficient and square it, then add and subtract it: \(x^2+bx=\left(x+\frac b2\right)^2-\frac{b^2}4\). See the rule
  3. Trig substitution: \(x+1=3\tan\theta\), \(dx=3\sec^2\theta\,d\theta\), and \((x+1)^2+9=9\sec^2\theta\).
  4. Substitute: \(\int\frac{3\sec^2\theta}{81\sec^4\theta}d\theta=\frac1{27}\int\cos^2\theta\,d\theta=\frac1{54}(\theta+\sin\theta\cos\theta)\).
  5. Back to x: \(\theta=\arctan\frac{x+1}3\), and \(\sin\theta\cos\theta=\frac{\tan\theta}{1+\tan^2\theta}=\frac{3(x+1)}{x^2+2x+10}\).Why? Reference triangle: the substitution gives two sides (e.g. \(x=2\sin\theta\) means \(\sin\theta=\frac x2=\frac{\text{opp}}{\text{hyp}}\)). Find the third side with Pythagoras, then read off any trig value with SOH-CAH-TOA. See the rule
  6. Simplify: \(\frac1{54}\arctan\frac{x+1}3+\frac{x+1}{18(x^2+2x+10)}+C\).

Answer\(\frac1{54}\arctan\frac{x+1}3+\dfrac{x+1}{18(x^2+2x+10)}+C\)

Other correct answers may look different and differ only by a constant.
